# P6 digest — 1897, her last year (Thérèse of Lisieux)

Source: `_by-period/P6.md` (807 cards). Every card ID appears in at least one event below. Events run in date order; events with the same date are grouped by time of day. Undated 1897 material is placed at the start or end of its month ("July, undated") and marked in the heading.

## Period summary

### Counts
- 807 cards read, merged into 418 events (E-P6-001 to E-P6-418).
- Many events merge cards from several witnesses: Mère Agnès (Process 1910, 1915, and her Carnet jaune), Céline, Marie du Sacré-Cœur, Marie de la Trinité (MT), Marie de l'Eucharistie (MdA), Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (TSA), Marie-Madeleine, Marthe, Aimée, Léonie, Jeanne Guérin, Godefroy, Maupas and Faucon, plus her own letters and poems and Laveille and Piat.

### Turning points
1. Hidden decline, Jan to May 1897. Letters state she expects a short life (E-P6-002, E-P6-011, E-P6-012, E-P6-021). Fever note 3 Apr (E-P6-016), cautery and blisters (E-P6-014), the Yellow Notebook begins 6 Apr (E-P6-017), Easter (E-P6-019), relieved of all offices (E-P6-031).
2. 2 to 4 June: Pauline's midnight visit and the order to write Manuscript C (E-P6-052, E-P6-055); Ms C opens (E-P6-059); it breaks off in pencil about 8 to 11 July (E-P6-155).
3. 4 to 9 June: farewell to her sisters, the white hen, the "shower of roses" (E-P6-066, E-P6-069), her "farewell" letter to Bellière (E-P6-071).
4. 8 July: carried to the infirmary (E-P6-138). Daily blood-spitting 6 July to 5 Aug.
5. 30 July: Extreme Unction and Viaticum (E-P6-226). "Heaven doing good on earth" is dated 17 July (E-P6-180).
6. 19 August: last Communion (E-P6-314). Month without a doctor and the refusal of morphine (E-P6-308, E-P6-356).
7. 29 to 30 September: the death (E-P6-383, E-P6-386 to E-P6-392).
8. 1 to 4 October: the body at the grille, the burial (E-P6-398, E-P6-401). The sandals burned (E-P6-394), the critic cured (E-P6-395).
9. After death: the community changes (E-P6-408), the manuscript is edited and printed (E-P6-406, E-P6-416).

### The five best scenes
1. The death sequence, 30 Sept: agony, "I would not want to suffer less", "I love Him", the gaze "for the space of a Credo" and the candle held to her eyes (E-P6-389, E-P6-390, E-P6-391).
2. The chestnut walk: a feverish Thérèse leaves her carriage to comfort a weeping novice on a tree stump (E-P6-124).
3. The kitchen remark and the meat juice: "she has never done anything worth speaking about"; "not even a good nun", and her joy (E-P6-085, E-P6-234).
4. The comic-sad infirmary scenes: buried under blankets by the deaf infirmarian (E-P6-088), "I don't know the trade" when she looks too cheerful to be anointed (E-P6-148).
5. The alpargates (patched rope sandals) thrown on the fire after her death (E-P6-394), with the lay Sister who scolded her cured at her feet (E-P6-395).
Runners-up: the stairs climbed in half an hour (E-P6-060); the broken pane (E-P6-007); the robin and turtle-dove (E-P6-381); the last photograph (30 Aug); "I tell the whole truth".

### The five best "new to English readers" facts
1. Her own words "if there is a Heaven, it is for me" (LT 216), a phrase absent from the 1911 edition, and later scratched out in the original (E-P6-002).
2. "If I had not had faith, I would have killed myself" (22 Sept, Carnet jaune), absent from Novissima Verba (E-P6-370). Sensitive: quote with its context.
3. The 1911 edition splits and redates her farewell letters to Bellière, so that "I am not dying, I am entering life" (written 9 June) appears as a saying of 14 Aug (E-P6-165); and Mère Agnès scratched out a saying of 16 July (E-P6-175).
4. The medical record: a month without a doctor because the prioress refused Dr La Néele; morphine syrup given by stratagem and injections refused (E-P6-308, E-P6-356); conflicting witnesses on who was responsible.
5. The Sister who was to be cured at her feet had said she was "not even a good religious" and had brought her the meat juice; Thérèse was glad of it; the lobster letter to Roulland (E-P6-011) and the TSA "black door" dream with her darkest words (E-P6-001) are also new.

### People who matter in this period
- Mère Agnès (Pauline): nurse, recorder of the Yellow Notebook, first editor; Marie du Sacré-Cœur, Céline (Sr Geneviève, assistant infirmarian); Léonie.
- Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress): daily communion, refusal of a doctor and of morphine, the printed circular, changed after 1897.
- Novices: Marie de la Trinité, Marthe, Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin), TSA; the Sisters Saint-Vincent de Paul, Saint-Jean-Baptiste, Saint-Stanislas (old first infirmarian), Hermance.
- Doctors: de Cornière (community doctor), La Néele (cousin by marriage). Priests: Maupas (superior), Youf (chaplain, died 15 days later), Faucon, Godefroy Madelaine (Mondaye).
- Missionary brothers: Roulland, Bellière. The Guérin family (Isidore, Céline, Jeanne, Marie). Théophane Vénard (picture, relic, poem).

### Open conflicts
- Time of the agony (3 pm / 4:30 pm / about 5 pm) and of death (7:15 / about 7 / 7:20 / 7:30); whether she spoke after 5 pm (Marie) or not; who held the candle at the gaze; whether anyone watched the last night.
- Who withheld relief: the prioress (Marie, Céline, TSA, Marie du Sacré-Cœur) or the doctor (Marie des Anges, MdA); the number of times La Néele was allowed in.
- Date and wording of many sayings: the arena and the "little saint" (July or August), the flies (30 July or 26 Sept), the devil scene (16 Aug, late Sept or 28 Sept), the petals (six wordings), the sacraments (who gave Extreme Unction), the rose saying (7 June or 9 June), the painting request (25 Apr or 28 May), Easter (18 or 25 Apr), the hymn place (oratory or garden hermitage).
- Source criticism: the Yellow Notebook was recopied in 1921 to 1923 from a destroyed 1904 to 1905 notebook (E-P6-017); Pauline's "strategic" sayings (permission to edit, the manuscript, the "mission") are the most open to reworking; MT's testimony recites her own 1897 notes.
- Publication: date (30 Sept, 21 Oct or "October 1898"), imprimatur 7 or 8 March, and the claim that Gonzague wanted to destroy the autographs (E-P6-416).

### Notes for later stages
- Event headings show a working date; the `Cards` line lists all card IDs. Cross-references inside events use event IDs.
- Cards outside 1897 (for example the 1910 exhumation, the 1923 translation, the 1929 and 1935 circulars) sit in events of the year of the fact, or at the end of the year of the card's main subject.
- Each event ends with a scene-potential note. "Conflicts" keeps both versions with card IDs.

---

## Events

### E-P6-001 — 8 Jan 1897; the talk in Feb-Mar 1897; summer 1897 in the infirmary — TSA's dream of the black door: "Sister Thérèse is called"
- What happened: TSA dreamed on 8 Jan 1897 that she was in a very dark room closed by a heavy black door with a line of dazzling light under it. From the next room a voice said: "Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus is called!" She felt that beside her, in an even darker part, her little sister was being prepared. The voice insisted: "She must be very beautiful!" She woke sure that Thérèse would soon leave for heaven. Some weeks later in recreation she told Thérèse. Thérèse was deeply moved and said God had sent the dream to encourage her through TSA: the dark room and black door were the image of her darkness. Thérèse insisted on the words "through you", with tears in her eyes, and told her of her trial of faith, which TSA had not known. TSA recorded Thérèse's words: "How beautiful! It's not a dream, it's a vision, and you had it for me... I don't believe in eternal life; it seems to me that after this mortal life there is nothing more. I can't express the darkness I'm plunged in... the black door is so much the image of what is happening in me." In the infirmary: "Your dream is my only ray of light."
- Cards: F-EW-2551, F-OW-220
- Best details:
  - « Je ne crois pas à la vie éternelle, il me semble qu'après cette vie mortelle il n'y a plus rien. » (F-OW-220).
  - "Your dream is my only ray of light" (F-OW-220).
  - TSA says she knew nothing of the trial of faith until this talk (F-EW-2551 notes).
- Evidence: TSA's own account, as retold by Mère Agnès in TSA's circular of 8 Sep 1929; TSA's own record in "Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs". Medium (a later account by one Sister; strongest wording of the trial found in this family of sources).
- Conflicts: Thérèse's trial began at Easter 1896 (Ms C), so it had lasted about ten months. Modern editors identify TSA as the Sister "who had the talent of displeasing me" (Ms C 13v); treat the identification with care (F-OW-220 notes). Compare F-EW-1131.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the black door and the darkest words.

### E-P6-002 — 9 Jan 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 216: "if there is a Heaven, it is for me"
- What happened: To Mère Agnès (9 Jan 1897): "if you knew how touched I am to see how much you love me!" She hopes to go "up there" soon. Since "If there is a Heaven, it is for me", she will be rich and "will have all the treasures of the good God", and will repay Mère Agnès a hundredfold. It hurts her to "always receive without ever giving". She was sorry to see Mère Agnès's tears but glad of "the good effect they produced", which "was magic". She says she blames nobody "when my little Mother is looked at sideways", since the Sisters are "only instruments set at an angle by Jesus himself" so that Mère Agnès's way "resembles the one he chose for himself" when his face was hidden. She compares her to the swallow that leads her companions to their new homeland. The Carmel editors say that in the original of LT 216 the second half of the phrase "since if there is a Heaven, it is for me" was scratched out after the 1910 Process copy, probably because the recipient thought it improper to write a doubt of Heaven.
- Cards: F-OW-470, F-OW-575
- Best details:
  - « Puisque «S'il y a un Ciel, il est pour moi», je serai riche » (F-OW-470).
  - « les soeurs ne sont que des instruments posés de travers par Jésus lui-même » (F-OW-470).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 216); Carmel editors (on the scratching). High for the words; medium for the scratching (the editors' inference about who scratched it).
- Conflicts: The cause of Mère Agnès's tears is not given. The quotation marks around "S'il y a un Ciel..." show she quotes someone (unnamed). The phrase is absent from the 1911 edition.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-003 — Jan (?) 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 217: "the little trade of the shop" and the Wandering Jew's song
- What happened: LT 217 (to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph): "Charming, the little verses". It is ugly "to go begging at others' doors when one's purse is full". Sleeping, being gentle and cheerful is "the little trade of the shop", which "must never close, not even on Sundays and feasts", "the days Jesus keeps to try our souls". She tells the Sister to sing "like a finch"; she herself "like a poor little sparrow groans in my corner, singing like the Wandering Jew: Death can do nothing to me, I see it well!" She asks about "the famous tablecloth" (the nappe). LT 239 (3 (?) Jun 1897) has her again: "I must walk until my last moment. It is he who will end my torment, like the poor Wandering Jew".
- Cards: F-OW-471, F-OW-492
- Best details:
  - « chantant comme le Juif errant : La mort ne me peut rien, je m'en aperçois bien !... » (F-OW-471).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 217, LT 239). High for the words; the reference is to a folk song ("Le Juif errant", outside knowledge).
- Conflicts: none. Absent from the 1911 edition.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: her joking about death three months before the worsening.

### E-P6-004 — 21 Jan 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — PN 45 "My joy": "All my soul is there!"
- What happened: The Carmel editors say PN 45 "Ma joie" was written at Mère Agnès's request for her feast on 21 Jan 1897, and that Thérèse said as she gave it: "All my soul is there!" It has seven stanzas (tune "Rêve, parfum ou frais murmure"). Every stanza except the last begins "Ma joie, c'est..." ("My joy is to remain little", "to hide myself, to lower myself", "to love suffering"). Last stanza: "Long yet I am willing to live, Lord, if that is your wish; in Heaven I would like to follow you if that pleased you... What are death or life to me? Jesus, my joy is to love you!" She sang it while dressing on 7 May (see E-P6-026).
- Cards: F-OW-575
- Best details:
  - « Ma joie, c'est de rester petite » ; « Que me font la mort ou la vie? / Jésus, ma joie, c'est de t'aimer ! » (F-OW-575).
- Evidence: Her own writing (PN 45); Carmel editors (context). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The scratching of LT 216 is discussed under E-P6-002.
- New to English readers: yes ("Toute mon âme est là"); partial for the poem
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-005 — 27 Jan 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 218 to Frère Siméon in Rome: "my course here below will not be long"
- What happened: Thérèse joins Céline in thanking Frère Siméon for "the precious favour you obtained for our Carmel" (the letter does not say what). She is sad to hear his health has been shaken. She hopes he will stay in exile "so that the immense weight of your merits may make up for other souls who will appear before God with empty hands". She writes: "I believe my course here below will not be long. When I appear before my beloved Spouse I will have only my desires to offer him." If he goes first, she hopes he will meet her and present his works for her. She recalls he obtained the blessing of Leo XIII for them "so many times". She asks only the grace "to love Jesus and to make him loved".
- Cards: F-OW-472
- Best details:
  - « je crois que ma course ici-bas ne sera pas longue... lorsque je paraîtrai devant mon Epoux Bien-Aimé je n'aurai que mes désirs à Lui présenter » (F-OW-472).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 218). High. Absent from the 1911 letters. One of her earliest dated statements that she expected to die soon after Good Friday 1896.
- Conflicts: The favour obtained is not named. The "empty hands" idea is in her Act of Oblation (June 1895).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-006 — Jan-Feb 1897 — The St Stanislas play as a vehicle for her hopes
- What happened: Thérèse told MT that what pleased her most in her last recreation play, on St Stanislas Kostka (Jan-Feb 1897), was that she could express her certainty that the blessed can still work for souls on earth. MT copies a long passage from the play into her deposition. The Carmel page dates RP 8 "Saint Stanislas Kostka" to 8 Feb 1897, the golden jubilee (50 years of profession) of Sr Saint-Stanislas. The cast: the Virgin and the Child Jesus; St Stanislas Kostka; St Francis Borgia, duke of Gandía, "lord of the court of Charles V", General of the Jesuits; and Brother Stephen Augusti, a young novice. Scene 1 is in Rome, in St Francis Borgia's room. Tunes: "L'Ange et l'âme" and "Le Soir". PS 3 "Depuis cinquante ans" is a second piece for the same Sister.
- Cards: F-EW-1382, F-OW-577
- Best details:
  - The blessed "can still work for souls on earth" (F-EW-1382).
  - « Mon Révérend Père, je suis confus de votre bonté ; comment pouvez-vous me traiter en ami, vous, le général de la compagnie de Jésus » (F-OW-577).
- Evidence: MT (heard; PO 17). High. Her plays were written for community feasts. | Her own writing (RP 8); date and occasion from Carmel editors. High for date and cast.
- Conflicts: MT's account dates the play "Jan-Feb 1897"; the Carmel gives 8 Feb 1897.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: her last play, written while already ill.

### E-P6-007 — 2 Feb 1897, refectory — The broken hatch pane
- What happened: On 2 Feb 1897, serving in the refectory, she broke a pane of the service hatch with the corner of the tray. Already very ill, she could not hide her emotion quickly, and Céline saw her cry. Helping pick up the glass, Céline tried to console her. Thérèse said she had asked God for a big pain to offer for Théophane Vénard's martyrdom anniversary, and here it was. She would not have chosen a fault against poverty, but it was involuntary.
- Cards: F-EW-1383
- Best details:
  - A pane of glass as the "big pain" asked for Vénard's anniversary (F-EW-1383).
- Evidence: Céline (saw and heard). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a broken pane turned into an offering.

### E-P6-008 — 2 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — PN 47 "To Théophane Vénard", on the anniversary of his death
- What happened: The Carmel page dates PN 47 to 2 Feb 1897, the anniversary of Vénard's death, and says it is offered by Thérèse to "her much-loved saint" (priest of the Foreign Missions, martyred in Tonkin at 31). Tune "Les adieux du Martyr". Seven stanzas. Stanza 6: "I too love that unfaithful shore that was the object of your ardent love; with happiness I would fly to it if the good God called me there one day." Stanza 7 asks him to come at her last hour: "If I were a spring flower that the Lord would soon gather, come down from Heaven at my last hour, I beg you, O Blessed Martyr!" She also asked God on 2 Feb for a big pain to offer for his anniversary (see E-P6-007).
- Cards: F-OW-576
- Best details:
  - « Avec bonheur, je volerais vers elle / Si le Bon Dieu m'y appelait un jour... » (F-OW-576).
  - « Descends du Ciel à mon heure dernière » (F-OW-576).
- Evidence: Her own writing (PN 47); date and dedication from Carmel editors. High.
- Conflicts: The year 1861 is outside knowledge, not on the page.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-009 — 22 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 219: Mère Agnès's "broken nose"
- What happened: In a note to Mère Agnès (22 Feb 1897) Thérèse thanks her and jokes: "Your nose has been broken, yes, but it IS long! You will always have enough left, whereas mine, if I had it broken, there would be none left." "How happy we are to know how to laugh at everything!"
- Cards: F-OW-473
- Best details:
  - « votre nez a été cassé, oui mais !! il EST LONG !... » (F-OW-473).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 219). Medium: the note does not say whether the "broken nose" is literal or a figure (for instance a snub from the community).
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-010 — 24 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 220 to Bellière: the sacristan's job, the poems, "no more grilles"
- What happened: On Wednesday evening 24 Feb 1897 ("before entering the silence of holy Lent") Thérèse adds a note to the prioress's letter to Abbé Bellière. She says he will get more of her poems, at the prioress's order, only in some weeks: "I have few free moments, even on Sunday, because of my job as sacristan (sacristine)". She says the poems reveal "not what I am, but what I would like and ought to be". She accepts to be godmother of "the first child you will have the joy of baptising" and chooses the Virgin, St Joseph and St Maurice. She gives him a short prayer to say daily for her. She asks for "the memorable dates of your life". She writes that he must not regret having "a sister who seems to want to go and enjoy eternal rest and leave you to work alone". "If in Heaven I could no longer work for his glory, I would prefer exile to the homeland." "I do not know the future. Yet if Jesus makes my forebodings come true, I promise to stay your little sister Up There... Then there will be no more enclosure, no more grilles, and my soul will be able to fly with you to the distant missions. Our roles will stay the same: to you the apostolic arms, to me prayer and love."
- Cards: F-OW-474, F-OW-475
- Best details:
  - « j'ai peu d'instants libres même le dimanche à cause de mon emploi de sacristine » (F-OW-474).
  - « alors il n'y aura plus de clôture, plus de grilles et mon âme pourra voler avec vous dans les lointaines missions » (F-OW-475).
  - « Ces pauvres poésies vous révéleront non pas ce que je suis, mais ce que je voudrais et devrais être » (F-OW-474).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 220, Bellière). High. Absent from the 1911 letters ("Maurice", "j'en ai l'espoir mon exil sera court", and the sacristan remark).
- Conflicts: The letters do not say when she was made sacristan. The letter does not mention her illness.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-011 — told 19 Mar 1897; event early 1897, Carmel kitchen — LT 221: the lobster that "was possessed"
- What happened: In LT 221 she tells Roulland that "Carmel, like Sichuan, is a country foreign to the world, where one loses the most basic customs". A charitable person sent a small lobster "well tied in a hamper". It had long been since such a marvel was seen in the monastery. The Sister cook remembered that it must be put in water to cook, "groaning at having to exercise such cruelty on an innocent creature". The lobster "seemed asleep", but at the first heat "its gentleness changed to fury" and it jumped into the middle of the kitchen because the cook had not put a lid on the pot. The cook, armed with tongs, chased it. She went "in tears" to the prioress, declaring that the lobster "was possessed by the devil". The prioress laughed, went to the kitchen, took the lobster ("who had made no vow of obedience, made some resistance"), put it back in its "prison" and shut the door, that is, the lid. That evening at recreation "the whole community laughed to tears", and next day each Sister tasted a mouthful.
- Cards: F-OW-476
- Best details:
  - « le homard est endiablé » ; « n'ayant pas fait voeu d'obéissance fit quelque résistance » (F-OW-476).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 221 to Roulland, 19 Mar 1897). High. Absent from the 1911 edition ("homard").
- Conflicts: The Sister cook is not named. The story answers Roulland's own funny story about his cook breaking a pot.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a lobster on the loose in the Carmel kitchen and the prioress laughing.

### E-P6-012 — 19 Mar 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 221: "I will leave one day for the Carmel of Hanoi"; "stay in purgatory until the end of the world"
- What happened: Thérèse tells Roulland she has read the life of Théophane Vénard and was "interested and touched more than I can say". She composed verses (PN 47), whose next-to-last verse says that "with happiness I would leave for Tonkin if God called me there". She says it is not a dream: "if Jesus does not come soon to fetch me for the Carmel of Heaven, I will one day leave for the Carmel of Hanoi", which the Saigon Carmel founded recently. Vocations there are rare and superiors often do not let go the Sisters. She reports that at the end of the year thieves entered the Hanoi monastery and took the prioress's crucifix from beside her pillow and the community's small money. The prioress believes in her vocation but not that it can be realised: "the scabbard must be as strong as the sword", and "the scabbard would be thrown into the sea before reaching Tonkin". She calls the body "frère l'âne, as St Francis called it". She asks Roulland not to pray to free her from purgatory and quotes St Teresa: "What does it matter to me to stay in purgatory until the end of the world if by my prayers I save one soul!" She tells him to choose "Mary" over "Thérèse" for a girl he baptises, and later to name two little sisters "Céline and Thérèse", "the names we bore in the world". Céline "told me lately her wish that through you Céline and Thérèse go and live again in China". The Carmel of Lisieux calls him "the missionary of our Mother".
- Cards: F-OW-477, F-OW-478
- Best details:
  - « il faudrait pour cela que le fourreau soit aussi solide que l'épée » (F-OW-477).
  - « Que m'importe à moi de rester jusqu'à la fin du monde en purgatoire si par mes prières je sauve une seule âme! » (F-OW-478).
  - « ce misérable frère l'âne, comme l'appelait saint François d'Assise » (F-OW-477).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 221). High. Absent from the 1911 edition ("frère l'âne", the thieves story, the Céline-and-Thérèse request); the Hanoi wish itself is in 1911 in its Ms C form.
- Conflicts: The prioress's view is reported by Thérèse; we do not have the prioress's own words. Céline did not know the full extent of the Roulland link. Whether he named Chinese girls "Céline and Thérèse" is not recorded.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-013 — 19 Mar and 4 (?) Apr 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 222 and 223: "my little arm will be as if it were long"
- What happened: LT 222 (19 Mar 1897): "Thank you, little Mother. Oh yes, Jesus loves you and I do too! He gives you proofs of it every day and not me... yes, but when I am up there, my little arm will be as if it were long, and my little Mother will hear from me." LT 223 (4 (?) Apr 1897): "I am afraid I have hurt my little Mother; I love her, though! Oh yes! But I cannot tell her all I think; she must guess it."
- Cards: F-OW-480
- Best details:
  - « quand je serai là-haut, mon petit bras sera tout comme s'il était long » (F-OW-480).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 222, LT 223). High for the words; the cause of the hurt in LT 223 is not stated. The "little arm" image goes with the "elevator" of Ms C.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-014 — spring 1897 (end of Lent onward) — Cautery, blisters and a folded blanket: the treatments of 1897
- What happened: At the end of Lent 1897 her state worsened and the fever became continuous. She was treated with blistering plasters (vésicatoires), pointes de feu (cauterizing needles), tincture of iodine and rubbings, all without effect. Weeks earlier a persistent cough had put her on a meat diet. Céline, assistant infirmarian without permission to give a mattress, folded a large blanket in four under her sheet after the cautery. Céline saw her receive more than 500 cauterisation points on the back and counted them. While the doctor worked, chatting with Mother about ordinary things, Thérèse stood leaning on a table. She said afterwards she offered it for souls and thought of the martyrs. Then she went up alone to her cell and sat trembling on the edge of her straw mattress. Céline could only fold her own blanket in four and slide it onto the mattress, since Thérèse was not yet recognised as seriously ill. She never criticised the "primitive" care of the sick. Pauline (1915) adds that before the end of Lent she was rubbed with a horsehair glove and had a high fever every day from 3 p.m.
- Cards: F-EW-1225, F-EW-1221, F-EW-1366, F-EW-1367, F-EW-1385
- Best details:
  - Céline once counted 500 applications of the cautery needle (F-EW-1221).
  - After the needle she went at night to her straw mattress; Céline folded a blanket in four under the sheet (F-EW-1221).
  - The remedies were blisters, cautery needles, iodine and rubbings; all failed (F-EW-1225).
  - She offered the cautery for souls and thought of the martyrs (F-EW-1366).
  - The doctor chatted with Mother about ordinary things while he worked (F-EW-1366).
  - Céline's own word "primitive" criticises the house (F-EW-1367).
  - Horsehair glove and 3 p.m. fever appear only in 1915 (F-EW-1385).
- Evidence: Pauline (eyewitness, Process 1910); Céline (eyewitness, Summarium 1919 via Laveille). High. | Céline (saw, counted); Mère Agnès 1915. High.
- Conflicts: Céline says a mattress was out of the question because she was not yet recognised as seriously ill (F-EW-1367); F-EW-1221 says Céline had no permission. 1910 lists similar treatments without the glove or the 3 p.m. fever.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the doctor talking, the girl leaning on a table, then alone trembling on her straw mattress.

### E-P6-015 — 1897 — With Pauline she only listened
- What happened: The prioress let Mère Agnès go and speak to Thérèse sometimes, but Thérèse had no matching permission to open her own heart. So she only listened to her "little mother" and said nothing of her own thoughts. She could have obtained permission with one word but refused to seek permissions that soften the martyrdom of religious life.
- Cards: F-EW-1244
- Best details:
  - "one must not get permissions that could soften the martyrdom of religious life" (F-EW-1244).
- Evidence: Marie (heard Thérèse; Process 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low: explains a silence; useful context.

### E-P6-016 — 3 Apr 1897 (one year after Good Friday 1896), Carmel of Lisieux — LT 222 bis: "it is the fever I have, every day at 3 o'clock, military time"
- What happened: On a small note to her uncle M. Guérin (3 Apr 1897) Thérèse signs herself "Thérèse of the Child Jesus, who is the smallest but does not have the least love!!!" and adds: "It is not true, it is the fever I have, every day at 3, military time." The last line of the transcription reads: "Our Father wishes that Thérèse Pougheol enter here on trial."
- Cards: F-OW-479
- Best details:
  - « Ce n'est pas vrai, c'est la fièvre que j'ai, tous les jours à 3h, heure militaire. » (F-OW-479).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 222 bis). Medium: the note is a fragment; "It is not true" refers to something written before it in a joint note. "Heure militaire" probably means 3 p.m., but the note does not say. First mention of a daily fever in the letters read (LT 1-222 bis). Absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: Fever at "3 o'clock" (LT 222 bis) versus the evening fever "at the hour when finches sleep" (LT 255, July) and "every day from 3 p.m." (F-EW-1385).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-017 — from 3-6 Apr 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — The Yellow Notebook begins; how reliable are the "last words"?
- What happened: From 3 Apr 1897 she was seriously ill, with daily fever, indigestion and much coughing. She was treated with vesicatories (blistering plasters, very painful, up to twelve hours). On 6 Apr Mère Agnès began to note her sayings on small pieces of paper, later copied into the "Carnet jaune". Thérèse at first felt uneasy and then agreed. The first saying: "When we are misunderstood and judged unfavourably, what is the use of defending ourselves?... it is so sweet to say nothing." Laveille lists the themes (the little way, conformity to God's will, the privileges of Mary, the Holy Family's simplicity, communion of saints). From 8 July Agnès noted sayings through the day, "turning her long presence into a continuous interview", which Thérèse knew. The historian Claude Langlois: no Sister knew shorthand; four versions exist, all after her death: two made for the process (1909: 306 sayings; 1910: 275), the Novissima Verba (1927: 362), and the Carnet jaune (714 sayings). The Carnet jaune was written out in 1921-1923 from a "Gros carnet noir" of 1904-1905, compiled from loose sheets. The black notebook was destroyed and only one loose sheet survives. Langlois holds that Agnès, "by her own admission", reworked some sayings into "quasi-slogans", but most came through unrewritten; the "strategic" sayings (her authority to publish the writings; Thérèse's mission after death) need most care. Céline began her own notes on 21 July 1897 at Léonie's request and wrote only sayings addressed to her. Agnès's own "Remarque importante" claims Thérèse told her on 16 Jul 1897 "you alone know all the folds of my little soul", so that people could trust what Agnès would say and write about her.
- Cards: F-OW-222, F-OW-223, F-LF-231
- Best details:
  - « c'est si doux de ne rien dire, de se laisser juger n'importe comment ! » (F-OW-222).
  - Versions: 1909 (306 sayings), 1910 (275), 1927 Novissima Verba (362), Carnet jaune (714) (F-OW-223).
  - « Le carnet jaune a été rédigé en 1921-1923. » (F-OW-223).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune, editors' notes); C. Langlois, "Les dernières paroles de Thérèse sont-elles de Thérèse ?" (scholarship, facts-only); Laveille. High that this is Langlois's view; the underlying question stays open.
- Conflicts: Every Carnet jaune card in this dossier is "Thérèse as recorded by Mère Agnès, in a copy made 24-26 years later" (F-OW-223 notes). The biography must say this once, clearly. Jean-François Six (1973) is a critic. Pauline says the notebook began 6 Apr 1897; PO and PA cards date notes from May 1897 or 8 July.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low: essential for source criticism.

### E-P6-018 — 7 April 1897 — "God will draw you up like a little drop of dew"
- What happened: Mère Agnès asked Thérèse how she herself would die, showing her fears. Thérèse answered with a tender smile: "God will suck you up like a little drop of dew..."
- Cards: F-OW-224
- Best details:
  - « Le bon Dieu vous pompera comme une petite goutte de rosée... » (F-OW-224).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune, CJ 7.4). Medium-high; see the note on the Carnet jaune's reliability.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-019 — Easter 1897 (18 Apr) — Easter: "Have you had temptations against faith?"
- What happened: In a private talk at Easter 1897 Thérèse asked Marie whether she had ever had temptations against faith. Surprised, Marie asked if she had any. Thérèse answered vaguely and changed the subject. Marie understood that she did not want to pass on her temptations.
- Cards: F-EW-1271
- Best details:
  - The question, then the vague answer and the change of subject (F-EW-1271).
- Evidence: Marie (eyewitness, Process 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none (scene S-EW-030 cited in the card).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a quiet moment where the hidden trial almost surfaces.

### E-P6-020 — 18 Apr 1897 (Easter day) — Humiliations from Sisters; the magpies; "I tell the whole truth"
- What happened: She confided "very painful humiliations" given her by some Sisters: "I arrange things, even in the storm, to stay at peace inside... while listening, I must be able to look out of the window and enjoy the sight of the sky, the trees... Just now, during my struggle about Sr X., I watched the fine magpies playing in the meadow." As a child she read a story praising a school mistress who told one girl "you're not wrong" and another "you're right"; she thought it was wrong, and still does: "If I'm not loved, too bad! I tell the whole truth; let no one come to me if they don't want to know it." "Kindness must not degenerate into weakness."
- Cards: F-OW-225
- Best details:
  - « Si je ne suis pas aimée, tant pis ! Moi je dis la vérité tout entière » (F-OW-225).
  - The magpies playing in the meadow during her struggle about Sr X. (F-OW-225).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune, CJ 18.4.1, 18.4.3-4). Medium-high. Her firmness as novice guide counters the "sweet only" image.
- Conflicts: 18 April 1897 was Easter day; F-EW-1271 (Easter talk with Marie) is the same day. "Sr X." is unnamed.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-021 — 25 Apr 1897 (the card says Easter Sunday), Carmel of Lisieux — LT 224 to Bellière: "dear little brother", Joan of Arc's voice, "he has done great things in me"
- What happened: On Easter Sunday 1897 ("Alleluia") Thérèse tells Bellière her pen "or rather my heart refuses to call you 'Monsieur l'Abbé' any more". The prioress has allowed her to use the name she uses when she speaks of him to Jesus: "dear little brother". She says Jesus united their souls to work for sinners as he united Père de la Colombière and Margaret Mary, and copies a passage from Margaret Mary's Life. She adds that he is not yet a Colombière, "but I do not doubt that one day you will be a true apostle". She says "Vivre d'Amour" arrived twice by mistake. She asks whether their relations stay secret: "Nobody except your director must know the union Jesus has formed between our souls." On Joan of Arc: she dreamed in childhood of fighting on battlefields. "The account of the exploits of Joan of Arc enchanted me; I felt in my heart the desire and the courage to imitate her; it seemed to me that the Lord destined me too for great things." Instead of heavenly voices calling her to battle, "I heard in the depth of my soul a sweeter, stronger voice, that of the Spouse of virgins, who called me to other exploits... and in the solitude of Carmel I understood that my mission was not to have a mortal king crowned but to make the King of Heaven loved, to subject to him the kingdom of hearts." She also writes: "Do not think it is humility that keeps me from recognising God's gifts; I know he has done great things in me, and I sing it every day with joy." She hopes "my exile will be short", but "not because I am ready; I feel I never will be, unless the Lord himself transforms me". Since "the Lord seems to want to give me only the martyrdom of love", she hopes he will allow her, through Bellière, "to gather the other palm".
- Cards: F-OW-481, F-OW-482, F-OW-483
- Best details:
  - « ma mission n'était pas de faire couronner un roi mortel mais de faire aimer le Roi du Ciel, de lui soumettre le royaume des coeurs » (F-OW-482).
  - « Ne croyez pas que ce soit l'humilité qui m'empêche de reconnaître les dons du bon Dieu, je sais qu'Il a fait en moi de grandes choses et je le chante chaque jour avec bonheur. » (F-OW-483).
  - « Personne excepté votre Directeur ne doit connaître l'union que Jésus a formée entre nos âmes » (F-OW-481).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 224). High. Absent from the 1911 edition ("Colombière", "faire couronner un roi mortel", "he has done great things in me").
- Conflicts: The card dates 25 Apr 1897 as Easter Sunday; on the 1897 calendar Easter fell on 18 April (outside fact; check). Her plays "Jeanne d'Arc accomplissant sa mission" (RP 1, 21 Jan 1894) and "Jeanne d'Arc martyre" (RP 3, 1895) are the source for her identification with Joan.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-022 — 1890s; last painting 1897 — The jealous Sister and the ugly paintings; Thérèse's last use of her brushes
- What happened: Sisters brought objects for Thérèse to decorate as gifts for the prioress's feast. One very eccentric Sister, jealous of her and always ready to mortify her, asked for bizarre subjects in poor taste. Thérèse never refused. She looked for models and followed the odd instructions. In 1897 she painted small works for this Sister again, and this was the last time she used her brushes.
- Cards: F-EW-1219, F-EW-1236
- Best details:
  - She painted something "ridiculous and not in good taste" to please the Sister (F-EW-1219).
  - "It seemed that you had only to make Sr Thérèse suffer to get anything you wanted from her." (F-EW-1236, Pauline).
  - When one Sister found fault instead of thanking her, she did not refuse the next request (F-EW-1219).
  - Pauline notes the Sister has since left the Order (F-EW-1236).
- Evidence: Summarium 1919 via Laveille (deponent unnamed, medium); Mère Agnès 1915 (eyewitness, high).
- Conflicts: The Sister is not named in either card; F-EW-1236 notes say she is probably Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (left 1909), but this is the note's inference, not the card's.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: gentleness under petty cruelty, ending with the brushes put down for good.

### E-P6-023 — May 1897; remarks 21 Aug 1897 — The last poem, "Why I love you, O Mary"
- What happened: Marie asked Thérèse to write down what she thought about the Virgin, and in May 1897 she composed her last poem to Mary. She later said it expressed all she thought and what she would preach about Mary if she were a priest. Céline gives a different origin: already very ill, Thérèse said she still had something to do before dying, since she had always dreamed of expressing in a song all she thought of the Virgin. What delighted her was Mary walking "the common way", without raptures or miracles. On 21 Aug she said she would like to have been a priest to speak well of Mary. She felt Mary was shown more as queen than mother, and found it strange to say she would eclipse all the saints like the sun: a mother who eclipses her children, she thought the opposite. Rosary and Memorare were her daily prayers. The Carmel editors date PN 54 to May 1897 and say Thérèse wrote it for herself, encouraged by Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur. They quote her to Céline: "I have always dreamed of expressing in a song to the Blessed Virgin all that I think of her." It is her longest poem in her last year. On 21 Aug 1897 she said: "I have said in my Canticle 'Why I love you, O Mary!' all that I would preach about her" (CJ 21.8.3). Tune: "Pourquoi m'avoir livré l'autre jour, ô ma Mère".
- Cards: F-EW-1390, F-EW-1391, F-EW-1392, F-EW-1323, F-OW-580
- Best details:
  - « exprime tout ce que je pense et ce que je prêcherais sur la Sainte Vierge si j'étais prêtre » (F-EW-1390).
  - « J'ai encore quelque chose à faire avant de mourir » (F-EW-1392).
  - "A mother who eclipses her children" (F-EW-1323).
  - « Enfin j'ai dit dans mon Cantique : «Pourquoi je t'aime, ô Marie!» tout ce que je prêcherais sur elle. » (F-OW-580).
- Evidence: Marie (took part, 1910 and 1915); Céline (heard, 1915); MdA reporting Last Conversations. High. | Her own writing (PN 54); Carmel editors; Céline (PO 667). High. The date "May 1897" is the editors'; the exact day is not given.
- Conflicts: Origin of the poem: Marie says at her own request; Céline says Thérèse's own wish. The card says both can be true. | The editors say Thérèse wrote it "for herself, encouraged by Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur"; Marie says at her own request (F-EW-1390).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-024 — May 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Relieved of all duties; the secret of Roulland
- What happened: In May she coughed much, had cough syrup, fever again, vesicatories and sessions of "pointes de feu" (cauterisation). She was relieved of all work, stopped going to choir and stopped guiding novices. "It's not 'death' that will come for me, it's God. Death is not a phantom, a horrible spectre as in pictures." Mère Agnès notes that Thérèse had been forbidden by Mère Marie de Gonzague to tell her (Agnès) about Fr Roulland for nearly two years. She even secretly borrowed Agnès's burnisher to paint a parchment picture for him, returning it when Agnès was out. Only three months before her death did the prioress free her to speak.
- Cards: F-OW-226
- Best details:
  - « La mort, ce n'est pas un fantôme, un spectre horrible, comme on la représente sur les images. » (F-OW-226).
  - The burnisher borrowed in secret and returned when Agnès was out (F-OW-226).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 1.5.1-2 and her note); Carmel editors. Medium-high. Shows the prioress's control and the tension between Gonzague and Agnès.
- Conflicts: "Relieved of all work" in May versus 18 May "relieved of every office" (see E-P6-031).
- New to English readers: yes (the secrecy and burnisher)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-025 — 2 May 1897 (feast of the Good Shepherd), Carmel of Lisieux — LT 225 to Sr Anne du Sacré-Cœur at Saigon
- What happened: On 2 May 1897 Thérèse writes to a former Sister of the Lisieux Carmel now at Saigon. The prioress said she could write "if it gives you pleasure". She says: "the ardent sun of Saigon is nothing compared to the fire that burns in your soul." She asks Sr Anne to ask Jesus that she may love and make him loved "not with an ordinary love but like the saints who did mad things for him". She writes: "I am ready to cross the world... and I am also ready to die!" She sends respects to Sr Anne's prioress, who "does not know me, but I often hear our good Mother speak of her". She used the "licence of the Good Shepherd", a feast-day permission to talk and write.
- Cards: F-OW-484
- Best details:
  - « je suis prête à traverser le monde... et je suis prête aussi à mourir ! » (F-OW-484).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 225). High. The letter shows the prioress allowing a letter to an overseas Carmel just after the Hanoi wish; it does not mention Sr Anne's health or mission.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-026 — 7 May 1897 — "I cough like a locomotive"
- What happened: "I'm coughing! I'm coughing! It's like a railway engine arriving at the station. I'm arriving at a station too: the station of Heaven, and I'm announcing it!" Also: "Our family won't stay long on earth... We were all born crowned." She had sung her own poem "Ma Joie" while dressing.
- Cards: F-OW-227
- Best details:
  - « Ça fait comme la locomotive d'un chemin de fer quand elle arrive à la gare. » (F-OW-227).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune, CJ 7.5.1-3). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-027 — 9 May 1897 — "I close the learned book that splits my head"
- What happened: On 9 May 1897 she wrote to Roulland that treatises showing perfection through a thousand obstacles tired her poor little mind. She closed "the learned book that splits my head and dries my heart" and took Scripture. The same letter says she hopes as much from God's justice as from His mercy. The letter to Roulland (9 May 1897, LT 226) also says: Thérèse has received "with emotion" relics that Roulland sent, and his letter "is almost a farewell-until-Heaven letter". She quotes Théophane Vénard: "Every missionary's life is fruitful in crosses" and "True happiness is to suffer; to live we must die." She says Père Mazel, "ordained priest the same day as you", "has already gathered the palm". She writes that "what consoles me is to think that at your side I can serve some purpose: the zero has no value by itself, but placed next to the one it becomes powerful, provided it puts itself on the right side, after and not before!" She thanks him for remembering her parents at Mass; her parents "had so wished for a missionary son", and through her a missionary is now their son. On God's justice: she does not understand why he doubts entering Heaven at once if the unbelievers killed him. The justice that frightens many souls "is the subject of my joy and confidence": to be just is not only to punish but to "recognise right intentions and reward virtue". She hopes as much from God's justice as from his mercy, quoting Psalm 102. "This is what I think of God's justice; my way is all confidence and love; I do not understand souls who are afraid of so tender a Friend." She is glad there are "several dwellings" in God's kingdom, for she could not enter the one whose way she cannot understand.
- Cards: F-EW-1395, F-OW-485, F-OW-486
- Best details:
  - « Je ferme le savant livre qui me casse la tête et me dessèche le cœur » (F-EW-1395).
  - « le zéro par lui-même n'a pas de valeur, mais placé près de l'unité il devient puissant, pourvu toutefois qu'il se mette du bon côté, après et non pas avant !... » (F-OW-485).
  - « ma voie est toute de confiance et d'amour, je ne comprends pas les âmes qui ont peur d'un si tendre Ami » (F-OW-486).
  - « Ils avaient tant désiré un fils missionnaire !... » (F-OW-485).
- Evidence: Roulland, letter (LT 226; PO-09 and PA-25). High. | Her own writing (LT 226). High. The 1911 edition prints most of LT 226 as "Lettre VIe (1897)" without the zero image and the parents' paragraph.
- Conflicts: The letter does not say how Père Mazel died. The parents' wish for a missionary son is reported to her ("On m'a raconté") with no source given.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-028 — 13 May 1897 (LT 227); Apr-May 1897 (LT 228), Carmel of Lisieux — Notes to Céline: "prouder of your littleness than of millions of suns"; "Mr Clodion"
- What happened: LT 227 (13 May 1897): "Jesus is pleased with little Céline, to whom he gave himself for the first time 13 years ago; he is prouder of what he does in her soul, of her littleness, her poverty, than he is proud of having created millions of suns and the vastness of the heavens!" LT 228 (April-May 1897 (?)): Thérèse writes that she fears "Our Mother" will not be pleased: "she cares greatly about the E-P6-121 (rub-downs), above all on the back". "If Mr Clodion comes on Sunday to shake his long hair on my back, he will wonder why what he said was not done... Above all do not speak to this poor Mr; and remember that we must be rich, very rich, both of us!"
- Cards: F-OW-487
- Best details:
  - « il est plus fier de ce qu'Il fait dans son âme, de sa petitesse, de sa pauvreté, qu'Il n'est fier d'avoir créé des millions de soleils et l'étendue des Cieux !... » (F-OW-487).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 227, LT 228). High for the words; low for "Mr Clodion" (the notes do not say who he is: a doctor, a Sister, or a joke). Absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: The E-P6-121 show a nursing care ordered by the prioress in spring 1897; no illness is named. "Rich, very rich" is a private phrase of the two sisters (compare E-P6-122).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-029 — May-June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — If cured, she wanted the Carmel of Hanoi, to suffer alone
- What happened: Near the end she told Pauline she would like to be sent to the Hanoi Carmel, if she recovered, to suffer much, be alone and have no joy on earth. The Hanoi Carmel had asked for her. Later she said she would still accept cure to go to Cochinchina. The Carnet jaune dates the Hanoi saying to 15 May (CJ 15.5). In Ms C (9r-10v) she wrote that she had always thought that if Jesus did not take her soon, she would be sent like Noah's dove "to infidel shores". The prioress told her she had the special vocation for foreign Carmels and only her health prevented it. "If the Blessed Virgin cures me" she still desires to leave for a foreign land: "here I am loved, by you and all the Sisters... That is why I dream of a monastery where I would be unknown, where I would suffer poverty, lack of affection, in short the exile of the heart." She admits: "I know my incapacity... having no knowledge of earthly things." LT 221 (19 Mar 1897) is the earliest dated statement of the Hanoi wish: "if Jesus does not come soon to fetch me for the Carmel of Heaven, I will one day leave for the Carmel of Hanoi". The prioress: "the scabbard would be thrown into the sea before reaching Tonkin."
- Cards: F-EW-1389, F-EW-1404, F-OW-228, F-OW-236, F-OW-477
- Best details:
  - "to suffer much, be alone and have no joy on earth" (F-EW-1389).
  - « je rêve un monastère où je serais inconnue, où j'aurais à souffrir la pauvreté, le manque d'affection, enfin l'exil du coeur » (F-OW-236).
  - "the scabbard would be thrown into the sea before reaching Tonkin" (F-OW-477).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910 and 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune); her own writing (Ms C). High. | Her own writing (LT 221). High.
- Conflicts: The notebook places the saying under "15 May", but the editors' reference is DE 15 June (F-EW-1404). | NV dates the Hanoi saying "15 May", but the editors' reference is DE 15 June (F-EW-1404); the Carnet jaune gives 15 May. The Hanoi Carmel is named in her letters of 1896-1897, not in Ms C.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-030 — 15 May 1897 — "If I'm not surprised enough in Heaven, I'll pretend"
- What happened: "I have such a high idea of Heaven that sometimes I wonder how God will manage to surprise me when I die... If I'm not surprised enough, I'll pretend to be, to please God. There'll be no danger of letting him see my disappointment." God says he will reward "according to works": "I have no works! ...Well! he'll reward me according to his own works." She would like to go to Hanoi "to suffer much... to be all alone... As for being useful there, the thought doesn't even cross my mind." "It's all the same to me to live or die."
- Cards: F-OW-228
- Best details:
  - « si je ne suis pas assez surprise, je ferai semblant de l'être, pour faire plaisir au bon Dieu » (F-OW-228).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 15.5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-031 — 18 May 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Relieved of every office; she begs not to be excused the Offices of the Dead
- What happened: On 18 May 1897 she said she had been relieved of every office and thought her death would not disturb the community at all. Pauline had tried to get her dispensed from the Offices of the Dead owed for each deceased nun of the Order. Thérèse begged not to be, since it was all she could do for souls in purgatory. She always kept work ready so as never to waste time. She said that if she had said nothing, no one would think her ill. The Carmelite Constitutions required each nun to say Offices of the Dead for every deceased member of the Order. In May 1897 Thérèse begged not to be dispensed; later she was dispensed. On 6 Aug she asked God to pay her debt to the souls in purgatory "as God", recalling St John of the Cross's line "Acquittez toutes dettes". The Carnet jaune gives the same saying on 18-19 May: "I asked God to follow community life until my death, but he doesn't want it! I could go to all the offices, I'm sure, I wouldn't die one minute sooner. It sometimes seems to me that if I had said nothing, no one would find me ill." She asked not to be stopped from saying her "little" Offices of the Dead. "I always need work ready; that way I'm never preoccupied and never waste time." On 19 May: "Nothing gives me 'little' joys like 'little' troubles."
- Cards: F-EW-1397, F-EW-1510, F-OW-229
- Best details:
  - "if she had said nothing, no one would think her ill" (F-EW-1397).
  - "Acquittez toutes dettes" (F-EW-1510).
  - « Il me semble parfois que si je n'avais rien dit, on ne me trouverait pas malade. » (F-OW-229).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 248r-v). High. She was later dispensed anyway; on 6 Aug she spoke of this poverty as a grace (f. 273v). | Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 248v, f. 273v). High. | Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 18.5.2-4; 19.5). Medium-high; in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-032 — 19 May 1897 — "Nothing gives me joy like pain"
- What happened: Pauline says you could tell Thérèse was suffering when she was gayer at recreation and quicker at work. Asked why she was so joyful, she said it was because she was in pain: nothing gave her joy like pain. Céline also saw her smile at a sharp pain in her side and asked why. Thérèse said she had made a habit of welcoming suffering. Two months before her death, Mère Agnès, hearing her patience praised, came to see her hoping to surprise her in a moment of crisis. At that moment Thérèse's face lit with a heavenly smile. Asked why, she said: because I feel a sharp pain; I have always tried to love suffering and welcome it. The Carnet jaune records the 19 May saying: "Nothing gives me 'little' joys like 'little' troubles."
- Cards: F-EW-1230, F-EW-1262, F-EW-1292, F-EW-1437, F-OW-229
- Best details:
  - Gaiety at recreation and speed at work were the sign that she was in pain (F-EW-1230).
  - "j'ai pris l'habitude de faire bon accueil à la souffrance" (F-EW-1262).
  - Céline: visits were often tiresome but she never showed annoyance (F-EW-1262); she was so gentle that one could mistake the darkness of her soul and her state of health (F-EW-1292).
  - Pauline came "hoping to surprise her in a moment of crisis" (F-EW-1437).
  - "Nothing gives me little joys like little troubles" (F-OW-229).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard, 1910; DE 19 May); Céline (1910 and 1915). High. The smile at the side pain is undated (1897, infirmary). | Marie des Anges (hearsay from Mère Agnès; notes, TEMPERANCE). Medium. | Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune, CJ 19.5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium: the smile that hides the pain.

### E-P6-033 — 19 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — PN 51 "A rose stripped of its petals"
- What happened: PN 51 has five stanzas dated 19 May 1897; it was written at the request of Mère Henriette of the Carmel of Paris (Avenue de Messine). Image: Jesus, taking his first steps from his Mother's arms, and a rose stripped of its petals laid under his feet: "A stripped rose is simply thrown to the wind... like her I abandon myself to you, little Jesus." Mère Henriette, through Marie de la Trinité, asked for a last stanza on the reward in Heaven; Thérèse answered: "Let the good Mother make that verse herself."
- Cards: F-OW-579
- Best details:
  - « Une rose effeuillée sans recherche se donne / Pour n'être plus. » (F-OW-579).
  - "Let the good Mother make that verse herself." (F-OW-579).
- Evidence: Her own writing (PN 51); dates and context from Carmel editors. High for the texts.
- Conflicts: none. The "stripped rose" is the image behind the "shower of roses" saying.
- New to English readers: partial (the request from Paris and the reply about the last stanza are new)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-034 — 20 May 1897 — Her photograph: "that's the envelope; when will we see the letter?"
- What happened: Shown her photograph, she said: "Yes, but... that's the envelope; when will we see the letter? Oh, how I'd like to see the letter!"
- Cards: F-OW-230
- Best details:
  - « c'est l'enveloppe ; quand est-ce qu'on verra la lettre ? » (F-OW-230).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 20.5.2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-035 — May (?) 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — PN 50 "To Joan of Arc"
- What happened: PN 50 "To Joan of Arc" (5 stanzas, "May ? 1897", for herself): the editors say she wrote it spontaneously, very ill, rereading her plays, on the theme of prison. It says Joan appears "brighter and more beautiful" in her dark prison than at the king's coronation, and that betrayal brought her the heavenly glow.
- Cards: F-OW-579
- Best details:
  - « Jeanne tu m'apparais plus brillante et plus belle / Qu'au sacre de ton roi, dans ta sombre prison. » (F-OW-579).
- Evidence: Her own writing (PN 50); Carmel editors (dating "mai ?"). Medium for the date.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: her final view of Joan: not the victorious knight but the prisoner.

### E-P6-036 — 23 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 229: Mère Agnès is "the elevator"
- What happened: To Mère Agnès (23 May 1897), Thérèse fears she has hurt her: "I who would like to be her little joy, I feel on the contrary that I am her little sorrow." When she is "far from this sad earth where flowers wither and birds fly away", she will be very near "my dear Mother, the Angel that Jesus sent before me to prepare the way for me, the way that leads to Heaven, the elevator that was to raise me without effort to the infinite regions of love". "It is not I who will come down, it is my little Mother who will go up to where I am." What Mère Agnès did to her soul, she did to Jesus, "for he said: What you do to the least of mine you do to me. And I am the least!"
- Cards: F-OW-488
- Best details:
  - « ce n'est pas moi qui descendrai, c'est ma petite Mère qui montera là où je serai » (F-OW-488).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 229). High. Absent from the 1911 edition. In Ms C the elevator is Jesus's arms; here it is Pauline.
- Conflicts: "The elevator" as a person (Pauline) versus as Jesus's arms (Ms C, June-July 1897).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-037 — 21-26 May 1897 — "I'm a coward who wants to deserve the compliments"
- What happened: "They've told me so often that I have courage, and it's so little true, that I said: well, I mustn't make everyone a liar! And I set about acquiring this courage... like a warrior who, hearing himself praised for bravery, knowing very well he's only a coward, would end by being ashamed of the compliments and want to deserve them." "If I didn't have this trial of soul... I think I would die of joy at the thought of leaving earth soon."
- Cards: F-OW-232
- Best details:
  - « tout en sachant très bien qu'il n'est qu'un lâche » (F-OW-232).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 21/26.5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-038 — 21-26 May 1897 — "The one God has sent says the same things as God"
- What happened: One evening she was sad and wondered whether God was pleased with her, thinking what each Sister would say of her: one "a good little soul, she can become a saint", another "very sweet, very pious, but this... but that...", and several would find her "very imperfect, which is true". About Mère Agnès: "she loves me so much it blinds her, so I cannot believe her." Then Mère Agnès's note reached her, saying she pleased her in everything and was cherished by God, who had not made her climb "the rough stairs of perfection like the others but put her in an elevator so that she would be more quickly brought to him". Still not fully comforted, she opened her little Gospel asking God to answer, and fell on a passage she had never noticed: "He whom God has sent says the same things as God, because he has not given him his Spirit by measure." She wept with joy and woke "still perfumed". She said to Mère Agnès: "It is you whom God sent for me; you brought me up, you made me enter Carmel; all the great graces of my life I have received through you; so you say the same things as God, and now I believe the good God is very pleased with me, since you tell me so."
- Cards: F-OW-548
- Best details:
  - « Celui que Dieu a envoyé dit les mêmes choses que Dieu, parce qu'il ne lui a pas communiqué son Esprit avec mesure. » (F-OW-548).
  - She woke "still perfumed" (F-OW-548).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ Paroles retrouvées, 21-26 mai, no. 11: sayings left out of later copies). High.
- Conflicts: The elevator remark is Mère Agnès's own note; the Ms C elevator is written in the same weeks. The saying gave Pauline a divine authority: one theme in the later question of Mère Agnès's control of the record ("Remarque importante").
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-039 — late May 1897, Lisieux Carmel — No wreaths: ransom two slave children named Théophane and Marie-Thérèse
- What happened: She had seen wreaths sent for Mère Geneviève's burial. Fearing the same for herself, she asked that no wreaths be given for her coffin. She told Pauline to ask instead for the money to ransom two little African slave children, whom she would protect: "a little Théophane and a little Marie-Thérèse". The Carnet jaune (13 Jul) gives other names: instead of wreaths, "buy three little heathen babies [to baptise]: a little Marie-Louis-Martin, a little Marie-Théophane, a little girl Marie-Cécile... and a little Marie-Thérèse."
- Cards: F-EW-1386, F-EW-1387, F-OW-260
- Best details:
  - « Je voudrais un petit 'Théophane' et une petite 'Marie Thérèse' » (F-EW-1386).
  - Names for the ransomed children: Marie-Louis-Martin, Marie-Théophane, Marie-Cécile, Marie-Thérèse (F-OW-260).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 249r-v, 1910 and 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 13.7.16-17). Medium.
- Conflicts: The 1915 card calls them "little Black children"; the 1910 card "African slave children". | Late-May cards name two children, "Théophane" and "Marie-Thérèse"; this card names four names for three babies.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-040 — c. 25 May 1897, her cell — Still in her cell on the straw mattress
- What happened: Around 25 May 1897 she was still in her cell on her straw mattress (paillasse). She preferred it to the infirmary because no one heard her cough there and she disturbed nobody. Also, when too well cared for she no longer "enjoyed" suffering.
- Cards: F-EW-1398
- Best details:
  - « ici on ne m'entend pas tousser, je ne dérange personne » (F-EW-1398).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; Process 1910; NV f. 250r). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-041 — 27 May 1897 — Théophane Vénard preferred to St Aloysius: he was cheerful
- What happened: Thérèse said she liked St Aloysius Gonzaga less than Théophane Vénard. The Life of Aloysius says he was sad even at recreation, while Vénard was always cheerful. The Carnet jaune gives more (21-27 May): "Théophane Vénard pleases me even more than St Aloysius Gonzaga, because St Aloysius's life is extraordinary and his is quite ordinary. And he speaks himself, whereas for the saint it's another who tells the story." "Vénard loved his family very much, and so do I... I don't understand saints who don't love their family." She asked that no wreaths be bought at her death, but the money used "to buy back little Black children". She links her relapse to a novena to Vénard (Nov 1896) made to know whether she should go to Tonkin: "precisely during the novena I began coughing again... He is calling me."
- Cards: F-EW-1229, F-OW-231
- Best details:
  - Reason given: Aloysius sad even at recreation; Vénard always cheerful (F-EW-1229).
  - « Je ne comprends pas les saints qui n'aiment pas leur famille... » (F-OW-231).
  - The relapse during the Vénard novena of Nov 1896: "He is calling me." (F-OW-231).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, Process 1910; date from Derniers Entretiens 27 May). High. | Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 21/26.5, 27.5.10). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-042 — 27 May 1897 (Ascension) — The lottery ticket; "they want me!"
- What happened: "I'm like someone with a lottery ticket... I have a ticket, it's my illness, and I can keep good hope!" A little neighbour at Les Buissonnets, aged three, called by other children, said: "Mama! they want me! let me go, please..." "It seems to me the little angels are calling me today... I don't hear them, but I feel them." She wanted an obituary circular: "it's so sweet to know each other a little, to know a bit whom we'll live with eternally." "I always see the good side of things... If I have only pure suffering... I make it my joy... like Papa's trials, which make me more glorious than a queen."
- Cards: F-OW-233
- Best details:
  - « j'ai un billet, c'est ma maladie » (F-OW-233).
  - The three-year-old neighbour: "Mama! they want me!" (F-OW-233).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune CJ 27.5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-043 — 28 May 1897 (some sources cite a letter of 25 Apr 1897) — A feverish request to paint, and a flushed face
- What happened: A Sister asked the feverish Thérèse to paint for her. Agnès objected, but the nun insisted. Thérèse's face flushed from the inner struggle. That evening she wrote to Agnès that she was glad Agnès had seen her imperfection. PO 1 and PA 6 date it 28 May 1897, during a strong fever: someone came in front of Pauline to ask for help with a delicate painting job, and her face reddened for a moment as she fought impatience. That evening she wrote Pauline (LT 230) that she had shed tears of repentance and gratitude. She wrote that she had "shown her virtue, her treasures of patience", and was happier to have been imperfect. The Saint-Jean-Baptiste biography names the Sister as Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste, demanding immediate help with a painting for Gonzague's feast. Thérèse excused herself, Agnès objected, the Sister insisted and Thérèse grew impatient; the Sister left. That evening the Sister embraced her: "Poor little sister, you made me feel sorry for you; I was wrong." Thérèse "could not get over it"; God's mercy "overflowed" for her that evening. LT 230 (28 May 1897, the letter itself): "your little girl has shed sweet tears again, tears of repentance, but more of gratitude and love. This evening I showed you my virtue, my TREASURES of patience! And I who preach so well to others!" She is glad Mère Agnès saw her imperfection, and did not scold her: "your gentleness says more than harsh words; you are for me the image of God's mercy". "Sr St J. Baptiste, on the contrary, is usually the image of God's severity." She met her afterwards: Sr St John Baptist embraced her, saying "Poor little sister, you made me feel sorry for you, I do not want to tire you, I was wrong, etc." Thérèse says the Sister spoke so because "she thinks I am going to die". Back in her cell she remembered Jesus to the adulteress ("Has no one condemned you?") and answered: "No one, Lord... neither my little Mother, image of your tenderness, nor my Sr St J. Baptist, image of your justice, and I feel I can go in peace, for you will not condemn me either." She adds: "I am much happier to have been imperfect than if, held up by grace, I had been a model of gentleness." The 1911 edition (Lettre VIIIe, 28 May 1897) adds an editorial preface with a painting job and a fever, hides the Sister's name as "Sœur ***", and has "laissez l'ouvrage que je vous ai demandé, j'ai eu tort", which the letter does not have.
- Cards: F-EW-1220, F-EW-1399, F-EW-1400, F-EW-1401, F-OW-489, F-OW-490
- Best details:
  - She wrote that evening: glad Agnès had seen her imperfection; letter of 25 Apr 1897 cited (F-EW-1220).
  - « Ce soir, je vous ai montré ma vertu, mes trésors de patience! Et moi qui prêche si bien les autres!!! » (F-EW-1399, LT 230).
  - The Sister's reversal: « Pauvre petite soeur, vous m'avez fait pitié, je ne veux pas vous fatiguer, j'ai eu tort » (F-EW-1401).
  - « c'est parce qu'elle croit que je vais mourir qu'elle m'a ainsi parlé » (F-OW-489).
  - "No one, Lord... neither my little Mother, image of your tenderness, nor my Sr St J. Baptist, image of your justice" (F-OW-489).
  - The 1911 text drops the "à travers les barreaux" paragraph and the sentence about the Angel (F-OW-490).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès in the Summarium 1919, via Laveille. High. | Mère Agnès (saw, 1910 and 1915); Thérèse's own note, quoted in a Carmel biography of the Sister. High. | Her own writing (LT 230); the 1911 edition compared. High.
- Conflicts: The nun is not named. F-EW-1236 notes a possible link to a painting request in F-EW-1474 (later chunk); not confirmed. | Date: 28 May 1897 (F-EW-1399, F-EW-1400, F-EW-1401) versus the 25 Apr 1897 letter cited in F-EW-1220. F-EW-1400 notes it may be the same jealous Sister as F-EW-1236 (not stated); F-EW-1401 names Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste. | The letter does not say what Thérèse did or said that lost her patience, and does not mention a painting job or a fever. The painting job and the fever come from Pauline's memory (1911 preface, 1910 and 1915 depositions). The 1911 text adds a cause the letter does not give and hides the Sister's name (Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste). Do not state the cause from the letter alone.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a documented flash of impatience, and the Sister's apology that evening.

### E-P6-044 — 30 May 1897 — The note about the Good Friday 1896 haemoptysis
- What happened: On 30 May Mère Agnès was hurt not to have been told of the Good Friday 1896 haemoptysis. Thérèse wrote her a note: she had hidden "a little corner of the envelope", never "a single line of the letter". "It was on Good Friday that Jesus began to tear the envelope of your little letter a little." Mère Agnès learned of the 1896 haemoptysis only on 30 May 1897, over a year later. The Carmel's "fabrication" page says Agnès saw the illness was far advanced when Thérèse told her on 30 May. LT 231 and LT 232 (30 May 1897, two notes to Mère Agnès). First: "Do not be sad that your little girl seemed to hide something from you... if she hid a little corner of the envelope, she never hid a single line of the letter, and who knows it better than you, this little letter you love so much? To the others one can show the envelope from all sides, since they can only see that, but to you!!!" She adds: "you know now that it was on Good Friday that Jesus began to tear the envelope a little of your little letter; are you not glad he is preparing to read it, this letter you have been writing for 24 years?" In the second note she says she had put the first note in Sr Geneviève's hand as Geneviève gave her Agnès's own, regrets having posted it, and will "pay double postage" to say she understands Agnès's grief. She writes: "I beg her, after my death, not to believe what may be told her." She ends: "the letter is yours; please keep writing it until the day Jesus tears the little envelope completely."
- Cards: F-OW-230, F-LF-241, F-OW-491
- Best details:
  - "a little corner of the envelope", never "a single line of the letter" (F-OW-230).
  - « je la supplie après ma mort de ne point croire ce qu'on pourra lui dire » (F-OW-491).
  - « c'est le Vendredi Saint que Jésus a commencé à déchirer un peu l'enveloppe » (F-OW-491).
- Evidence: Thérèse's own written billet (CJ 30.5.1); Ms C 4v-5r gives her account of Good Friday 1896. High for the note; medium-high for the saying. | Her own writing (LT 231, LT 232). High. Absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: What Thérèse had "hidden" is not stated in the letters ("the envelope" seems to be her body and health, "the letter" her soul, but the note does not say so). This is the earliest recorded place where Thérèse asks her sister to distrust what others say after her death; it matters for how the community later spoke of her.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-045 — 1880s–1897 — Her chosen saints and friends
- What happened: Céline says Thérèse had a statuette of her guardian angel in her girlhood room. Her patrons were St Martin, St Francis de Sales and St Teresa, and she loved the writings of St John of the Cross. Her chosen friends were St Cecilia ("the saint of abandonment"), Joan of Arc, Théophane Vénard and the Holy Innocents.
- Cards: F-EW-1255
- Best details:

- Evidence: Céline (first-hand, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-046 — 1880s–1897 — "She would have been very E-P6-046"
- What happened: Céline says Thérèse worked without faltering on her natural inclinations, "for she would have been very lively (quick) if she had not overcome herself". Living with her in total intimacy, Céline never noticed the "tendency to impose herself" that Thérèse writes about.

- Best details:

- Conflicts: Céline saw no "tendency to impose herself"; Thérèse's own manuscript names it.

### E-P6-047 — 1897, ante-choir — Near fainting before Mass, she stayed for communion
- What happened: One morning, already very ill, Thérèse sat near the ante-choir before the 8 o'clock Mass, exhausted and close to fainting. She stayed because she would not miss a communion.

### E-P6-048 — 1897 — The smile as a learned habit
- What happened: The Sisters wondered at her serenity in great pain. Later they learned why. Thérèse had said that when she suffered much she answered with a smile. At first she did not always succeed, but now it was a habit.
- Cards: F-EW-1319
- Best details:

### E-P6-049 — 1897, before July, Lisieux Carmel — Dr de Cornière reads "Vivre d'amour" and gives up hope
- What happened: The prioress gave Dr de Cornière Thérèse's poem "Vivre d'amour". She was not yet ill then, only in her "mucous state". After reading it he said he would never cure her; she was a soul not made for earth. Marie de l'Eucharistie's letter of 8 July 1897 records this. Piat's account of the same remark: Marie wrote to her father that some time earlier Mother Agnès had given Dr de Cornière the poem "Vivre d'amour"; Thérèse was not yet ill, only in her mucous state. After reading it he said: "I will never cure her; it is a soul not made for earth."
- Cards: F-EW-1325, F-EW-1341, F-EW-2619
- Best details:
  - « Je ne vous la guérirai jamais: c'est une âme qui n'est pas faite pour la terre » (F-EW-1325).
  - "état muqueux" is the family's term for her condition before diagnosis (F-EW-1325).
  - Marie Guérin's notes were never given at the process because she died in 1906; the surviving text is a copy of c. June-July 1910 of a lost 1897 notebook (F-EW-1341).
  - Adds context to the doctor's remark: he said it after reading the poem, before the July crisis (F-EW-2619).
- Evidence: Marie de l'Eucharistie (letter 8 July 1897, hearsay). Medium. | Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The surviving text is a copy made during the process, open to editing (F-EW-1341 notes). | Piat says Agnès gave him the poem; F-EW-1325 says the prioress gave it.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-050 — June 1897 — "Your little lambs think me severe"
- What happened: She wrote to the prioress that the novices thought her severe.

  - "Your little lambs think me severe" (F-EW-1402).
