### E-P6-051 — June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — "If I could have been a priest, it would be this June ordination"
- What happened: In June 1897, feeling very ill and taken by pulmonary tuberculosis, she told Céline that God would take her at an age when she would not have had time to be a priest, had she been able. She said that if she could have been a priest, she would have received holy Orders at this June ordination. So that she should regret nothing, God let her be ill. She was delighted that St Barbara, a virgin, brought Communion to St Stanislaus Kostka, and thought those who desired it on earth would share its privileges in heaven. Céline's "Varia 4" records that early in 1897, "well before she was ill", Thérèse expected to die that year. In June she gave the reason: "God is going to take me at an age when I would not have had time to be a priest if I could have been a priest; it's at this June ordination that I would have received holy Orders. Well, so that I regret nothing, God lets me be ill, so I could not have gone, and I would die before exercising my ministry."
- Cards: F-EW-1408, F-EW-1409, F-EW-1412, F-OW-245
- Best details:
  - « Si j'avais pu être prêtre, ce serait à ce mois de juin, à cette ordination que j'aurais reçu les saints Ordres » (F-EW-1408).
  - She would have been 24½ (F-EW-1408 notes).
  - She expected to die in 1897 "well before she was ill" (F-OW-245).
- Evidence: Céline (heard; 1910, 1915, Conseils et Souvenirs). High. | An unnamed recorder (Varia in Céline's collection). Medium.
- Conflicts: Recorder not named. Links to Ms B 2v.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: illness as a way out of a desire she could not fulfil.

### E-P6-052 — night of 2-3 June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Pauline's midnight visit to Gonzague: the order to write Manuscript C
- What happened: On the evening of 2 June 1897, near midnight, Pauline went to the prioress, Mère Marie de Gonzague. She said she could not sleep before telling a secret. She described Thérèse's childhood notebook as "nice" but of little use for the death circular, and suggested that Gonzague order Thérèse to write about her religious life. Next morning Gonzague gave the order. The Carmel's account: in the night of 2 Jun 1897 Mère Agnès asked Marie de Gonzague to have Thérèse write more, and from 3-4 Jun Thérèse wrote Ms C in a black notebook (F-LF-231). Piat's account adds that the order followed Marie's veiling that day (F-EW-2616).
- Cards: F-EW-1413, F-LF-231, F-EW-2616
- Best details:
  - « c'est gentil, mais vous ne pourrez pas en tirer grand' chose pour vous aider à faire sa circulaire après sa mort » (F-EW-1413).
  - Pauline feared Gonzague would not value the text as she did, and hid that she had already read it closely (F-EW-1413).
  - The black notebook (F-LF-231).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (first-person account, 1910). High. | Carmel archive ("la fabrication" page); Piat. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a near-midnight visit that starts Manuscript C.

### E-P6-053 — 2 June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Marie Guérin's veiling and Thérèse's note
- What happened: Marie took the veil on 2 Jun 1897, sixteen years to the day after her First Communion, after a sermon by Abbé Levasseur. Thérèse gave her a picture of the Child God she had long kept in her breviary, with a dedication and a note contrasting the white communicant's veil with the dark veil that reminds the bride of Jesus "that she is exiled, that her Spouse is not one who is to lead her to feasts, but to the mountain of Calvary". Piat calls it "almost a testament". That evening Agnès asked Gonzague to order Thérèse to continue writing her memories. This was done the next day. A month later, on 2 Jul, Thérèse handed the prioress the notebook that would become Manuscript C. Thérèse's own words on Marie's veiling: on 2 June 1897 she writes to Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (LT 234, 235). She says that on 2 June long ago "all Heaven gathered to watch" the mystery of Jesus giving himself to Marie for the first time in Communion. "It is no longer the graceful veil with long snowy folds that must wrap Marie, it is a dark veil that recalls to the bride of Jesus that she is in exile, that her Spouse is not one who will lead her to feasts, but up the mountain of Calvary. From now on Marie must look at nothing here below but the merciful God, the Jesus of the Eucharist." A second note (LT 235): "Souvenir of the beautiful day of the taking of the veil... May Thérèse's Little Jesus always caress Marie de l'Eucharistie." On 3 Jun 1897 she writes to Léonie: "How sweet it is to think that one day we will follow the Lamb together for eternity" (LT 238).
- Cards: F-EW-2616, F-OW-493
- Best details:
  - The dark veil: her Spouse leads her "to the mountain of Calvary" (F-EW-2616).
  - 2 Jul 1897: Thérèse hands the prioress the notebook of Ms C (F-EW-2616).
  - « Ce n'est plus le gracieux voile aux longs plis neigeux qui doit envelopper Marie de l'Eucharistie, c'est un sombre voile qui rappelle à l'Epouse de Jésus qu'elle est exilée » (F-OW-493).
- Evidence: Fr Piat (1967), quoting Marie's notes and letters (modern author). Medium-high on the dates. | Her own writing (LT 234, 235, 238). High.
- Conflicts: Fixes 3 Jun 1897 for the second order to write; compare Agnès's own testimony (F-EW-1413). "The notebook handed 2 Jul" versus "stopped writing early July, last page in pencil" (F-EW-1424). | The letter says 2 June is also the day of Marie's first Communion (« le jour de sa première visite »); the letter does not describe the ceremony itself.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-054 — 1-3 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — Three tiny notes to Mère Agnès: "too moving", "the dove", "baby is going to sleep"
- What happened: LT 233 (1 Jun 1897): "It is too moving, far too melodious! I would rather be silent than try in vain to sing what is going on in my little soul. Thank you, little Mother!" LT 237 (2 Jun 1897): "No, the little dove does not want to leave her little Mother; she wants always to fly and rest in the delightful little world of her heart. Tomorrow I will say thank you to my little Mother; I say nothing tonight so as not to break her heart and because it is too late. Baby is going to sleep." LT 239 (3 (?) Jun 1897): "I must walk until my last moment. It is he who will end my torment, like the poor Wandering Jew".
- Cards: F-OW-492
- Best details:
  - « Bébé va faire dodo. » (F-OW-492).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 233, 237, 239). High for the words; low for what "it is too moving" refers to.
- Conflicts: The text does not say what "it" or "he" is in "it is he who will end my torment" (presumably death or Jesus).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-055 — June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — The notebook "too beautiful"
- What happened: Pauline chose an ordinary notebook for Manuscript C. Thérèse found it too beautiful and feared a fault against poverty. She asked if she should at least write the lines close together to save paper. Pauline told her to space the lines and write large because she was too ill. The first manuscript is on the thinnest, poorest paper one could find. Pauline showed the notebook to the tribunal.
- Cards: F-EW-1403, F-EW-1406
- Best details:
  - She asked to squeeze the lines to save paper (F-EW-1403, F-EW-1406).
  - Pauline showed the notebook in court (F-EW-1403).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; 1910 and 1915; object shown in court). High.
- Conflicts: 1915 says Gonzague gave the order to write the third part (F-EW-1406).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-056 — 2, 3 (?) and 6 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — Notes to the novice Marie de la Trinité: "bear your trial alone", "stop wanting to eat earth"
- What happened: LT 236 (2 Jun): "God wants you to bear your trial alone... but my dear little p., I suffer with you!!! and I love you much." "I will come and find you a few minutes tomorrow morning, and the day after the washing I will go with you to the breads." LT 240 (3 (?) Jun): "Dear little flower of Jesus, I have understood everything; you need not tell me a lot: the little eye in your calyx shows me what to think of the whole little flower... But you must no longer want to eat earth; the forget-me-not must open, or rather raise its corolla, so that the Bread of Angels may come like a divine dew to strengthen it." LT 242 (6 Jun): she is glad her sadness does not make Marie melancholy. She laughs at her question about her "writings" ("what in-folios do you mean?") and says: "You will learn one day, if not on earth then in Heaven".
- Cards: F-OW-494
- Best details:
  - « il ne faut plus avoir envie de manger de la terre, il faut que le myosotis entrouvre ou plutôt élève sa corolle afin que le Pain des Anges vienne comme une Rosée Divine la fortifier » (F-OW-494).
  - « à quels in-folio faites-vous allusion ? » (F-OW-494).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 236, 240, 242). High for the words; the trial is not described. Absent from the 1911 edition ("manger de la terre", "in-folio").
- Conflicts: In LT 242 Marie de la Trinité can no longer speak to Thérèse ("I understand your pain at no longer being able to speak to me"), so the exchange is by letters within the house: the talks were cut by illness or by the prioress (the note does not say). "In-folios" suggests the novices already knew she was writing (Ms C).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-057 — 4 June 1897 (editors' tag DE); Marie dates it 22 August — A beautiful death, asked of the Virgin and not of God
- What happened: Céline told Thérèse the angels would come for her and they would love to see them. Thérèse said they would not see them, but the angels would be there. She would like a beautiful death to please her sisters. She had asked the Virgin, not God, so as not to force God. Marie's second account adds why she asked the Virgin: the Virgin handles her little wishes and passes them on or not.
- Cards: F-EW-1414, F-EW-1415
- Best details:
  - « Je voudrais pourtant bien avoir une belle mort, pour vous faire plaisir. » (F-EW-1414).
  - « Je l'ai demandé à la Sainte Vierge » (F-EW-1415).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, present; Process 1910). High. | Marie (present, 1915). Medium on the date.
- Conflicts: Marie dates it 22 August; the editors' tag gives DE 4 June 1897. | Date: 22 Aug (Marie) versus editors' tag DE 4 June 1897.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-058 — 4-7 June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Farewell to her sisters in her cell: "I am soon to die"
- What happened: On 4 Jun 1897, with her three sisters in her cell, she bade farewell, "radiant... as if transfigured": "how happy I am! I see that I am soon to die… I would wish, however, to have an easy death in order to console you. But do not feel disturbed if I suffer greatly." On 7 Jun, returning from the garden with Mère Agnès, she saw a white hen with chicks and wept. Marie asked, "What a sorrow it will be for us when you die!" and she said, "Oh no… there will be as it were a shower of roses." The Carmel editors' note: Ms C begun 4 Jun 1897 after Mère Agnès discovered the 1896 haemoptysis. In early June she had side pain, vomiting, anguish and loss of appetite. On 6 Jun came "the famous snail syrup" and a milk diet. There was improvement in mid-June after a community novena to Notre-Dame des Victoires (5-13 Jun); fever and side pain were back on 25 Jun. On 4 Jun, lying on Céline's straw mattress in her cell, she said goodbye to her sisters: "Don't be surprised if I don't appear to you after my death and you see nothing extraordinary as a sign of my happiness... it's 'my little way' to want to see nothing." "Don't be upset if I suffer a lot and you see in me no sign of happiness at the moment of death. Our Lord died a Victim of Love, and see what his agony was!"
- Cards: F-LF-232, F-OW-237
- Best details:
  - "how happy I am! I see that I am soon to die" (F-LF-232).
  - "do not feel disturbed if I suffer greatly" (F-LF-232).
  - « Ne vous étonnez pas si je ne vous apparais pas après ma mort » (F-OW-237).
  - "Our Lord died a Victim of Love, and see what his agony was!" (F-OW-237).
- Evidence: Laveille (Summarium 1919 pp. 874-875). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 4.6.1); Carmel editors' note (cj-juin-1897). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Marie's version dates the rose saying 9 June at the refectory (F-EW-1421); Laveille puts it with the hen scene, 7 June. | The novena to Our Lady of Victories: begun 5 June (D6, F-EW-2106) and running 5-13 June with mid-June improvement (F-OW-237), but "no cure followed" (F-EW-2106).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-059 — June 1897, Ms C 1r-2r — Manuscript C opens: for Mère Marie de Gonzague
- What happened: Ms C opens "June 1897" addressed to Mère Marie de Gonzague, who asked her to finish "singing the mercies of the Lord". "Many Sisters think you have spoiled me... it is not so." She thanks her for "not having spared me": the little flower needed "the water of humiliation". For a year and a half Jesus has made the flower grow by "the sun" of the prioress's smile. "I act with you like a child because you do not act with me as a prioress but as a mother." She writes for obedience, "not to make a literary work", and says the prioress may burn it before her eyes without reading it.
- Cards: F-OW-234
- Best details:
  - « je n'éprouverais aucune peine si vous le brûliez sous mes yeux avant de l'avoir lu » (F-OW-234).
  - "the water of humiliation" (F-OW-234).
- Evidence: Her own writing (Ms C 1r-2r; 6r; 32v-33r). High.
- Conflicts: The 1898 and 1911 editions treat Ms A as if also addressed to Marie de Gonzague ("ma Mère vénérée"); see the Pauline-edits card.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-060 — c. June 1897, stairs to her cell — Half an hour to climb the stairs; MT raised the alarm
- What happened: The evening before she took to bed for good, Thérèse still came to evening recreation. She told MT that the night before she had needed over half an hour to climb to her cell, sitting on almost every step, and had to make huge efforts to undress alone. Against her wish, MT told Gonzague and Mère Agnès. From then on she was cared for seriously.
- Cards: F-EW-1410
- Best details:
  - Over half an hour, sitting on almost every step (F-EW-1410).
- Evidence: MT (heard from Thérèse; she acted herself). High.
- Conflicts: MT claims she caused the start of real care. Other witnesses may date the move to the infirmary differently (Thérèse moved to the infirmary 8 July 1897).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the stairs, the step-by-step climb, and the betrayal for her own good.

### E-P6-061 — 5 June 1897 — The novena to Our Lady of Victories
- What happened: The community began a novena to Our Lady of Victories on 5 June 1897 for her cure. No cure followed.
- Cards: F-EW-2106
- Best details:
  - "no cure followed" (F-EW-2106).
- Evidence: Prioress's Epilogue (D6 p. 207). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-062 — June 1897; letter of 6 June 1897 (LT 242) — MT removed from nursing, novices barred
- What happened: When Thérèse stopped in June 1897, MT lost her post as aide-infirmarian and novices were forbidden to talk with Thérèse so as not to tire her. MT found this very hard and wrote to her. Thérèse replied on 6 June 1897 (LT 242): she also suffered, and MT held "an immense place" in her heart. MT admits she had two parentheses added to the letter as read into the record, to "clarify" Thérèse's meaning. LT 242 (6 Jun 1897) is the letter in which MT's reply came: see the notes to the novice. In it Thérèse also answers whether she would be glad to go to Paradise (see E-P6-065).
- Cards: F-EW-1411, F-OW-494, F-OW-495
- Best details:
  - "an immense place" in her heart (F-EW-1411).
  - MT had two parentheses added to LT 242 in the record (F-EW-1411).
  - LT 242 is the dated letter that MT had glossed with two parentheses (F-EW-1411).
- Evidence: MT (PA 21 and PO 17). High. Source criticism: the witness glossed a text in the record; her gloss reads "sickness is too slow a guide" as about the arrival of death. | Her own writing (LT 242). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-063 — 6 June 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Only a particle of the host; the chaplain calls her doubts "dangerous"
- What happened: On 6 June she thanked Pauline for asking that she be given only a particle of the host, since she still found it hard to swallow. She cried as on her First Communion day. The chaplain told her not to dwell on her temptations against faith, "it is very dangerous", and asked if she was resigned to die. She said resignation was needed only to live. Thérèse cited the chaplain's reply as proof of how little consolation she received.
- Cards: F-EW-1417, F-EW-1418
- Best details:
  - She wept as on her First Communion day (F-EW-1417).
  - "resignation was needed only to live" (F-EW-1417).
  - The chaplain: "it is very dangerous" (F-EW-1417, F-EW-1418).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard Thérèse; NV f. 252v, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: The chaplain is not named in the cards (Abbé Youf per note). Card note: pastoral misunderstanding rather than hostility.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a chaplain who does not understand the trial of faith.

### E-P6-064 — 6 June 1897 — Snail syrup and raw eggs; the chaplain on her temptations
- What happened: They hid from her that her syrup was snail syrup. She noticed and laughed: "What does it matter to me to take snail syrup, as long as I don't see the horns! Now I eat snails like the little ducks! Yesterday I did like the ostriches, I ate raw eggs!" The chaplain Youf told her about her temptations against faith: "Don't dwell on that, it's very dangerous." "Not very comforting to hear." "Are you resigned to die?" "Ah, Father, I find one needs resignation only to live. To die, it's joy I feel." When Mère Agnès said she would photograph her "to please Our Mother", she quoted her father's Auvergnat story: "Little north wind, stop blowing! It's not for me, it's for my comrade who has no jacket..."
- Cards: F-OW-240
- Best details:
  - « pourvu que je ne voie pas les cornes ! » (F-OW-240).
  - Youf is named as the chaplain who called her doubts "dangerous" (F-OW-240; the NV cards do not name him).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 6.6). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes (snail syrup, Youf's warning)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-065 — 6 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — "I no longer count on sickness, it is too slow a guide; I count only on love"
- What happened: Marie de la Trinité asked whether Thérèse would be glad to go to Paradise. Thérèse answers: "I would be very glad if I went, but... I do not count on sickness, it is too slow a guide. I count only on love. Ask the good Jesus that all the prayers said for me may serve to increase the Fire that is to consume me."
- Cards: F-OW-495
- Best details:
  - « je ne compte pas sur la maladie, c'est une trop lente conductrice. Je ne compte plus que sur l'amour » (F-OW-495).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 242, 6 June 1897). High.
- Conflicts: The 1911 edition prints "la maladie est une trop lente conductrice, je ne compte que sur l'amour" in the editors' narrative about 1896, so the 1911 book puts the saying in spring 1896: a redating. The saying now has a dated source in her own hand: 6 June 1897.
- New to English readers: partial (the saying is in Taylor 1912, dated to 1896; the original letter and its date are not)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-066 — 7 June 1897, Carmel garden — The little white hen and her chicks
- What happened: Walking in the garden, supported by Pauline, Thérèse stopped to watch a little white hen sheltering her chicks under her wings. Her eyes filled with tears. Asked why, she covered her eyes and cried more. Later she said she wept thinking that God chose this image to show His tenderness, and that He had hidden her all her life under His wings. Laveille places the "shower of roses" reply to Marie right after the hen scene on 7 June. The Carnet jaune adds: sitting in the garden by the cemetery she laid her head on Mère Agnès's heart and sang "Me forget you, dear Mother, no, never!" Going down the steps she saw under the medlar tree the little white hen with all her chicks under her wings; she stood looking, eyes full of tears, and could not say why. That evening: "I cried thinking that God chose this comparison to make us believe in his tenderness. All my life that's what he has done for me! He has hidden me entirely under his wings!" It was ten years to the day since her father gave her the little white flower.
- Cards: F-EW-1419, F-EW-1420, F-LF-232, F-OW-241
- Best details:
  - She covered her eyes and cried more when asked why (F-EW-1419).
  - Gratitude made her cry even at small things (F-EW-1420).
  - "there will be as it were a shower of roses" (F-LF-232).
  - « Il m'a entièrement cachée sous ses ailes !... » (F-OW-241).
  - The hen under the medlar tree (F-OW-241).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Laveille. High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 7.6). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Date of the roses saying: 7 June (Laveille) versus 9 June (Marie). | Ms A 50v dates the flower to Pentecost 29 May 1887 (Pentecost 1897 was 6 Jun; the anniversary is liturgical).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a hen, chicks and the tears of a dying girl.

### E-P6-067 — 7 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 243 to Céline: "let us run to the last place"
- What happened: To Céline, 7 June 1897: "Let us never seek what looks great in the eyes of creatures. Solomon, the wisest king ever on earth, looked at the works that occupy men under the sun, painting, sculpture, all the arts, and understood that all these things are subject to envy; he cried that they are vanity and affliction of spirit." "The only thing that is not envied is the last place; so only the last place is not vanity and affliction of spirit." She says we sometimes catch ourselves wanting what shines. Then "let us humbly rank ourselves among the imperfect, think of ourselves as little souls that God must hold up at every moment." Quoting Psalm 93: "But as soon as I said: my foot has slipped, your mercy, Lord, held me up." "It is enough to humble oneself, to bear one's imperfections gently. That is true holiness! Let us take each other by the hand, dear little sister, and run to the last place... no one will come to dispute it with us."
- Cards: F-OW-496
- Best details:
  - « Prenons-nous par la main, petite soeur chérie, et courons à la dernière place... personne ne viendra nous la disputer... » (F-OW-496).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 243). High. The 1911 edition prints "il suffit de s'humilier... voilà la vraie sainteté" in a different setting; "courons à la dernière place" is absent.
- Conflicts: none. She gives an artist (Céline) advice about painting and envy; the letter gives no occasion.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-068 — 8 June 1897 — "Even to be taken in dislike"
- What happened: On 8 June, after a talk about long illnesses that tire the nurses, which is a great suffering for patients who notice it, she said: "I am willing to stay like this until the end of a very long life; if it pleases the good God I am even willing to be 'taken in dislike' (prise en grippe)."
- Cards: F-OW-549
- Best details:
  - « je veux même bien être «prise en grippe.» » (F-OW-549).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ Paroles retrouvées, 8 juin). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-069 — 9 June 1897 (DE); novena March 1897; year of her death — "I too will make roses rain after my death"
- What happened: After a novena of grace to St Francis Xavier (March 1897), Thérèse told Marie she had asked to do good after death and was sure to be heard. In the year of her death the Life of St Aloysius Gonzaga was read in the refectory. In the week Marie was reader, it told of a sick person who knew she would be cured when roses fell softly on her bed. Right after the meal Thérèse said to Marie "with an accent I shall never forget": "I too will make roses rain after my death." TSA reads the daily communion granted immediately after her death as the promised "shower of roses". Marie's 1910 account: she was reading aloud in the refectory a story from the life of St Aloysius Gonzaga, where a sick man asking for his cure saw roses fall on his bed. At recreation afterwards Thérèse told her she too would make roses rain after her death. TSA says the shower of roses Thérèse promised was, "without any doubt", the daily communion the community received right after her death and has had since without break (PA-09 [817], 1915). An unusual, local reading of the famous promise. The Carnet jaune gives the 9 June exchange: to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, "How sad we'll be when you leave us!": "Oh no, you'll see, it will be like a shower of roses." According to Claude Langlois (Carmel site), the two process versions of the sayings date the "shower of roses" to 9 Jun 1897. Marie explained in her deposition that she had read in the refectory the Life of St Aloysius Gonzaga, where a sick man saw roses fall on his bed as a sign of healing. Thérèse then said at recreation: "Me too, after my death I will make roses rain." The other saying, "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth", was said separately on 17 Jul 1897. The first Histoire d'une âme (1898, ch. XII) printed "After my death, she had told us gracefully, I will make a shower of roses fall". The phrase vanished from the 1899-1900 editions and returned in 1901, paired with "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth", which had been written on the wooden cross of her grave. At the process the witnesses cite "spend my heaven" about twenty times and mostly ignore the roses. Céline drew "Thérèse covering her crucifix with roses" in 1912.
- Cards: F-EW-1374, F-EW-1384, F-EW-1342, F-EW-1421, F-EW-1711, F-OW-242, F-OW-243
- Best details:
  - « Moi aussi je ferai pleuvoir des roses après ma mort. » (F-EW-1374).
  - "Moi aussi" means "I too", like St Aloysius (F-EW-1384).
  - TSA: the promise fulfilled as daily communion, continued without break (F-EW-1342).
  - The setting is the refectory reading, at recreation afterwards (F-EW-1421).
  - "without any doubt" (F-EW-1711).
  - « Oh ! non, vous verrez, ce sera comme une pluie de roses. » (F-OW-242).
  - The phrase vanished from the 1899-1900 editions and returned in 1901 (F-OW-243).
  - "spend my heaven" is cited about twenty times at the process; the roses are mostly ignored (F-OW-243).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, Feuilles ajoutées and 1915); TSA (her interpretation). High; medium for TSA's reading. | Marie (heard, 1910; date 9 June from DE). High. | TSA (her own interpretation, 1915). High that she held this view. | Mère Agnès (CJ 9.6); Langlois (scholarship, facts only; secondhand via a modern historian quoting the process). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Marie dates the saying 9 June 1897 (DE); the novena was March 1897. TSA's interpretation agrees with F-EW-1337. | 1910 says she told her at recreation; the Feuilles ajoutées say right after the meal. | Marie's deposition places the refectory reading and the recreation remark on 9 June; Laveille puts the roses reply with the hen scene on 7 June. The famous saying is recorded by her Sisters, not written by her.
- New to English readers: yes (the refectory-reading origin)
- Scene potential: high: the famous promise in its actual setting.

### E-P6-070 — 9 June 1897 to 12 July 1897 — "God will come like a thief": the Thief
- What happened: "It says in the Gospel that God will come like a thief. He will come and steal me very nicely. Oh, how I'd like to help the Thief!" "My trial?" "Not passed, but it's as if suspended. The nasty snakes aren't hissing in my ears any more." "I'm like a little child on the railway waiting for his papa and mama to put him on the train. Alas, they don't come and the train leaves! But there are others..." Céline's Varia records: "God has gardens he entrusts to owners, and usually he never picks fruit without their permission; but God wants a little bunch of grapes so much that the owner won't give him that he's going to steal it." The prioress could not bring herself to give Thérèse "permission" to die; from then on Thérèse called Jesus "the Thief". Marie du Sacré-Cœur recorded (8-9 Jul): "The 'Thief' is far away, he's gone to steal other children!" "On 9 June I saw the Thief." After the doctor found her better: "The 'Thief' has gone again!"
- Cards: F-OW-242, F-OW-259
- Best details:
  - "Oh, how I'd like to help the Thief!" (F-OW-242).
  - « on m'a mise «dans un lit de malheur», dans un lit qui fait manquer le train » (F-OW-259).
  - "Not passed, but it's as if suspended": the trial suspended on 9 June (F-OW-242).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 9.6.1-5); Céline (Varia 3); Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Dernières paroles). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Céline's Varia note ties "the Thief" to the prioress's refusal of "permission" to die; Piat gives the thief as death or Christ (see E-P6-159). The trial "suspended" on 9 June versus the trial of faith described as unremitting in July-September.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-071 — 9 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 244 to Bellière: "a last little word of farewell"; "I am not dying, I am entering into life"
- What happened: On 9 June 1897 Thérèse writes to Bellière while "the infirmarian is absent", "a last little word of farewell; when you receive it I will have left exile". She says she is "happy to die", "not because I will be freed from suffering here below (suffering, on the contrary, is the only thing that seems desirable to me in this valley of tears) but because I feel it is God's will." Her prioress "would like to keep me on earth"; a novena of Masses is being said "to Our Lady of Victories, who healed me in childhood, but I think the miracle she will do will be only to console the Mother who loves me so tenderly." She writes that "at the moment of appearing before God, I understand more than ever that only one thing is necessary: to work only for him and to do nothing for oneself or for creatures." She ends: "I am not dying, I am entering into life, and all I cannot tell you here below I will make you understand from the heights of Heaven. Goodbye, little Brother, pray for your little sister, who says to you: see you soon, goodbye until Heaven!"
- Cards: F-OW-497
- Best details:
  - « je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie » (F-OW-497).
  - « je profite d'un moment où l'infirmière est absente pour vous écrire un dernier petit mot d'adieu » (F-OW-497).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 244). High. The 1911 edition prints part of this text as "Lettre Xe" dated 14 Aug 1897.
- Conflicts: She writes as though she would die in days; she lived nearly four more months. The letter mentions the infirmarian on 9 June, a month before her move to the infirmary on 8 July. In Ms A (1911) her father asked in 1883 for a novena of Masses at Notre-Dame des Victoires; here it is a novena in June 1897.
- New to English readers: partial ("un dernier petit mot d'adieu" absent from 1911; "je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie" is in 1911, redated)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-072 — June 1897, Ms C 2v-3r — The little way: "an elevator"
- What happened: "Compared to the saints, there is between them and me the same difference as between a mountain whose summit is lost in the sky and the obscure grain of sand trodden underfoot." Instead of being discouraged, she decided God would not inspire impossible desires; "to grow bigger is impossible; I must bear with myself as I am". She wanted "a little way, very straight, very short, a completely new little way. We are in a century of inventions... in the houses of the rich an elevator replaces the stairs." "The elevator that must lift me to Heaven is your arms, O Jesus!... I must stay little, and become so more and more."
- Cards: F-OW-235
- Best details:
  - « l'ascenseur qui doit m'élever jusqu'au Ciel, ce sont vos bras, ô Jésus ! » (F-OW-235).
- Evidence: Her own writing (Ms C 2v-3r); reflecting on a discovery of c. 1894-1895. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: no (the elevator image is in the baseline)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-073 — 11-25 June 1897 — "Writing my little life is like fishing with a line"
- What happened: "To write my 'little' life I don't rack my brains; it's like fishing with a line: I write what comes to the end." On 15 June she threw flowers to the statue of St Joseph "to please him. I don't want to give in order to receive." "I'm happy, I don't offend God at all during my illness. Just now I was writing on charity and I was often disturbed; so I tried not to lose patience, to practise what I was writing." (25 Jun) During a sermon: "I played truant... I consider my notebook as my little homework."
- Cards: F-OW-244
- Best details:
  - « c'est comme si je pêchais à la ligne ; j'écris ce qui vient au bout » (F-OW-244).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 11.6.1-2; 15.6.5; 25.6.2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-074 — 12-30 June 1897 — "No one believes I'm ill"
- What happened: "My soul is exiled, Heaven is closed to me, and on the earth's side it's trial too... I can see people don't believe I'm ill, but God allows that." (12 Jun) "People don't think I'm as ill as I am. It's harder then to be deprived of Communion, of the Office." (30 Jun)
- Cards: F-OW-246
- Best details:
  - "Heaven is closed to me" (F-OW-246).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 12.6.1; 30.6). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-075 — June 1897, garden of the Carmel — Writing Manuscript C in the garden, interrupted at every moment
- What happened: At the end of her life, very ill, she wrote her manuscript in the garden. Pauline saw Sisters disturb her every moment. Each time she put down her pen and closed her notebook with a gentle smile. Asked how she could put two ideas together, she said she was writing on fraternal charity, so this was the time to practise it. She said sadly she had not written what she wanted; she needed more solitude, but "the thought is there, you will only have to arrange it". Pauline says that when she wrote the first part she had no idea of publication. In June 1897 she knew Pauline planned to make the text known after her death, which is why she said Pauline could add or cut. Pauline adds that Thérèse wrote at one go, without crossings-out, but infirmarians and novices kept coming and going to profit from her last days. She said she did not know what she was writing. She stopped writing early in July 1897. The last page is in pencil because she was too weak. Ms C 17r-17v gives the garden scene in her own words. Writing in the garden in June 1897, she is constantly interrupted: "a good Sister passes by with a pitchfork on her shoulder" and chats about "hay, ducks, hens, the doctor's visit"; another haymaker drops flowers on her knees "perhaps thinking to inspire poetic ideas". Tired of opening and closing "this famous notebook", she opens a book (which will not stay open) and says she is copying Psalms for the prioress's feast. A haymaker: "My poor little Sister, it must tire you to write like that all day." "Don't worry, I seem to write a lot but really I write almost nothing." "Good!" said she, "but all the same, I'm glad we're haymaking, it always distracts you a little." The infirmarians "do not fear to take two thousand steps where twenty would do".
- Cards: F-EW-1405, F-EW-1407, F-EW-1424, F-OW-238
- Best details:
  - « vous n'aurez plus qu'à classer » (F-EW-1405).
  - She was writing on fraternal charity, so she practised it when interrupted (F-EW-1405).
  - « Je ne sais pas ce que j'écris. » (F-EW-1424).
  - The last page is in pencil (F-EW-1424).
  - NV, 15 June: she tried not to become impatient when disturbed while writing on charity (f. 253v-254r).
  - « Ma pauvr'ptite soeur, ça doit vous fatiguer d'écrire comme ça toute la journée. » (F-OW-238), in the lay Sisters' country speech.
  - "the infirmarians do not fear to take two thousand steps where twenty would do" (F-OW-238).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard, 1915). High; medium-high for the publication claim. | Mère Agnès (saw and heard, 1910). High. | Her own writing (Ms C 17r-17v). High. A strong comic scene near her death.
- Conflicts: Relevant to Pauline's later editing of the text (F-EW-1209, an earlier card). Pauline says publication awareness did not change the spontaneity (F-EW-1407). Compare Marie's claim that Thérèse did not think of publication (F-EW-1276).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: charity practised while writing about charity.

### E-P6-076 — June (?) 1897; the 1911 edition says after 6 Sept — Her own "farewell card": "Do not weep for me, for I am in Heaven"
- What happened: In June (?) 1897 Thérèse made a farewell keepsake for Mère Agnès, Marie and Geneviève. The front top: "Do not weep for me, for I am in Heaven with the Lamb and the Sacred Virgins!" The bottom: "I see what I believed. I possess what I hoped. I am united to him whom I loved with all my power to love." The sides quote St John of the Cross: "The smallest movement of pure Love is more useful to the Church than all other works together!" On the back she copied passages from the last letters of Théophane Vénard in the first person: "I find nothing on earth that makes me happy; my heart is too big..."; "I am a spring flower that the master of the garden picks for his pleasure... I, a little ephemeral one, go first!" She signs "Thérèse of the Child Jesus borrowing the thoughts of the angelic Martyr Théophane Vénard."
- Cards: F-OW-499
- Best details:
  - « Ne pleurez pas sur moi, car je suis au Ciel avec l'Agneau et les Vierges Sacrées !... » (F-OW-499).
  - « Je vois ce que j'ai cru / Je possède ce que j'ai espéré / Je suis unie à Celui que j'ai aimé / de toute ma puissance d'aimer » (F-OW-499).
- Evidence: Her own writing with copied words (LT 245). High for the text; medium for the date.
- Conflicts: The Carmel editors date the card to June (?) 1897; the 1911 edition says she gave it as a "souvenir d'adieu" after 6 Sept 1897, when the Carmel received a relic of Vénard. In 1911 she tells her sisters that "Théophane Vénard is a little saint... I do not understand saints who do not love their family... These are my thoughts; my soul is like his." "Ne pleurez pas sur moi" and "Je vois ce que j'ai cru" are absent from the 1911 edition.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-077 — 19 June 1897 — "You are the Superior General of my heart"
- What happened: On 19 June Mère Agnès wrote: their cousin Mère Marguerite (Superior General in Paris of the Auxiliatrices Sisters, nurses) had sent Thérèse a pretty basket of artificial lilies for 21 June, the feast of Mère Marie de Gonzague. Mère Agnès brought it, saying joyfully: "It is the Superior General of the Auxiliatrices who sends you this!" Thérèse answered "suddenly with élan and affection": "Well, it is you who are the Superior General of my heart."
- Cards: F-OW-549
- Best details:
  - « Eh ! bien, c'est vous qui êtes la Supérieure Générale de mon coeur. » (F-OW-549).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ Paroles retrouvées, 19 juin). High. The cousin "Mère Marguerite" is named only by first name; do not add a surname without another source.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-078 — June 1897, Ms C 25r-25v — "The rosary costs me more than an instrument of penance"
- What happened: Thérèse wrote: "Alone (I am ashamed to admit it) saying the rosary costs me more than putting on an instrument of penance... I feel I say it so badly! However hard I try to meditate on the mysteries of the rosary, I cannot fix my mind." She cannot bear searching books for beautiful prayers: "it gives me a headache, there are so many!" "I do as children do who cannot read: I simply tell God what I want to tell him." Prayer is "a surge of the heart, a simple look turned towards Heaven". When dry she says one Our Father "very slowly".
- Cards: F-OW-198
- Best details:
  - « la récitation du chapelet me coûte plus que de mettre un instrument de pénitence » (F-OW-198).
- Evidence: Her own writing (Ms C). High.
- Conflicts: The rosary confession is not in the 1911 edited text (F-OW-198 notes; see gaps).
- New to English readers: yes (if absent from Taylor)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-079 — June (?) 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 241 to Sr Marthe: "let us be so small that everyone can trample on us"
- What happened: In a note to Sr Marthe de Jésus (June (?) 1897): "do not be afraid to tell Jesus you love him, even without feeling it; it is the way to force Jesus to help you, to carry you like a child too weak to walk." "It is a great trial to see everything in black, but it does not depend entirely on you." "In Heaven you will no longer see everything in black but all in white, all clothed in the divine whiteness of our Spouse, the Lily of the valleys." "Above all let us be little, so little that everyone can trample on us without our seeming to feel or suffer from it."
- Cards: F-OW-500
- Best details:
  - « soyons petites, si petites que tout le monde puisse nous fouler aux pieds » (F-OW-500).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 241). High. The 1911 edition prints most of this note in a story about a Sister in darkness.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-080 — 21 June 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 247 to Bellière: Pentecost, "permission to become an apostle in Carmel", "love drove out all fear"
- What happened: Thérèse tells Bellière she thanked Jesus with him for "the great grace he gave you on Pentecost day" (6 June 1897, the letter implies). It was also on that feast, "10 years ago", that she got "not from my director, but from my father" permission to become an apostle in Carmel. After reading "your first letter of 15 Oct 95" she thought the same as his director: "You cannot be a half saint; you must be a saint fully or not at all." She loves St Augustine and Mary Magdalene, "their repentance and above all their loving audacity". She thanks him for his dates ("I have already celebrated your 23 years"). On her way of love: since she understood the Heart of Jesus, "it has chased all fear from my heart. The memory of my faults humbles me... but even more it speaks to me of mercy and love." "When one throws one's faults with filial confidence into the devouring furnace of Love, how would they not be consumed without return?" "I did not choose an austere life to atone for my faults, but those of others." "Now we are two, the work will go faster (and with my way I will do more work than you), and I hope one day Jesus will make you walk the same way as I do."
- Cards: F-OW-501, F-OW-502
- Best details:
  - « c'est aussi le jour de cette belle fête (il y a 10 ans) que j'ai obtenu, non de mon directeur, mais de mon père la permission de me faire apôtre au Carmel » (F-OW-501).
  - « je n'ai pas choisi une vie austère pour expier mes fautes, mais celles des autres » (F-OW-502).
  - « Vous ne pourrez être un saint à demi, il vous faudra l'être tout à fait ou pas du tout » (F-OW-501).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 247). High. The 1911 edition prints the "brasier" passage; "je n'ai pas choisi une vie austère pour expier mes fautes mais celles des autres", "now we are two" and the "apostle in Carmel" framing are absent from 1911.
- Conflicts: The Carmel text says only "10 years ago" for the earlier Pentecost. Bellière wrote his first letter to the Carmel on 15 Oct 1895; she wrote to him a year later (21 Oct 1896, LT 198).
- New to English readers: yes (framing); partial (fear passage)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-081 — 22 June 1897 (cross-reference), garden — Pushed home in her little carriage
- What happened: Near the end, Pauline wheeled her in her little carriage from the garden to the infirmary. On the way Thérèse quoted Christ's words to St Teresa that everything not related to Him is a lie.
- Cards: F-EW-1422
- Best details:
  - "everything not related to Him is a lie" (F-EW-1422).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (present, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: The infirmary move was 8 July; in June she was still in her cell (card note).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-082 — 23 June 1897 and summer 1897 — The doctor's changing verdicts and de Cornière's judgment
- What happened: Thérèse told TSA that the doctor said one day she was lost and the next that she was better. She said the back-and-forth was tiring but did not touch her peace, because she abandoned herself. Dr de Cornière said it was dreadful how she suffered, and they should not wish to keep her in that state. He was amazed at her patience and her smile. Marie's card says he said she suffered a true martyrdom and there was nothing to do. TSA's notes give another version: today the doctor says I am lost, tomorrow better; he even hints I may still be here in April. TSA said she knew better: she was lost and would not see April. LT 255 (16 Jul 1897) gives her own view of the doctor's shifts: "One day I think I am at the door of Heaven because of the dismayed look of Mr de Cornière, and the next day he goes off joyful, saying 'You are on the way to recovery'." She judged she would not be cured but could drag on a long time.
- Cards: F-EW-1311, F-EW-1226, F-EW-1300, F-EW-1614, F-OW-506
- Best details:
  - « C'est affreux ce qu'elle souffre, ne désirez pas la conserver dans cet état. » (F-EW-1226).
  - He came very often; "a real martyrdom; nothing to be done" (F-EW-1300, MT, probably hearsay).
  - The doctor's verdict changed daily; her peace did not (F-EW-1311, date 23 June from DE).
  - "maybe still here in April" (F-EW-1614).
  - "the dismayed look of Mr de Cornière" (F-OW-506).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1910); TSA (heard from Thérèse); MT (probably hearsay, medium). | TSA (Souvenirs §17; notes). High. | Her own writing (LT 255). High.
- Conflicts: The file spells the doctor "de Cornières" and "de Cornière" (F-EW-1226). | Dated Aug-Sept 1897 here, against 23 June in F-EW-1311.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-083 — 29-30 June 1897 — "How shy I was with my uncle! What contrasts in my character!"
- What happened: After a parlour with her uncle, where as usual she said almost nothing: "How shy I was with my uncle! Coming back I scolded a novice a lot, I didn't recognise myself. What contrasts there are in my character! My shyness comes from an extreme embarrassment I feel when people pay attention to me." She preferred saints "who fear nothing, like St Cecilia" to St Simeon Stylites.
- Cards: F-OW-246
- Best details:
  - « Quels contrastes il y a dans mon caractère ! » (F-OW-246).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 29.6.3; 30.6.1-2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-084 — 1897, infirmary — "What bad luck if I were one of the two in a hundred!"
- What happened: The doctor said that of a hundred people as ill as she, not more than two survived. Thérèse said pleasantly to Céline how unfortunate it would be if she were one of those two. To a Sister who asked if she feared death, she said it frightened her when shown on pictures as a spectre, but that is not death; the catechism says it is the separation of soul and body. Marie's bulletin of 9 July 1897 gives the dated version. That morning Thérèse said "If I were to be one of the two!... one of the two in a hundred! Would that not be unfortunate!" because Mother Agnès had told her that Dr de Cornière said only two of a hundred in her state would survive. She feared to be one of those who can be saved. Marie reported: "Since she believes she is dying she is gay as a chaffinch. There are moments when one would pay for a seat beside her." Marie's 1915 card (May 1897, DE) adds: asked in her last illness if death frightened her, she said yes, when shown on pictures as a spectre; but that is not death. She recalled her catechism: death is the separation of soul and body, and she did not fear a separation that would unite her to God.
- Cards: F-EW-1249, F-EW-1279, F-EW-2621, F-EW-1393
- Best details:
  - « Si j'allais être une de ces deux sur cent, que ce serait malheureux! » (F-EW-1249).
  - Death on holy pictures as a skeleton is not what death is (F-EW-1249).
  - "gay as a chaffinch" (F-EW-2621).
  - "one would pay for a seat beside her" (F-EW-2621).
  - Death as "the separation of soul and body", not a spectre (F-EW-1393).
- Evidence: Céline (heard; Process 1910 and 1915). High. Editors mark it "first source". | Marie Guérin, letter of 9 July (via Piat). Medium-high. | Céline (1915). High.
- Conflicts: 1915 card names no doctor; note identifies de Cornière. | The doctor's statistic is "two survive out of a hundred" here; Céline gives "not more than two survive" (F-EW-1249). | Dated May 1897 (DE) here, against July 1897 in the other cards.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: humour in the face of death.

### E-P6-085 — summer 1897 — The obituary remark in the kitchen: "never done anything worth speaking about"
- What happened: Resting in her cell next to the kitchen during recreation, Thérèse overheard a Sister say that the prioress would be puzzled what to write in her obituary, since, "amiable as she is", she had "certainly never done anything worth speaking about". The infirmarian heard it too. Thérèse said she had long been indifferent to the opinion of creatures. The De Teil Articles admit that several Carmelites were mistaken about her, and that one asked, after her death, what there was to say. Laveille's version, with a dated location. He says that while she lay in her cell after cautery during recreation, she heard a Sister in the kitchen say: "Sœur Thérèse… will soon die, and in truth, I ask myself what can our Mother say of her after her death? She will be embarrassed, for this little Sister, amiable though she is, has surely done nothing worth recounting." (The prioress writes a circular for each dead nun.) Thérèse said to the infirmarian, "Ah, happily the good God has always given me the grace of being absolutely indifferent to [the opinion of creatures]." Another lay Sister, temporary infirmarian, offered her food that would have made her sick; Thérèse refused gently. The Sister said afterwards, "I do not know why they talk so much of Sœur Thérèse; she does nothing remarkable; one cannot even say that she is exactly a good religious." Thérèse smiled: "To hear on my bed of death that I am not a good religious, what joy!"
- Cards: F-EW-1218, F-EW-1215, F-LF-230
- Best details:
  - "this little Sister, amiable as she is, has certainly never done anything worth speaking about" (F-EW-1218, Taylor's English).
  - The cell was next to the kitchen; the infirmarian heard too (F-EW-1218).
  - The Articles concede that "several of the Carmelites were mistaken about her" (F-EW-1215).
  - "for this little Sister, amiable though she is, has surely done nothing worth recounting" (F-LF-230).
  - The kitchen remark and the E-P6-234 remark told as two linked episodes (F-LF-230).
- Evidence: Prioress's Epilogue by Mère Agnès (D6); De Teil Articles (official argument, an admission against interest). High. | Laveille (pp. 344, 372-373; eyewitness source type). Medium: nuns unnamed; the "circular" note is Laveille's. Surprise: the community did not expect a story to tell.
- Conflicts: The Sister is unnamed; F-EW-1215 note says F-EW-1218 is the likely source of the admission. The infirmarian is named Sr Saint-Stanislas only by other sources. | Laveille joins the kitchen remark and the "not a good religious" remark in one narrative, where Pauline, TSA and others place them as separate remarks.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: she hears her own obituary judged, and does not mind.

### E-P6-086 — summer 1897, garden — Walking for a missionary
- What happened: Ordered by the infirmarian to walk a quarter of an hour a day in the garden, she walked even when exhausted. To a Sister who told her to rest, she said she offered each step for some tired missionary far away. Marie heard the same. Marie du Sacré-Cœur's notes say she took a quarter-hour walk in the garden every day to obey the infirmarian.
- Cards: F-EW-1216, F-EW-1270, F-EW-1348
- Best details:
  - « Je marche pour un missionnaire » (F-EW-1270).
  - A quarter of an hour a day, ordered by the infirmarian (F-EW-1216).
  - The web page of these notes stops here; the notes seem incomplete (F-EW-1348).
- Evidence: De Teil Articles and Prioress's Epilogue; Marie (heard, 1915). High. | Marie du Sacré-Cœur (notes). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a small exhausted figure walking for someone she will never meet.

### E-P6-087 — summer 1897, garden hermitage / oratory of the Sacred Heart — Made to stand and sing though feverish
- What happened: During her illness the community went to sing a hymn at the Sacred Heart hermitage in the garden. She followed with difficulty and sat. A Sister signed to her to come and sing, or to stand. She rose at once and stood to the end, in spite of fever and breathlessness. Céline, as infirmarian, was displeased and asked who inspired such blind obedience. Thérèse said she had got into the habit of obeying each Sister as if God were showing His will. Céline's Conseils et Souvenirs give the same scene: at a community gathering in a hermitage to sing hymns, she sat down, worn out. A Sister signed to her to stand; she did at once with a pleasant face. Asked by Céline, she said she obeyed everyone in small things as if God were speaking.
- Cards: F-EW-1217, F-EW-1257, F-EW-1286, F-EW-1359
- Best details:
  - « J'ai pris l'habitude d'obéir à chacune comme si c'était le bon Dieu qui me manifestait sa volonté » (F-EW-1286).
  - Céline "very displeased, since I was the infirmarian" (F-EW-1257).
  - She stood to the end despite fever and breathlessness (F-EW-1217).
  - She stood "with a pleasant face" (F-EW-1359). It shows a Sister who did not spare her.
- Evidence: De Teil Articles; Céline (saw, 1910 and 1915). High. | Céline (saw and heard). High.
- Conflicts: Place: "oratory of the Sacred Heart" (F-EW-1257) versus "hermitage of the Sacred Heart in the garden" (F-EW-1217, F-EW-1286). Wording of the Sister's gesture differs: "signed to stand" / "called to come and sing".
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: blind obedience to the last drop of strength.

### E-P6-088 — summer 1897, infirmary — Buried under blankets by the deaf old infirmarian
- What happened: Told to say by obedience what would relieve her, Thérèse, burning with fever, asked the first infirmarian to take off a blanket. The very old, somewhat deaf Sister understood she was cold and piled on every blanket she could find, up over her head. Céline came back to find her streaming with sweat. Thérèse said she accepted all out of obedience. Since she smiled at each one, the Sister kept bringing more. Céline names the first infirmarian as "a good old nun, a little deaf" (Sr Saint-Stanislas, per F-EW-1362). Thérèse accepted it all in obedience to her first infirmarian.
- Cards: F-EW-1250, F-EW-1287, F-EW-1360
- Best details:
  - « Cette sœur très âgée et un peu sourde comprit qu'elle avait froid » (F-EW-1250).
  - Covered "right over her head"; Céline found her "streaming with sweat" (F-EW-1287).
  - She told the story smiling, without a word of complaint (F-EW-1287).
  - "a good old nun, a little deaf" (F-EW-1360).
- Evidence: Céline (saw the result, heard Thérèse's account; 1910 and 1915). High. | Céline (saw the result, heard the story). High.
- Conflicts: none; the infirmarian is named Sr Saint-Stanislas only in the notes (probably).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: comic and moving (scene S-EW-040 in the card).

### E-P6-089 — 1897, infirmary — Soft linen for the body, the same care for souls
- What happened: She pointed out to Céline that the first infirmarian always chose very soft linen to ease her. One should take the same care of souls. A Sister with a migraine who does half her work does more than a healthy one who does all of it. All bodily penances, she said, were nothing next to charity. Céline names the first infirmarian: Sr Saint-Stanislas des Sacrés-Cœurs (died 23 May 1914 aged 89½).
- Cards: F-EW-1253, F-EW-1280, F-EW-1362
- Best details:
  - « Plusieurs âmes sont malades, beaucoup sont faibles, toutes sont souffrantes. » (F-EW-1253).
  - « Pourquoi ne les soulage-t-on pas avec la même délicatesse que les corps? » (F-EW-1280).
  - Sr Saint-Stanislas, aged 89½ at death (F-EW-1362).
- Evidence: Céline (heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Céline (heard). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-090 — 1897, infirmary — Nail parings, lip skin, eyelashes and rose petals given to be kept
- What happened: Céline believes Thérèse foresaw her glorification. "With charming simplicity" Thérèse gave Céline to keep the clippings of her nails, the flakes of skin from her lips, and even eyelashes fallen on her handkerchief. She also helped gather the rose petals she had used to caress her crucifix. Pauline, in her own account, wiped and kept Thérèse's tears on fine linen. In her last three months she told her three sisters that her relics would be sought and she would have a mission. She told them to keep carefully even her nail clippings. In 1915 Pauline says that when she cut her nails, Thérèse gathered the parings and gave them to Pauline herself, inviting her to keep them. That is dated September 1897. The Carnet jaune for 25 July says that Mère Agnès wiped her tears with a fine cloth: "Sr Geneviève keeps this relic." On 30 July the notebook records that Mère Agnès used to collect the little dry skins of Thérèse's lips.
- Cards: F-EW-1254, F-EW-1289, F-EW-1238, F-EW-1579, F-EW-1587, F-OW-539, F-OW-559
- Best details:
  - « elle me donnait à conserver les rognures de ses ongles » (F-EW-1254).
  - Nails, lip skin, eyelashes on the handkerchief, rose petals (F-EW-1289).
  - Pauline's cloth soaked with tears; Thérèse let her do it out of kindness, though Pauline later says Thérèse knew it was a revered souvenir (F-EW-1238).
  - Céline adds parallels: Benedict Joseph Labre and Felix of Cantalice (F-EW-1289).
  - Nail parings handed to Pauline by Thérèse herself (F-EW-1587).
  - "Sr Geneviève keeps this relic" (F-OW-539).
- Evidence: Céline (1910, 1915, first-hand); Mère Agnès 1915 about herself. High. | Mère Agnès (heard/saw; 1910, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 25.7, 30.7). High.
- Conflicts: CHANGE between 1910 ("recommended us to keep carefully even the parings of her nails") and 1915 (Thérèse gathers and hands them over). The nail clippings detail is not in the NV notebook (F-EW-1579 notes). | Who kept the cloth: Céline (Carnet jaune) versus Pauline (1915, F-EW-1238).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-092 — 1897 — St Barbara, St Stanislaus, and the priesthood in heaven
- What happened: The story that St Barbara brought communion to St Stanislaus Kostka delighted her: "Why not an angel, not a priest, but a virgin?" She thought those who desired the priesthood on earth would share its honour in heaven. She also imagined that the places at Christ's right and left, refused to great saints, would go to little children.

### E-P6-093 — summer 1897, infirmary — Missionaries in cannibals' stomachs; the day of death as the greatest feast
- What happened: When the purchase of a new cemetery was discussed in front of her, she said her place mattered little. Many missionaries are in cannibals' stomachs, and martyrs had wild beasts for a cemetery. Told she might die on some feast day, she said she needed no feast: her death day would be the greatest feast. Marie Guérin's letter of 31 July 1897 gives the same saying: "the day of my death will be the greatest feast day for me". Céline's Conseils et Souvenirs put the cemetery remark in September 1897, shortly before her death: many missionaries are in the stomachs of cannibals, and martyrs had wild beasts as cemetery. The card notes she was the first buried in that new plot (general knowledge, not stated in the card).
- Cards: F-EW-1261, F-EW-1503, F-EW-1624
- Best details:
  - « Il y a bien des missionnaires qui sont dans l'estomac des anthropophages » (F-EW-1261).

  - Date September 1897 (F-EW-1624).
- Evidence: Céline (heard, 1910). High. | Marie de l'Eucharistie (contemporary letter). High. | Céline (heard, Conseils et Souvenirs). High.

### E-P6-094 — 1897–1906 — An older Sister's private prayer to Thérèse
- What happened: Sr Saint-Jean-de-la-Croix (d. 3 Sept 1906), one of the older nuns who had sometimes asked Thérèse's advice, wrote a prayer to her and kept it in her office book. She asked to share Thérèse's love and that "the seraph who pierced her heart" do the same in her. After Thérèse's death Sr St Jean de la Croix composed a daily prayer to her, kept it in one of her office books, and signed it "Un tout petit rien" (a very little nothing). The prayer asks Thérèse to be "a Mother" to her.

### E-P6-096 — infirmary, 1897 — "A baby who is an old man"
- What happened: Near the end of her life Marie heard Thérèse call the Virgin "maman" and God "Papa le bon Dieu". When Marie asked, "So you are a baby?", Thérèse became grave and said yes, but a baby who thinks a great deal, a baby who is an old man. Céline adds that in her illness, speaking of God, Thérèse used one word for another and called Him "Papa". The Sisters laughed. She said, moved, that He really was her Papa and it was sweet to call Him so. Céline dates the slip of the tongue "Papa" to 5 June 1897 (DE), in the infirmary. Compare Marie's account (F-EW-1265).
- Cards: F-EW-1265, F-EW-1357, F-EW-1416
- Best details:

  - The slip of the tongue that the Sisters laughed at (F-EW-1357).
  - Date 5 June 1897 (DE) (F-EW-1416).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, 1915). High. | Céline (saw). High. | Céline (present, 1915). High.

### E-P6-097 — summer 1897; fulfilment from 15 Oct 1897 — "I will make you change your mind": daily communion after her death
- What happened: Thérèse told Mère Marie de Gonzague, who opposed daily Communion, that from heaven she would make her change her mind. She also told the sisters they would not lack "our daily bread" after her death. TSA heard from Marie du Sacré-Cœur that Abbé Hodierne would one day be chaplain and give daily communion. Hodierne was named chaplain on 15 Oct 1897 and his first sermon, on "Come and eat my bread", invited daily communion. Céline says that in the 13 years after, only one day lacked it, for want of a priest, and that Gonzague suddenly changed her practice. Marie du Sacré-Cœur's notes repeat the saying to Gonzague, who feared daily communion. After Thérèse's death the chaplain who succeeded Abbé Youf gave daily communion, and Gonzague, instead of protesting as before, was very happy. Marie (1910) adds that not receiving communion daily was Thérèse's great suffering, and that Gonzague "was afraid of" it. Marie also saw the aftermath: after the death the chaplain gave the nuns communion every day.
- Cards: F-EW-1266, F-EW-1252, F-EW-1309, F-EW-1337, F-EW-1427
- Best details:
  - « Ma mère, quand je serai au ciel, je vous ferai changer d'avis » (F-EW-1266).
  - Hodierne's first sermon took the text "Come and eat my bread", unasked (F-EW-1309).
  - Marie credits Thérèse for the decrees of Pius X on frequent and early Communion, and thinks Thérèse foretold a change in Church practice, but she hedges (F-EW-1266).
  - « Ma mère, quand je serai au ciel, je vous ferai changer d'avis. » (F-EW-1337).
  - « Ma mère, quand je serai au ciel je vous ferai changer d'avis » (F-EW-1427).
- Evidence: Marie ("I remember perfectly" for the words to Gonzague); Céline; TSA (second-hand, medium). Confidence high for the saying, low-medium for the general prediction. | Marie du Sacré-Cœur (heard or was told). Medium. | Marie (heard and saw the aftermath). High.
- Conflicts: TSA writes that Abbé Youf's death came "several years after" the words; the card notes Youf in fact died in Oct 1897. Marie's memory of the general prediction is hedged. MdA confirms a prediction of daily communion without naming Hodierne (F-EW-1309 notes).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: a promise to the prioress that was kept.

### E-P6-099 — 1897, infirmary — Useless remedies offered for a sick missionary
- What happened: In her illness she let herself be given the most repugnant and repeated remedies, knowing they were useless. She told Céline and Marie she offered them for an abandoned sick missionary who lacked care or time to care for himself. Marie calls this "reversibility even of natural benefits". Late May 1897, Mère Agnès heard she was sure the remedies would not cure her, so she "arranged with God" that everything given to her should serve to heal poor missionaries who had neither time nor means to care for themselves. Céline's own notes repeat that she accepted the most repugnant remedies while noting they were useless. The Carnet jaune (21-26 May) adds: "I'm convinced remedies are useless to cure me; but I've made an arrangement with God so that poor sick missionaries, who have neither time nor means to look after themselves, benefit from them."
- Cards: F-EW-1251, F-EW-1268, F-EW-1354, F-EW-1388, F-OW-232
- Best details:
  - The missionary "with neither time nor means to care for himself" (F-EW-1268).
  - "arranged with God" that all she was given should heal poor missionaries (F-EW-1388).
  - "I've made an arrangement with God" (F-OW-232).
- Evidence: Céline and Marie (heard). High. | Mère Agnès (heard, 1915); Céline (heard). High. | Mère Agnès (Carnet jaune). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-100 — 1897 — Prayers for the dead and the "heroic act"
- What happened: Even in great pain she said six Our Fathers and Hail Marys every evening for the souls in purgatory. She said the devil tried to make her forget them but rarely succeeded. She begged to keep saying the Office of the Dead for deceased sisters until she was totally exhausted. Céline says she had made the "heroic act", giving the Virgin all her daily merits and all suffrages after her death for the souls in purgatory. Pauline knows of the "heroic vow" from Sr Geneviève. Céline adds daily prayers kept to the end: "O bon et très doux Jésus", the six Paters and Aves of the scapular of the Immaculate Conception, and a practice rich in indulgences. When too ill for vocal prayers they wanted to dispense her from this last one, but she begged: it was all she could still do for the souls in purgatory. She did the Way of the Cross several times a week as long as she could.
- Cards: F-EW-1237, F-EW-1251, F-EW-1394
- Best details:
  - Six Our Fathers and Hail Marys each evening (F-EW-1237).
  - The devil tried to make her forget them but rarely succeeded (F-EW-1237).
  - « Je ne puis plus faire que cela pour les âmes du purgatoire, et c'est si peu de chose! » (F-EW-1394).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès 1915 (high for prayers; medium for the vow, hearsay from Sr Geneviève, and she says so); Céline (heard). | Céline (1915). High.
- Conflicts: Pauline does not surely remember whether Thérèse told her of the vow herself (F-EW-1237).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-101 — Carmel refectory; infirmary, 1897 — Sticky spoon, foul remedy drop by drop, the painful dress
- What happened: At dinner, if the handle of her knife or spoon was badly wiped and sticky, she kept it so, though it cost her greatly. Céline saw her drink a foul remedy drop by drop and urged her to gulp it. Thérèse said she tasted it on purpose, since she was forbidden big mortifications. In winter she smothered the foot-warmer given on the doctor's orders. She wore a dress that hurt her badly and said it was as indifferent to her "as to a Chinese man". She ate all leftovers in the refectory. In her last illness she did not ask for iced water or grapes. She was glad to have no copies of her poems. Pauline adds the story of a new habit that fitted very badly because it was badly cut. People kept telling her she was badly dressed. Pauline asked if it annoyed her, and she laughed and said she cared no more than if the dress were worn by a Chinese woman two thousand leagues away.
- Cards: F-EW-1283, F-EW-1284, F-EW-1396
- Best details:
  - « je fais exprès de le bien goûter » (F-EW-1283).
  - The dress "as to a Chinese man" (F-EW-1284).
  - She would have liked copies of her poems to sing while working, but gave them away as soon as written (F-EW-1284).
  - « cela m'est aussi indifférent que si ma robe était portée par une chinoise » (F-EW-1396).
- Evidence: Céline (saw, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (heard; 1915). High. Absent in 1910.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-104 — 1897, infirmary — She refused night watchers

Pauline and Marie both say she was watched only on the last night of her life; before that she refused to have anyone stay with her, so as not to tire the infirmarian. Marie (1910) says she refused even the evening before her death, and begged to be left alone.

### E-P6-106 — summer 1897, Lisieux Carmel / infirmary — "If our Mother burned the notebooks": what she said about the manuscript
- What happened: Pauline told Thérèse that the manuscript could be used for publication, and also that the prioress might burn it. Thérèse answered it would not matter; God would use other means. On her deathbed she said with assurance the manuscript must be published without delay after her death. If Pauline waited or told anyone except Mother, the devil would set a thousand traps. As with Joan of Arc, God's will would be done despite men's jealousy. Marie says neither Thérèse nor her sisters thought the notes would be printed. Only in her last months did Agnès think of it, and Thérèse accepted simply. Laveille gives the 1 Aug 1897 exchange: Mère Agnès said she meant to have the manuscript read in community and printed. Thérèse said: "My Mother, after my death, my manuscript should not be spoken of to anyone until it is published… the demon will set many snares." She also asked Mère Agnès to revise an incomplete passage, and was found in tears: "these pages will do a great deal of good… Ah, I know it, everyone will love me." Mère Agnès testified that Thérèse told her to print the manuscript with prudence, "whatever you do, I will have done it". Ms C itself (1r-2r) says the prioress may burn the notebook before her eyes without reading it. The Carnet jaune records on 1 Aug: "After my death you must speak to no one about my manuscript before it is published; you must speak of it only to Our Mother. If you do otherwise, the devil will set you more than one trap to spoil God's work... a very important work!" She also said: "I won't write any more now!" The Carnet jaune (27 July) adds: Mère Agnès mentioned difficulties she foresaw for the publication of Thérèse's life. Thérèse answered: "Well, I say like Joan of Arc: '...And God's will shall be accomplished in spite of men's jealousy.'"
- Cards: F-EW-1223, F-EW-1240, F-EW-1276, F-LF-231, F-LF-241, F-OW-234, F-OW-266, F-OW-544
- Best details:
  - « Oh! mais qu'est-ce que cela ferait; c'est que le bon Dieu ne voudrait pas se servir de ce moyen » (F-EW-1223), told twice by Pauline, consistent.
  - "Si notre mère prieure brûlait tous ces cahiers, cela ne me troublerait aucunement" (F-EW-1276).
  - Pauline: it was never the custom at Lisieux for nuns to write autobiographical notes for their circulars (F-EW-1223).
  - Marie: the memoirs are "far below reality", not idealised; the poems were for feasts and on request; letters were not for publication (F-EW-1276).
  - "the demon will set many snares" (F-LF-231).
  - "Ah, I know it, everyone will love me" (F-LF-231).
  - "whatever you do, I will have done it" (F-LF-241).
  - « une oeuvre bien importante ! » (F-OW-266).
  - « Eh bien, je dis comme Jeanne d'Arc : «...Et la volonté de Dieu s'accomplira malgré la jalousie des hommes.» » (F-OW-544).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1910 and 1915); Marie (1915). Medium-high; no other witness for the deathbed instruction. | Laveille (Mère Agnès as source). High for the sequence; the words may be edited. | Her own writing; Mère Agnès (CJ 1.8.2-3). Medium (a "strategic" saying in Langlois's sense). | Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7.6). High. Novissima Verba has a similar saying. It shows Agnès already worried in July 1897 about publishing the life.
- Conflicts: Pauline's account puts the idea of publishing on herself; Marie says only in the last months. Pauline asked whether it would be burned; the fire question points to fear of Gonzague's reaction (F-EW-1240 notes). | Compare F-EW-1240 (same theme, source: Pauline's own notes). | Langlois warns that sayings giving Mère Agnès authority over the writings are among those most open to later reworking. | The words are given as Joan's. Compare F-EW-1240 (same "Joan of Arc" theme, on the deathbed).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: "if it is burned, no matter" shows her calm about her own legacy.

### E-P6-109 — summer 1897, infirmary — MT complains aloud and is sent away; LT 249
- What happened: Unable to bear being kept away, MT went to the infirmary and complained aloud in front of one of Thérèse's sisters. This pained Thérèse, who sent her away with a severe rebuke. That evening Thérèse sent a note (LT 249): she spoke severely because of the perfection she dreamed for MT; she would have comforted her gently had she kept her pain in her heart. MT's own circular adds: in Thérèse's last months the novices could seldom see her, to spare her fatigue "and perhaps also because of the danger of contagion". Once the novice poured out her grief at the infirmary before another Sister; Thérèse scolded her and sent her away, then that evening sent a note: she had spoken severely because of the perfection she wanted for her; their affection must now be hidden.
