### E-P6-113 — 1897, infirmary — "Not for the reward, but to please God"
- What happened: TSA told Thérèse God would reward her greatly for her suffering. Thérèse answered: not for the reward, but to please God. She also told TSA not to worry: God would not give her more than she could bear. PA adds that she accepted even the terrors that come with death, but said they would not purify her: "it is the fire of love I need." TSA says that when some Sisters believed she would have the terrors of death, Thérèse said they had not come yet. If they came she would bear them, but they would not be enough to purify her, "only bleach": she needed the fire of love.
- Cards: F-EW-1310, F-EW-1312, F-EW-1346
- Best details:
  - "it is the fire of love I need" (F-EW-1312 notes).
  - « ce ne sera que de l'eau de javel. C'est le feu de l'amour qu'il me faut. » (F-EW-1346).
- Evidence: TSA (heard, 1911 and 1915). High. | TSA (Souvenirs §18; notes, ESPERANCE). High. Shows that some nuns expected a hard, fearful death for her.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-114 — summer 1897, infirmary — "Don't throw them away, they may make people happy": the fallen petals
- What happened: During her illness roses were brought for her to cover her crucifix. Petals fell and a Sister gathered them to throw away. Thérèse, lowering her voice mysteriously, said: don't throw them away, they may make people happy. TSA says she urged the Sisters to collect them carefully. TSA also reports, from other Sisters, that Thérèse said nothing extraordinary would happen at her death or burial, because all "little souls" must be able to imitate her. Petal wordings differ. In 1911 MdA has Thérèse say, lowering her voice: do not throw them, they will be precious one day. In 1915, "with a mysterious air", they could make people happy. Pauline's version (NV 14 Sept): do not lose them, you will give pleasure with them later. MdA says she heard it from Mère Agnès and Marie du Sacré-Cœur, who were present. Dated 14 Sept 1897. In her last days she told her three sisters to keep carefully the rose petals she plucked over her crucifix, because later they would be useful (14 Sept 1897, Marie 1910). Laveille and De Teil give the petals scene on 14 Sept: she touched the wounds of her crucifix with each petal of a rose and told her sisters to gather the fallen petals: "later on, they will enable you to give pleasure." The Articles note the petals were later used as relics (D3 art. 122). The Carnet jaune (14 Sep): given a rose, she pulled off the petals over her crucifix, touching Christ's wounds with each petal: "In September little Thérèse still strips 'the spring rose'." As the petals slid to the floor: "Gather these petals carefully, my little sisters; they will help you give pleasure later... don't lose any." The Carnet jaune links to PN 51 "Une rose effeuillée".
- Cards: F-EW-1350, F-EW-1318, F-EW-1611, F-EW-1587, F-EW-1579, F-EW-1635, F-EW-2050, F-LF-236, F-OW-317
- Best details:
  - « oh, ne les jetez pas... elles pourront faire des heureux... » (F-EW-1350).
  - TSA says she did not hear the "nothing extraordinary" words herself (F-EW-1318).
  - « elles seront précieuses un jour » (1911) / « elles pourront faire des heureux » (1915) (F-EW-1611).
  - Léonie later used such a petal in a cure (F-EW-1611 notes, F-M13).
  - Petals kept "because later they would be useful" (F-EW-1635).
  - She touched each petal to the wounds of the Crucified (F-EW-2050, F-LF-236).
  - « Ramassez bien ces pétales, mes petite soeurs, ils vous serviront à faire des plaisirs plus tard... n'en perdez aucun... » (F-OW-317).
- Evidence: Marie des Anges and TSA (notes); TSA (1915, hearsay for the "nothing extraordinary" words). High for the petals; medium for the rest. | MdA (heard from those present); Mère Agnès (saw). Medium: the wording shifts. | Marie (heard, 1910). High. | Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 925); De Teil (D3 art. 122). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 14.9.1, 14.9.3). Medium-high. Petals later used as relics (check other parts).
- Conflicts: See MdA's two wordings on the petals (F-EW-1611, later chunk). | MdA's two wordings differ (1911 versus 1915). | A fourth wording of the petal saying. | A fifth wording of the petal saying. | A sixth wording of the petal saying.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-115 — 1897, infirmary — Dr La Néele called twice to her bedside
- What happened: Dr La Néele was called twice to Thérèse in the absence of Dr de Cornière, the community doctor. He told Jeanne he was struck by her holiness and angelic gentleness in pain. Jeanne says she suffered more than usual in pulmonary tuberculosis. Pauline charges that during Dr de Cornière's five weeks of holiday (August 1897) the prioress let Dr La Néele in only three times, although he declared she needed a doctor every day.
- Cards: F-EW-1326, F-EW-1514
- Best details:
  - "holiness and angelic gentleness in pain" (F-EW-1326).
  - « la mère prieure ne fit entrer que trois fois le docteur La Néele » (F-EW-1514).
- Evidence: Jeanne, heard from her husband (PA-23). High. The editors say he treated her "on a few rare occasions". | Mère Agnès (1915). Medium-high: Pauline's charge; La Néele was the Martins' cousin by marriage.
- Conflicts: Absent in 1910 (no mention of La Néele at all). Jeanne says he was called twice in de Cornière's absence (F-EW-1326). A major new criticism of Gonzague's care.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-116 — undated; 1897 — "How E-P6-116 I am"
- What happened: Thérèse said to TSA: if you knew how E-P6-116 I am; when I start a thing I carry it through and nothing stops me. In her illness, when saints did not answer, she kept praying, saying they wanted to see how far she would push her trust. On 7 July 1897 Céline was surprised she got no relief from heaven. Thérèse said that often when she prayed for help she was most abandoned, but she did not lose heart. She thanked God and the saints anyway: they want to see how far she would push her hope. She quoted Job 13:15.
- Cards: F-EW-1343, F-EW-1445
- Best details:
  - Saints who did not answer wanted to see how far she would push her trust (F-EW-1343).
  - « je crois qu'ils veulent voir jusqu'où je pousserai mon espérance » (F-EW-1445).
- Evidence: TSA (notes, ESPERANCE). High. | Céline (heard, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-117 — 1897 — No refusal to God since age three
- What happened: Asked by Marie Guérin whether she ever refused God a sacrifice, Thérèse said never. As a small child even less: from age three she refused Him nothing, because she already did "practices".

### E-P6-119 — 1897, after Communion — "No doubt he was asleep!"
- What happened: After one of her Communions in her last illness she said it was like two little children together who say nothing. She said something to Jesus, He did not answer, "no doubt he was asleep!" Céline's 1910 account dates it to July 1897.

### E-P6-120 — 1897, infirmary — Lili and Toto: nicknames from a picture book
- What happened: A favourite children's tale (the album is now in the toy case at Les Buissonnets) had a girl Mlle Lili and her little brother M. Toto. Céline, the elder, was Lili; Thérèse was Toto. Even in Carmel, when tired and afraid she would not hear the wake-up clapper (matraque), Thérèse asked Céline to check "if M. Toto heard the matraque". Or she said: "Don't forget to wake M. Toto tomorrow, poor Mlle Lili, humiliated by all, but loved by Jesus and M. Toto."

### E-P6-121 — 1897 — Frictions were "a martyrdom", yet she asked for them
- What happened: By doctor's order Céline gave her E-P6-121 (rubbings). It was a martyrdom for her, as she later told Mère Agnès, but she asked Céline for them. When Céline wanted to skip them, she reminded her that Mother wanted them, "especially on the back", and the doctor would ask on Sunday. She called Céline "Pauvre, Pauvre", a nickname from a romance (song).

### E-P6-122 — 1897 — The almanac joke: "Che suis riche, drès riche"
- What happened: A novice showed her an almanac caricature of a wealthy Jew boasting he started with nothing. His friend replied that the man he did business with had something. Thérèse repeated it in the accent: she was like this man; she began with nothing, but the One she dealt with had something. She used "we must be rich, very rich both of us" in a note to Céline.
- Cards: F-EW-1370
- Best details:
  - « Che suis riche, drès riche, eh pien ! quand ch'ai commencé les affaires, che n'avais rien ! » (F-EW-1370).
- Evidence: Céline (heard). High.
- Conflicts: The joke is an ethnic caricature of its time; handle with care (card note).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low: shows mimicry and humour, but hard to use.

### E-P6-123 — summer 1897, infirmary — "Bo-bonne", the Plockhorst picture and "how shabbily done up you are"
- What happened: In her illness she called Céline "bo-bonne" (little maid), easier to say when exhausted; she asked permission first. She would not use "Céline", a name so dear to her that a calendar without St Céline (21 Oct) seemed charmless. She compared their course to two children in a Plockhorst picture: a boy in a simple tunic pulls his sister, who resists, loaded with a big bouquet, by a precipice under their guardian angel. She made up a story of a "demoiselle" with riches and her poor little brother who falls ill and asks her to burn her riches and become his "bo-bonne". Céline slept in a small room opening into the infirmary. One evening, seeing her undress, Thérèse took pity on their poor clothes and exclaimed: "Poor thing, how badly turned-out you are! But you won't always be like that." Céline: "We spoke together a sort of childish language the others could not grasp." Sr Saint-Stanislas, the first infirmarian, said admiringly: "How sweet those two little girls are with their unintelligible jargon!" Thérèse called Céline "bobonne" (nursie) with the prioress's permission, because it was easier to say than her name; later she stopped the nicknames as "not religious" (CJ 22.9.4). She told Céline stories and allegories (the rich "demoiselle" and her poor little brother; a dream of a lamb). Céline made a sling from folded linen hung from the bed canopy to hold her painful right arm: "God will make 'hangings' for bobonne too!" On 22 Sep she stopped the use of nicknames ("bobonne", "poupée"): "We mustn't call each other all sorts of names. All the same, it's not religious!"
- Cards: F-EW-1371, F-EW-1372, F-EW-1373, F-OW-300, F-OW-321
- Best details:
  - « Pauvre, Pauvre, comme vous êtes torée ! » (F-EW-1373).
  - The word "demoiselle" comes from "Franchise" by Mme Colomb, 1879 (F-EW-1372).
  - Her list of "too rich demoiselle" items: rosebuds, singing birds (from a Vénard life), a petticoat, kitchen pans, little parcels (F-EW-1372 notes).
  - « Qu'elles sont gentilles ces deux petites filles-là avec leur jargon inintelligible ! » (F-OW-300).
  - The sling of folded linen hung from the bed canopy (F-OW-300).
- Evidence: Céline (heard; editor's note in her text). High. | Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline); Mère Agnès (CJ 22.9.4). Medium.
- Conflicts: Thérèse ended the nicknames on 22 Sept as "not religious" (F-OW-321), after using them through August.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the sisters' private language in the infirmary.

### E-P6-124 — summer 1897, garden — The chestnut walk: "Even if I die of it, I want to console you"
- What happened: One afternoon the novice (Marie de la Trinité) saw Thérèse alone in her invalid carriage under the chestnut trees. She refused to approach without permission and went to cry in the Holy Face hermitage nearby. Looking up, she found Thérèse sitting beside her on a tree stump. Thérèse said she was not forbidden to go to her, wiped her tears, pressed her head to her heart, trembling with fever, and would not go back until the novice smiled. The novice helped her back to the carriage. The same story in the novice's own notes: crying in the Holy Face hermitage, she found Thérèse, feverish, had left her invalid carriage to sit by her on a tree trunk: "I'm not forbidden to come to you; even if I die of it, I want to comfort you" and "not before you've laughed for me!"
- Cards: F-EW-1377, F-OW-288
- Best details:
  - « Moi, je n'ai pas la défense de venir à vous ; dussé-je en mourir, je veux vous consoler. » (F-EW-1377).
  - She sat on a tree stump, trembling with fever, and would not leave until the novice smiled (F-EW-1377).
  - "not before you've laughed for me!" (F-OW-288).
- Evidence: Marie de la Trinité (eyewitness; circular of 20 Feb 1944). High. | Marie de la Trinité (recorder). Medium.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the dying novice-mistress comforts the novice who obeyed the rule.

### E-P6-125 — 1897 — "Fill my hands with little sacrifices"
- What happened: Thérèse told the novice that when she was in heaven, she should often fill her hands with little sacrifices and prayers so that Thérèse could throw them as a rain of graces on souls.
- Cards: F-EW-1379
- Best details:
  - « il faudra souvent remplir mes mains de petits sacrifices et de prières, pour me donner le plaisir de les jeter en pluie de grâces » (F-EW-1379).
- Evidence: Marie de la Trinité. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-126 — July 1897, infirmary — "I want to die at eighty"
- What happened: A few weeks before her death Thérèse told Marie that if God offered great glory for dying at once, or less glory but more pleasure to Him for dying at eighty, she would choose eighty. She would be glad to suffer the worst without God knowing, if only it made Him smile. Marie du Sacré-Cœur's notes give the same saying: a few weeks before death, if God said "die now and have great glory, or die at 80 with less glory but more pleasure for Me", she would choose 80, because she sought only His pleasure. Marie du Sacré-Cœur's record (16 Jul): she would choose to die at 80 with less glory if it gave God more pleasure: "I work for his sole pleasure, for his whims." The Carnet jaune (16 Jul, added later in the notebook): "If the good God said to me: if you die now you will have great glory; if you die at 80 the glory will be much less, but it will please me much more. Oh! then I would not hesitate to answer: 'My God, I want to die at 80, for I do not seek my glory but only your pleasure.'" "The great saints worked for the glory of the good God, but I, who am only a very small soul, I work for his single pleasure, and I would be glad to bear the greatest sufferings, even if only to make him smile once."
- Cards: F-EW-1426, F-EW-1431, F-EW-1617, F-OW-261, F-OW-524
- Best details:
  - « Mon Dieu, je veux mourir à quatre-vingts ans, car je ne cherche pas ma gloire mais votre plaisir » (F-EW-1426).
  - "she would not hesitate to choose 80" (F-EW-1617).
  - "his whims" (F-OW-261).
  - « je serais heureuse de supporter les plus grandes souffrances, quand ce ne serait que pour le faire sourire même une seule fois. » (F-OW-524).
- Evidence: Marie (heard; 1910, 1915). High. Also in Last Conversations. | Marie du Sacré-Cœur (notes). High. | Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Dernières paroles). Medium-high. | Mère Agnès (CJ 16.7.6, marked "Oublié par Mère Agnès et rajouté plus loin"). High. It shows how the notebook was assembled.
- Conflicts: 1910 says "quatre-vingts ans", 1915 "80 ans"; same words.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-127 — mid-June to mid-July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — Small notes: the blanket, "the branch that held the little bird", "I want to have her whole"
- What happened: LT 248 (to Léonie, late June (?) 1897): "I am more than touched by your eagerness to please me. I thank you with all my heart and am delighted with the little blanket you made me. It is just as I wanted it... I will take Communion for you tomorrow." LT 246 (13 Jun 1897, to Marie de la Trinité): a wish that her soul be "a dwelling perfumed with the roses of Love". LT 249 (mid-July (?) 1897, fragments to Marie de la Trinité): "I do not want you to be sad", "the devil goes away", and "Goodbye, poor little p., whom I must take quickly to Heaven! I want to have her whole". LT 250 (July (?) 1897, to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph): "That gives so much good to see that He alone is perfect, that He alone must suffice for us when it pleases Him to remove the branch that held up the little bird! The bird has wings, it is made to fly!" LT 251 (Jun-Jul (?) 1897, to Sr Marthe): "Do not be a sad little girl seeing that you are not understood, judged wrongly, forgotten; but catch everyone out by trying to do for yourself what others do for you, that is, forget all that is not Jesus, forget YOURSELF." LT 252 (13 Jul 1897, to Mère Agnès): "I love you very much, my little Mamma, you will see it soon!... oh yes!"
- Cards: F-OW-503
- Best details:
  - « lorsqu'Il Lui plaît d'ôter la branche qui soutenait le petit oiseau ! L'oiseau a des ailes, il est fait pour voler ! » (F-OW-503).
  - « suis ravie de la petite couverture que tu m'as faite. Elle est telle que je la désirais » (F-OW-503).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 246, 248-252). High for the words; the "infirmary" line in LT 249 is a fragment with gaps, so its sense is uncertain. All absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: The note from Léonie's blanket does not say where Léonie lived. LT 250: "the branch removed" for a bird is a saying about a Sister's loss (she was consoled by Céline, per the note).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-130 — 3 July 1897 — "What a pity I couldn't do the autopsy!"
- What happened: A friend of the Carmel had died of a tumour. Dr de Cornière said in front of Thérèse, "What a pity I couldn't do the autopsy!" Thérèse: "Ah! that's how people are indifferent to each other on earth! Would he say that about a mother or a sister?" When the doctor prescribed "lait maternisé" (condensed milk from the chemist) she cried hot tears for various reasons. "I need food for my soul; read me a saint's life... not to distract me, but for examples of humility." "Even the saints abandon me! I asked St Anthony at Matins to find our lost handkerchief. He didn't bother! But I told him I loved him all the same."
- Cards: F-OW-250

### E-P6-131 — 4 July 1897, infirmary — "Fr Pichon treated me too much like a child"
- What happened: On 4 July 1897 Thérèse said she was very grateful to Fr Alexis, who did her much good. She said Fr Pichon treated her too much like a child, but also did her good by telling her she had not committed mortal sin. The Carnet jaune adds for 4 Jul: "I'm very grateful to Fr Alexis; he did me much good. Fr Pichon treated me too much like a child; yet he did me good too by telling me I had not committed mortal sin." "Our Lord died on the Cross in anguish, and yet that's the most beautiful death of love... Dying of love is not dying in transports. I confess frankly, it seems to me that's what I'm experiencing."
- Cards: F-EW-1440, F-OW-251
- Best details:
  - « Le P. Pichon me traitait trop comme une enfant » (F-EW-1440).

- Evidence: Editors' note citing Derniers Entretiens. High. A cool note on the director who testified so warmly. | Mère Agnès (CJ 4.7.2, 4.7.4). Medium-high.

- What happened: Céline complained that her youthful zeal had died and she felt diminished. Thérèse said that was youth. True courage is to desire the cross in anguish of heart while almost pushing it away, like Our Lord in the garden. She also said never to blame lack of virtue on illness, weather or sorrow.

### E-P6-134 — 6 July 1897 (words), Lisieux Carmel — The little niece "Madeleine" and Thérèse's picture of meeting God
- What happened: Sr Marie-Élisabeth's niece, a little girl called "Madeleine", once recited a small compliment to the nuns at the parlour grille, ending with a curtsy and "Happiness for all those I love". Thérèse told this to her sisters on 6 July 1897: when she is in heaven she will go up to God like the little niece. He will say "What do you want, little girl?" and she will answer "Happiness for all those I love". She will do the same before all the saints.
- Cards: F-EW-2560

### E-P6-135 — 7 July 1897, infirmary — She refuses to "say something edifying" to Dr de Cornière
- What happened: Pauline asked her to say a few edifying and pleasant words to Dr de Cornière to edify him more. Thérèse refused: that was not her way. Let him think what he likes; she loves only simplicity and hates the opposite; to do it would be wrong. The Carnet jaune adds: "Ah, little Mother, that's not my little style... Let M. de Cornière think what he likes. I love only simplicity; I have a horror of 'pretence'." Of the "gentle remedies": "I suffer, but not enough to scream." Then, slyly: "Yet He sent us trials 'to scream'... and we didn't 'scream' all the same" (of their father's illness). "I'm like a poor 'little grey wolf' who wants to go back to his forest" (her father's nickname for her, "mon petit loup gris").
- Cards: F-EW-1443, F-EW-1444, F-OW-253
- Best details:
  - « Que monsieur de Cornière pense ce qu'il voudra! Je n'aime que la simplicité » (F-EW-1443).
  - Pauline tells a story against her own request (F-EW-1444).
  - « Je n'aime que la simplicité, j'ai horreur de la «feintise». » (F-OW-253).
  - "little grey wolf" (F-OW-253).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 7.7.4-7). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Place: infirmary (1910) versus "cell" (1915); the doctor is named only in 1910.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: Thérèse resisting Pauline's wish to stage her holiness.

### E-P6-136 — 7 July 1897 (event: a few days after 9 Jun 1895) — The wound of love after her Offering, told again
- What happened: Asked to tell again what happened after her Offering to Love ("I told you the same day, but you paid no attention"): "I was beginning my Way of the Cross, and suddenly I was seized by such a violent love for God that I can only explain it by saying it was as if I'd been plunged entirely into fire. Oh, what fire and what sweetness at the same time! I was burning with love and felt that one minute, one second more, I could not have borne this ardour without dying... I experienced it only once and only for an instant, then fell back into my usual dryness." "Since 14 I had assaults of love too... but it was not a real flame."
- Cards: F-OW-252
- Best details:
  - « c'était comme si on m'avait plongée tout entière dans le feu » (F-OW-252).
  - Mère Agnès admits she "seemed to attach no importance" to it in 1895 (F-OW-252 notes).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 7.7.2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-137 — summer 1897 (infirmary from 8 July) — Céline sleeps next door; Agnès takes notes on loose sheets
- What happened: As second infirmarian Céline had charge of Thérèse, slept in the adjoining cell, and left only for Office and care of other patients. Then Mère Agnès replaced her and wrote Thérèse's words on loose sheets as she spoke them. Céline says that for the illness and death she knows no details beyond those Mère Agnès wrote down day by day, word for word, plus the daily health bulletins Marie Guérin sent her father. She certifies that Agnès's notes are so true and complete that nothing need be removed or added. Pauline admits that writing Thérèse's words in a notebook as they were said seemed to tire her and "paralysed her outpourings", but Thérèse let her do it simply, fearing to hurt her. On 18 Aug Thérèse worried her merit would be lost "since I told you and you're writing it". Marie too took notes; on one occasion Thérèse said it was much better to lose a saying and make an act of regularity. Laveille adds that from 6 Apr 1897 Mère Agnès wrote what struck her in their talks. On 1 Aug 1897 she said she meant to have the manuscript read in community and printed.
- Cards: F-EW-1365, F-EW-1596, F-EW-1602, F-EW-1581, F-EW-1593, F-LF-231
- Best details:
  - Words noted on loose sheets, the origin of the Yellow Notebook (F-EW-1365).
  - « ce qui paraissait lui être une fatigue et paralysait ses épanchements » (F-EW-1581).
  - « il n'y a rien à y retrancher, comme aussi rien à y ajouter » (F-EW-1596).
  - « Il vaudrait mieux, à beaucoup près, perdre cela et faire un acte de régularité. » (F-EW-1593).
  - Agnès's plan to read the manuscript in community and print it, 1 Aug 1897 (F-LF-231).
- Evidence: Céline. High. Origin of "Last Conversations". | Céline (first-hand); Mère Agnès (self-admission); Marie (saw and heard). High. Important for judging "Last Words" as a source; the card notes a sign of a shared, pre-agreed record (see gaps). | Laveille (Summarium 1919 pp. 874-875). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-138 — 6-8 July 1897, cell then infirmary — New haemorrhages of 6 July and the move to the infirmary on 8 July
- What happened: Before the end of Lent 1897 she had fallen ill with daily fever, and she had several series of "points of fire". On 6 July new haemorrhages began. The doctor found a very serious lung congestion, forbade all movement, and prescribed ice, mustard poultices, cupping glasses and more. She spent a very bad night on her hard straw mattress with high fever, breathlessness, great weakness and heavy sweats. On 8 July she was carried down to the infirmary, where she stayed until death. Haemorrhages recurred two or three times a day through the month. Piat's bulletin adds: three or four days after Marie's veiling Thérèse said "Dying will be my happiness; living will be so too, because I want only what God wants" and "Either the Blessed Virgin will heal me or take me. It cannot last long." On 7 Jul Dr de Cornière diagnosed a right-lung congestion with tubercular flare-ups. Haemorrhages and fever rose, which made Thérèse say "in Purgatory they cannot burn more". On the evening of 8 Jul, during a lull, she was carried to the infirmary facing the statue of the Virgin of the Smile. Laveille adds that she told Mère Agnès on leaving her cell: "I have suffered greatly here; I would have liked to die here." He says a haemorrhage came on 4 Jul, and that Marie de Gonzague had let her sleep in the cell rather than the infirmary because her cough would disturb no one. The Carmel editors' July note: haemoptysis almost daily 6 Jul-5 Aug; 7 Jul suffocation, high fever; "gentle remedies": ice, pancreatin, mustard poultices, cupping; right lung "wound"; 8 Jul moved to the infirmary; stomach, head, heart pain, diarrhoea; lost unless a miracle; 30 Jul Extreme Unction and Viaticum from Canon Maupas. The Carnet jaune for 8 July: so ill that extreme unction was discussed, she was carried down to the infirmary: "I think I'm dreaming!... At last they're not mad" (Youf and de Cornière). The Carmel editors' introduction to the July notebook (their words): from 6 July the haemoptysis returns almost every day (until 5 August); on 7 July she is suffocating, very weak, with high fever; she is treated with "gentle remedies": ice to stop the bleeding, pancreatin to digest milk, mustard poultices, cupping. Dr de Cornière diagnoses a wound in the right lung and forbids her to move. On 8 July she is taken down to the infirmary. She loses weight daily, with stomach, head and heart pains and diarrhoea, and can no longer eat fruit. On 25 July she still gets up for two hours a day, but from 28 July the "great sufferings" begin (continual haemoptysis, suffocation, too oppressed to speak). On 30 July she receives extreme unction and communion as Viaticum from Canon Maupas.
- Cards: F-EW-1448, F-EW-1432, F-EW-1506, F-EW-2618, F-LF-233, F-OW-250, F-OW-254, F-OW-550
- Best details:
  - Ice, mustard poultices and cupping glasses on 6 July (F-EW-1448).
  - A bad night on the hard straw mattress (F-EW-1448).
  - "I have suffered greatly here; I would have liked to die here." (F-LF-233).
  - « Dying will be my happiness; living will be so too » (F-EW-2618).
  - « I think I'm dreaming!... At last they're not mad » (F-OW-254).
  - "gentle remedies": ice, pancreatin, mustard poultices, cupping (F-OW-550).
  - "great sufferings" from 28 July (F-OW-550).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; 1910, 1915); Céline (1915). High. | Piat (1967), quoting Marie's notes; Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 893 § 2585). Medium-high; high. | Carmel editors (cj-juillet-1897); Mère Agnès (CJ 8.7.1). Medium-high. | Carmel editors (scholarship; facts only). Medium: modern summary; the name and treatments are the editors', not in the entries.
- Conflicts: Marie dates the move to the infirmary to 8 July (F-EW-1432 notes). | Laveille gives a haemorrhage on 4 July; most cards give 6 July for the new haemorrhages. | The name "Canon Maupas" is only in the editors' summary; the notebook entries of 30 July say "Notre Père".
- New to English readers: yes (details)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-139 — 8 July 1897, infirmary — The eau-de-Cologne bottle: her examination of the senses before Extreme Unction
- What happened: Before Extreme Unction she went through the senses with Pauline, looking for sins to confess. For the sense of smell she remembered that she once used with pleasure a bottle of eau de Cologne given to her on a journey. The Carnet jaune names the journey and the donor: "on my last journey from Alençon to Lisieux I used a bottle of eau de Cologne Mme Tifenne had given me, and with pleasure."
- Cards: F-EW-1446, F-OW-254
- Best details:
  - « je me suis servie autrefois avec plaisir d'une bouteille d'eau de Cologne qu'on m'avait donnée en voyage » (F-EW-1446).
  - The donor was Mme Tifenne, an Alençon friend (F-OW-254).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 257r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 8.7.6). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The card notes it is probably the 1887 Rome trip; the text does not say. | F-EW-1446 notes suggest the 1887 Rome trip; the Carnet jaune says the journey from Alençon to Lisieux.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a saint confessing a bottle of cologne.

### E-P6-140 — 8 July 1897 (cross-reference) — Warning about "spiritual brotherhoods" with priests
- What happened: In her last illness she warned that many young priests would ask for spiritual sisters because she had been given to two missionaries. This could be a real danger. Any Sister could write what she wrote and get the same compliments. Correspondence should be rare and not allowed at all to some nuns who would be preoccupied and think they did wonders.
- Cards: F-EW-1447
- Best details:
  - "Any Sister could write what she wrote and get the same compliments" (F-EW-1447).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-141 — 8 July 1897, infirmary — "Today it is the statue; back then it was not"
- What happened: The day she went down to the infirmary to die, the statue of the Virgin that had cured her was placed there. Looking at it she told Marie it had never seemed so beautiful, "but today it is the statue, and back then, you know well, it was not the statue". In her last illness the statue of the Virgin of the Smile was placed facing her bed, and her eyes were always turned to it. Laveille repeats the scene: on a table by the bed stood the statue of the Virgin of the Smile. Marie asked, "What do you see?" She answered: "Never has it appeared so beautiful to me; but today it is the statue; before, as you well know, it was not the statue."
- Cards: F-EW-1449, F-EW-1453, F-EW-1625, F-LF-233
- Best details:
  - « aujourd'hui c'est la statue et autrefois vous savez bien que ce n'était pas la statue » (F-EW-1449).
  - Confirms she said she saw the Virgin herself in 1883 (F-EW-1453 notes).
  - Her eyes always turned to the statue (F-EW-1625).
  - Marie's question: "What do you see?" (F-LF-233).
- Evidence: Marie (heard; 1910 and 1915). High. | Céline (saw). High. | Laveille. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: the cured girl and her statue, on the day she comes to die.

### E-P6-142 — 8 July 1897, infirmary — The exhausting novice: "a good soldier is not afraid of battle"
- What happened: One day when Thérèse was suffering badly, a novice wore her out with a grievance against a companion. Thérèse argued in vain and finally had to reproach her. Afterwards Marie said such a novice should make her afraid to see her coming. Thérèse answered that a good soldier is not afraid of battle.
- Cards: F-EW-1450
- Best details:
  - « Un bon soldat n'a pas peur du combat; est-ce que je n'ai pas dit que je mourrai les armes à la main? » (F-EW-1450).
- Evidence: Marie (saw and heard, 1910). High. Shows she still directed novices from her sickbed and could scold.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-143 — 8 July 1897 (words); summer 1897 — Face unchanged while the body wasted: "It's turning into a skeleton, that's what pleases me"
- What happened: In the last illness her body became extremely thin, but "a strange thing, which much astonished the doctor", her face did not look altered by illness up to death. Looking at her fleshless hands she said she rejoiced to see herself destroyed: "it's becoming a skeleton".
- Cards: F-EW-1451
- Best details:
  - « Ça devient squelette, voilà ce qui me plaît » (F-EW-1451).
- Evidence: Marie (saw and heard, 1910). High.

- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-144 — 8 July 1897, Carmel infirmary — Marie Guérin's letter: thin but joking; "When one loves, there can be no purgatory"
- What happened: Marie de l'Eucharistie wrote that Thérèse was much changed and thin but still calm and "with a word to make you laugh". She had no fear of death. When the sisters spoke of going to purgatory, Thérèse said they hurt her and insulted God by thinking it. When one loves, there can be no purgatory.
- Cards: F-EW-1455, F-EW-1456
- Best details:
  - « bien amaigrie, mais toujours le même calme, et le mot pour rire » (F-EW-1455).
  - « quand on aime, il ne peut y avoir de purgatoire » (F-EW-1456).
- Evidence: Marie Guérin, contemporary letter (filed by Jeanne). High. The letters now appear in Derniers Entretiens, "Lettres de témoins".
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-145 — 8-10 July 1897, infirmary — "Too bad if they laugh at me afterwards": some Sisters doubted she was dying
- What happened: She said she very much wanted Extreme Unction, "too bad if they laugh at me afterwards". Pauline explains it was in case she recovered, since she knew certain Sisters did not think her in danger of death. On 10 July she herself wondered if the doctor was mistaken.
- Cards: F-EW-1462
- Best details:
  - « Tant pis, si on se moque de moi après! » (F-EW-1462).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; her own gloss; NV f. 257v, 258v). High. Community scepticism about her illness.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-146 — 7-8 July 1897 — Marie Guérin's dream of Thérèse playing with little angels
- What happened: In the same bulletin Marie wrote that the evening before she had asked God to let her dream of Thérèse, and that night she saw her "of dazzling beauty, surrounded by little angels and playing with them". She added: "Is not that the reality, for by her simplicity she resembles little children?"
- Cards: F-EW-2620
- Best details:
  - "playing with little angels" (F-EW-2620).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting Marie. Medium: a novice's dream, not an event.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-147 — 8 July 1897, infirmary — "A little Gennin box, not a bier"; "I'd rather be dust than preserved"
- What happened: Looking at her thin hands: "It's getting to be a skeleton already, that's what 'suits' me." "I'd rather be reduced to dust than preserved like St Catherine of Bologna." "I'd like to be put in a little Gennin box, not in the 'bière'" (pun: bière = coffin/beer; Gennin sent artificial flowers in long boxes). She warned that many young priests would ask for Carmelite "sisters" and that such correspondence could be "false money to buy souls". For smell she recalled: "on my last journey from Alençon to Lisieux I used a bottle of eau de Cologne Mme Tifenne had given me, and with pleasure."
- Cards: F-OW-254
- Best details:
  - « Je voudrais être mise dans une petite boîte à Gennin, pas dans la bière. » (F-OW-254).
  - "false money to buy souls" (F-OW-254).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 8.7.1-2, 8.7.6, 8.7.8, 8.7.16-17). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The eau-de-Cologne journey is "from Alençon to Lisieux" (Mme Tifenne) here, where F-EW-1446 notes suggest the 1887 Rome trip.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-148 — 9 July 1897, infirmary — "I don't know the trade": too cheerful to be anointed
- What happened: On 9 July 1897 the superior came to see if she should be anointed but did not find her ill enough, because she had been so pleasant and smiling. Pauline told her she did not know how to get what she wanted from the superior; she did not look ill at all. Thérèse answered nicely: "I don't know the trade!" (of using little tricks to get what one wants). Marie Guérin's bulletin of 9 July gives the full comic scene. Canon Maupas visited that morning and said: "You want to make us believe... you are not dying, and soon you will run in the garden; you do not have the face of a dying woman. Give you Extreme Unction? The sacrament would not be valid; you are not ill enough." After he left Thérèse said: "Another time I will not go to so much trouble to be polite: I sat on our bed, I was pleasant, and he refuses what I ask! Another time I will use 'pretence': I will drink a cup of milk before his arrival, because I always look worse after; then I will scarcely answer him, saying that I am in agony." Marie says "she was literally playing the comedy for us... Yes, I see that I do not know my trade; I do not know how to go about it."
- Cards: F-EW-1457, F-EW-2622
- Best details:
  - « Je ne connais pas le métier! » (F-EW-1457).
  - The plan to drink milk before his visit, "because I always look worse after" (F-EW-2622).
  - "she was literally playing the comedy for us" (F-EW-2622).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (present, 1915). High. | Marie Guérin's letter, via Piat. Medium-high. Compare Maupas's own depositions (PO 25, PA 15).
- Conflicts: Pauline's version (F-EW-1457) puts "I don't know the trade" with Pauline, not with Marie Guérin; Piat gives it after Maupas's visit, to the novices.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: her cheerfulness delays her own last sacraments.

### E-P6-149 — 9 July 1897, infirmary — "Not afraid of death, not yet... it's quite a passage"
- What happened: Marie asked if she was not at all afraid of death. She became serious and said not yet, but she could well be afraid like others, "for it is quite a passage". She abandoned herself to God.
- Cards: F-EW-1458, F-EW-1459
- Best details:
  - « Non, pas encore... mais je pourrais bien en avoir peur comme les autres, car c'est un fameux passage » (F-EW-1458).
- Evidence: Marie (heard; 1910 and 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-150 — 9 July 1897 (DE), infirmary — "No, I will come down"
- What happened: Céline asked: you'll look down on us from above, won't you? She replied quickly: no, I will come down. She also said she believed she would save more souls in heaven, "the proof is that God is making me die". Céline's Dernières paroles (Sept nn. 1-2) give the exchange as a September saying: "You'll look down on us from Heaven, won't you?" — "No, I'll come down!" The same passage records "I don't say anything to him, I love him!"
- Cards: F-EW-1460, F-OW-309
- Best details:

  - « Non, je descendrai ! » (F-OW-309).
- Evidence: Céline (involved, 1915). High. | Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline). Medium.
- Conflicts: Date: 9 July (Céline PA 1915) versus September (Dernières paroles).

- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-151 — 9 July 1897, infirmary (letter of 10 July) — "It is God who made my crown"
- What happened: The superior (Canon Maupas) told her she could not go to heaven soon because her crown was only begun. She answered it was true she had not made her crown, but God had made it. Céline's 1910 card includes the superior's remark that her crown was only begun, and her answer that God had made it, not she. Marie's message of 10 July adds: "I shall be still more with you than before; I will not leave you. It is I who will watch over my Uncle, my Aunt, my little Léonie, all. When they are ready to enter Heaven I will run to meet them." When Canon Maupas teased her ("Your crown is not made! You are only beginning it!"), she answered, as Marie recorded (calling them her "Novissima verba"): "Oh Father, it is true, I have not made my crown, but it is God who made it."
- Cards: F-EW-1461, F-EW-1518, F-EW-2623
- Best details:
  - « je n'ai pas fait ma couronne, mais c'est le bon dieu qui l'a faite » (F-EW-1461).

  - "I will run to meet them" when they are ready to enter Heaven (F-EW-2623).
  - Marie's phrase "Novissima verba" shows the novices were already writing the sayings down (F-EW-2623 notes).
- Evidence: Marie de l'Eucharistie, letter of 10 July 1897, written next day. High. | Céline (heard, 1910). High. | Piat (1967), quoting Marie. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-152 — 9 July 1897, Carmel infirmary — "A little girl of contradiction"
- What happened: To Marie's promise to write to reassure the family, Thérèse said: "Tell them I love them and that I am a little girl of contradiction: they think me dying and I do not yet turn my eyes; they think me alive and I am nearly dead; I am pure contradiction; but above all tell them I love them very very much."

### E-P6-153 — 9 July 1897, infirmary — "Eat dates as much as you like"; the wounded mouse
- What happened: Told she would perhaps die on 16 Jul or 6 Aug: "Eat as many 'dattes' [dates/fruit] as you like; I don't want any more... I've been caught out too often by dates." She wanted the Guérins to "have a party" at La Musse, not be sad. When a mouse was caught in the infirmary she made up a whole story, asking for the wounded mouse to be laid beside her and examined by the doctor, to make the Sisters laugh. "Holy Poverty! How funny, a saint who won't go to Heaven!"

### E-P6-154 — 10 July 1897 (DE), infirmary — "Little children are not damned"
- What happened: Céline quoted a saint who, after years of penance, still feared damnation while a breath remained. She answered at once that she could not share this fear: she was too small; little children are not damned. Céline's 1910 account puts it in July-August 1897: "I'm too little to be damned; little children are never damned".

### E-P6-155 — early July 1897 (editors: c. 8-11 Jul), Ms C 36v-37r — Manuscript C breaks off in pencil
- What happened: Ms C ends on folio 37r with "par la confiance et l'amour...": "even if I had on my conscience all the sins that can be committed, I would go, my heart broken with repentance, and throw myself into Jesus' arms". It is the last page of the autobiographical manuscripts.

### E-P6-156 — 11 July 1897 (recalling July 1889) — "How little I have lived"; "if I had committed every possible crime"
- What happened: "Alas, how little I have lived! Life always seemed very short to me. My childhood days, it seems they were yesterday." "People might think it's because I haven't sinned that I have such great confidence in God. Tell them clearly, Mother, that if I had committed every possible crime I would still have the same confidence." She recited verses of "La jeune poitrinaire" ("my days are condemned... when you see the dead leaves fall... pray to God for me"). She recalled a grace in "the grotto of St Mary Magdalene" in July 1889: for a week "it was as if a veil were thrown over all earthly things... I did things as if not doing them; it was as if I'd been lent a body" (while in charge of the refectory).
- Cards: F-OW-256
- Best details:
  - « si j'avais commis tous les crimes possibles, j'aurais toujours la même confiance » (F-OW-256).

- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-157 — night of 12-13 July 1897, infirmary — A communion stanza composed in the night
- What happened: During the night of 12 July 1897 she composed a stanza to prepare for Communion. It begins "Toi qui connais ma petitesse extrême" and ends "Viens en mon cœur!" (catalogued PS 8). Another dating exists for this verse. Piat and the Prioress's Epilogue place it at the Communion of 16 July: she composed verses the night before, and Marie Guérin sang them (see E-P6-174). The Carmel editors date PS 8 to "a few days before 16 July 1897". The Carnet jaune (13 Jul 1897, no. 4) says she composed the verse "during the night" for the Communion: "I composed it very easily, it is extraordinary; I thought I could no longer make verses." On the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, 16 Jul 1897, Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie sang it before her Communion in the infirmary, and after Communion the verse of "Vivre d'amour" (stanza 14). The original is lost; the text is known from a facsimile of the Process (session 19).
- Cards: F-EW-1464, F-EW-2626, F-EW-2104, F-OW-581
- Best details:
  - First line "Toi qui connais ma petitesse extrême" (F-EW-1464).
  - Date conflict: night of 12-13 July (Pauline, F-EW-1464) versus the night before 16 July (Epilogue, Piat).
  - « Je l'ai composé bien facilement, c'est extraordinaire ; je croyais que je ne pouvais plus faire de vers. » (F-OW-581).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès, who recorded it (NV f. 260r). High. | Pauline (NV f. 260r); Prioress's Epilogue; Piat. High. | Her own writing (PS 8); Mère Agnès (CJ 13.7.4); Carmel editors. High.
- Conflicts: 12-13 July versus 15-16 July for the composition of the stanza "Toi qui connais ma petitesse extrême". | Composed about 13 July (Carnet jaune, Carmel editors) versus the night of 12-13 July (Pauline NV) versus the night before 16 July (Epilogue, Piat).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: she thought she could no longer write verses, and wrote one in a sleepless night.

### E-P6-158 — July-Sept 1897, infirmary — "Oh, it's my Sr Thérèse de St Augustin!"
- What happened: A few days after Thérèse went down to the infirmary, Thérèse de St Augustin visited. Thérèse held out her arms, cried "Oh, it's my Sr Thérèse de St Augustin!" and asked the young Sisters present to leave them alone. Another time a Sister present said: "What a look! How happy she is to see her." After a community visit Thérèse told one of her sisters she had noticed TSA "especially": "one can see she loves me".
- Cards: F-EW-1613
- Best details:
  - "one can see she loves me" (F-EW-1613).
- Evidence: TSA (Souvenirs §15-16; the last remark was reported to her). Medium-high. The standard reading: the warmth was Thérèse's victory over natural antipathy (not stated).
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-159 — 12 July 1897, Carmel infirmary — "A bed of misfortune, one always misses the train"
- What happened: Marie wrote that on 12 Jul Thérèse had been lowered into the infirmary bed of Mother Geneviève, who had also several times wanted and been disappointed of death. Thérèse said: "What a bed of misfortune! When you are in it you always miss the train." Then: "The thief has gone far away, he left me to steal other children. When will it be my turn? I do not know now." And: "Tell my dear little Uncle, my Aunt, Léonie, all, that when I am in Heaven what will give me the most happiness will be to be able then to express all my love to them. I cannot on earth; my love is too strong; in Heaven I will make them understand." Asked what she would say on seeing God for the first time: "Do not speak of it, I cannot think of it, it makes me so happy. What I will do... I will weep for joy." Marie du Sacré-Cœur's record (8-9 Jul) adds: Mère Geneviève had received Extreme Unction three times in the same bed. After the doctor found her better: "The 'Thief' has gone again!"
- Cards: F-EW-2624, F-OW-259
- Best details:
  - « What a bed of misfortune! When you are in it you always miss the train. » (F-EW-2624).
  - "The thief" is death (Gospel image); "the train" is a modern image (F-EW-2624 notes).
  - Mère Geneviève anointed three times in the same bed (F-OW-259).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting Marie's notes. Medium-high. | Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Dernières paroles). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-160 — 18 June to July 1897, Lisieux — The Guérins on Thérèse's dying: "I will engrave all she says and does"
- What happened: On 12 Jul 1897 Mme Guérin wrote to Marie: "We are in admiration of all that is told us of her. But that is not enough: we must imitate her. And how to reach such detachment, such happiness in dying? Until now I had only read that in the lives of the saints. Today we have the example before us." Later she wrote that Thérèse entered Carmel "with her baptismal innocence". Isidore wrote: "I see her with her little angelic face waiting for death with joy... I will engrave in my memory all she says and does, to try to reproduce it on the day of my death." On 18 Jun 1897 he had written to Céline: "I keep silence... a silence of admiration and love for God and for the poor dear little creature he has raised to so high a perfection."
- Cards: F-EW-2625
- Best details:
  - "I will engrave in my memory all she says and does" (F-EW-2625).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting family letters. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The 18 June letter is addressed to "Sœur Geneviève" (Céline) per Piat.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-161 — June-July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — "I'll come and fetch you"; "Mademoiselle Lili"; the "Bon Sauveur"
- What happened: Sr Geneviève recorded that on 12 Jul 1897 Thérèse said Céline would feel her departure most, "because as soon as she has trouble she comes to me and she'll have no one... but God will give her strength... and then, I'll come back!" "I'll come and fetch you as soon as possible and I'll get Papa in on it, you know he was always in a hurry..." In June, pointing her finger at the Child Jesus as if giving a lesson: "My little Jesus, if you take me, you'll have to take Mademoiselle Lili too. That's my condition... no middle way, take it or leave it!" When Céline said she would go mad losing her: "If you go mad, bobonne, the 'Bon Sauveur' will come and get you!" (the asylum where their father had been).
- Cards: F-OW-258
- Best details:
  - « Si vous êtes folle, bobonne, le "Bon Sauveur" viendra vous chercher !.. » (F-OW-258).
  - "you'll have to take Mademoiselle Lili too. That's my condition" (F-OW-258).
- Evidence: Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline). Medium: Céline's notes reworked later; Langlois quotes them as authentic in substance. A joke about her father's asylum shows how the family spoke of it between themselves.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-162 — 13 July 1897, Carmel garden / infirmary — The robin, the blackbirds and the strawberries
- What happened: A little robin came to visit her from time to time, and she protected all the birds of the garden. When Marie wanted to set traps for blackbirds eating the strawberries, Thérèse told her not to hurt them. She promised to send fruit from heaven if Marie did not destroy the little birds.
- Cards: F-EW-1465
- Best details:
  - « Ne leur faites pas de mal, ils n'ont que la vie pour jouir. » (F-EW-1465).
  - Marie, as provisoire, was responsible for the garden produce; fulfilment is in M-EW-103 (F-EW-1465 notes).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a promise of fruit from heaven for blackbirds.

### E-P6-163 — 13 July 1897, infirmary — "I will go down there to help the missionaries"
- What happened: On 13 July 1897 she told Marie how many plans she was making for heaven. She would begin her mission of making God loved, help priests and the whole Church, help the missionaries and stop little "savages" from dying before baptism. The Carnet jaune, 13 Jul: "Don't think that when I'm in Heaven I'll drop roast larks into your beaks... That's not what I had or wanted. You may have great trials, but I'll send you lights to value and love them." "Don't imagine I feel a lively joy at dying, like the joy I used to feel going for a month to Trouville or Alençon... Only one expectation makes my heart beat: the love I'll receive and that I'll be able to give. And I think of all the good I'd like to do after my death: have little children baptised, help priests, missionaries, the whole Church... but first comfort my little Sisters." "Since my childhood God gave me the deep feeling that I would die young." Marie du Sacré-Cœur recorded (12-16 Jul): "Plans to come back with my little Sisters, and go over there to help the missionaries, and then stop the little heathens from dying before being baptised." "If you had to begin your life again, how would you do it?" "I'd do as I did."
- Cards: F-EW-1466, F-EW-1467, F-OW-260, F-OW-261
- Best details:
  - « Si vous saviez comme je fais des projets, que de choses je ferai quand je serai au ciel! » (F-EW-1466).
  - « J'irai là-bas aider aux missionnaires et empêcher les petits enfants de mourir sans baptême » (F-EW-1467).
  - « Ne croyez pas que lorsque je serai au Ciel je vous ferai tomber des alouettes rôties dans le bec... » (F-OW-260).
  - "If you had to begin your life again?" "I'd do as I did." (F-OW-261).
- Evidence: Marie (heard; 1910 and 1915). High. Céline links this to the 1898 mission statistics (F-EW-1757); Marie links it to the missionary letters (M-EW-015). | Mère Agnès (CJ 13.7); Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Dernières paroles). Medium (Langlois: sayings about her mission after death are the ones most open to reworking).
- Conflicts: The famous "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth" is dated 17 Jul (F-OW-260 notes).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-164 — 13 July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 253 to Bellière: "I will do more than write... I will be very near him"
- What happened: On 13 Jul 1897 Thérèse writes: "Perhaps when you read this little note I will no longer be on earth but in eternal delights! I do not know the future, but I can tell you with assurance that the Bridegroom is at the door; it would take a miracle to hold me in exile and I do not think Jesus will do this useless miracle." She is "happy to die... because I feel that such is God's will and because, much more than here below, I will be useful to the souls dear to me, yours especially". If the Lord prolongs her pilgrimage some weeks she will "scribble" more notes, "but most likely I will do more than write to my dear little brother... I will be very near him, I will see all that he needs, and I will give the good God no rest until he has given me all I want!" When he goes to Africa "I will follow him no longer in thought or prayer; my soul will be always with him... a little sister that Jesus gave him not to be his support for two years but until the last day of his life." "The good God has always treated me as a spoiled child; his cross has followed me since the cradle, but Jesus made me love it with passion." The prioress "would have liked to keep me here below a long time yet" and had a novena of Masses said at Our Lady of Victories; "feeling the miracle would not take place, I asked and obtained from the Blessed Virgin that she console my Mother's heart, or rather make her consent that Jesus take me to Heaven".
- Cards: F-OW-504
- Best details:
  - « il faudrait un miracle pour me retenir dans l'exil et je ne pense pas que Jésus fasse ce miracle inutile » (F-OW-504).
  - « ne laisserai pas de repos au bon Dieu qu'Il ne m'ait donné tout ce que je voudrai !... » (F-OW-504).
  - « le bon Dieu m'a toujours traitée en enfant gâtée » (F-OW-504).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 253). High. The 1911 edition prints the first part as "Lettre IXe, 26 juillet 1897" (see E-P6-165).
- Conflicts: The letter says Bellière is to go to Africa, so his posting was known by July 1897. The novena was made by the prioress (also LT 244).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-165 — texts of 9 Jun and 13 Jul 1897; edition of 1911 — The 1911 edition splits and redates her farewell letters to Bellière
- What happened: The 1911 edition prints two short "letters" to Bellière as "Lettre IXe, 26 juillet 1897" and "Lettre Xe, 14 août 1897". Comparison with the Carmel text shows: (1) "Lettre IXe" ("Quand vous lirez ce petit mot, peut-être ne serai-je plus sur la terre... O mon frère, que je suis heureuse de mourir...") is the opening of LT 253, written on 13 Jul 1897, so 1911 moves it 13 days later. (2) In LT 253 she is glad to die "because I feel it is God's will and that much more than here below I will be useful to souls dear to me"; the 1911 text keeps only the second reason. (3) "Lettre Xe" ("Au moment de paraître devant le bon Dieu, je comprends plus que jamais qu'il n'y a qu'une chose nécessaire... Je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie...") is the ending of LT 244, written on 9 Jun 1897, so 1911 moves it more than two months later, to 14 Aug 1897. (4) 1911 leaves out the first words of LT 244 and the sentence about the novena to Our Lady of Victories.
- Cards: F-OW-498
- Best details:
  - LT 253: « parce que je sens que telle est la volonté du bon Dieu et que bien plus qu'ici-bas, je serai utile aux âmes qui me sont chères » (F-OW-498).
- Evidence: LT 244 and LT 253 (Carmel site) compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911. High (both texts read).
- Conflicts: The 26 July date given in 1911 is the date of a different letter (LT 261); the reason is not known. The 1911 letters make her "happy to die because useful after death" look like the words of her last weeks (26 July) and "I am not dying, I am entering life" look like 14 Aug; in fact the second is from 9 June, nearly four months before her death.
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the edited letters)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-166 — 14 July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 254 to Roulland: "for five or six weeks I live only on milk"; "I intend not to be idle in Heaven"
- What happened: On 14 Jul 1897 she answers Roulland's "I am a baby learning to talk": "Well, I too, for five or six weeks I have been a baby, for I live only on lolo (baby word for milk), but soon I will sit at the heavenly banquet." "I intend not to stay idle in Heaven; my desire is to work still for the Church and souls; I ask it of God and I am certain he will grant it." She will ask for him "the palm of martyrdom" and "be near you, supporting your hand so that it gathers without effort this glorious palm". "Suffering has long been my Heaven here below, and I truly find it hard to imagine how I will get used to a country where joy reigns without any mixture of sadness; Jesus will have to transform my soul and give it the capacity to enjoy, otherwise I could not bear the eternal delights." She asks him to console the prioress, "whose sensitive and motherly heart has trouble consenting to my departure".
- Cards: F-OW-505
- Best details:
  - « depuis cinq ou six semaines, je suis aussi un bébé, car je ne vis que de lolo » (F-OW-505).
  - « Je compte bien ne pas rester inactive au Ciel, mon désir est de travailler encore pour l'Eglise et les âmes » (F-OW-505).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 254). High. The 1911 edition prints "not idle in Heaven" and "mon ciel ici-bas"; "je ne vis que de lolo" and "commissions pour le Ciel" are absent.
- Conflicts: The letter does not mention her move to the infirmary on 8 July. "Five or six weeks" from 14 Jul is early June 1897: a dated milk-diet detail. A rare admission that she does not know how she will get used to Heaven.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-167 — 15 July 1897 (NV) — Her way is for "little souls", not for extraordinary ways
- What happened: Pauline says Thérèse's life was very simple, otherwise she could not be the model of "little souls", which she called her way. Thérèse said her manuscript would help all souls "except those in extraordinary ways", and that dying in ecstasy after Communion "would not be like my little way". When the sisters said they might have a heavenly vision at her death, she said quickly she had never wanted extraordinary graces; that was not her little way.
- Cards: F-EW-1234, F-EW-1285
- Best details:
  - « jamais je n'ai désiré pour moi de grâces extraordinaires, ce n'est pas 'ma petite voie' » (F-EW-1285).
  - Dying in ecstasy after Communion "would not be like my little way" (F-EW-1234).
  - She recalled her poem on Mary's life at Nazareth, without raptures or miracles (F-EW-1285).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1910); Céline (present, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-168 — July 1897 (to 1 Aug), infirmary — Haemorrhages two or three times a day; blood as martyrdom
- What happened: Until 1 August the haemorrhages came two or three times a day and the choking was terrible. She inhaled ether, but the breathlessness was so strong it no longer worked. A burning fever consumed her every day, and she said she felt she was in purgatory. When the haemorrhages came she was glad, thinking she was shedding her blood for God, since she was dying a martyr of love.
- Cards: F-EW-1425, F-EW-1429
- Best details:
  - Ether no longer helped (F-EW-1425).
  - "a martyr of love" (F-EW-1429).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw, 1915); Céline (heard, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-169 — told 15 July 1897; event undated — Marie Guérin's candle lit from the half-dead lamp
- What happened: Thérèse told how Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie, needing to light candles for a procession without matches, lit her candle from the almost-extinct lamp before the relics, and from it lit all the community's candles. Thérèse used it as an image of hidden souls and the communion of saints, and explained it to Pauline in the infirmary. The Carnet jaune adds: "It's the same with the Communion of Saints... How many times have I thought I might owe all the graces I've received to the prayers of a soul who asked God for me and whom I'll know only in Heaven." "In Heaven one won't meet indifferent looks."
- Cards: F-EW-1469, F-EW-1470, F-OW-262
- Best details:
  - A nearly dead night-light lighting every candle in the procession (F-EW-1469, F-EW-1470).
  - « Au Ciel on ne rencontrera pas de regards indifférents » (F-OW-262).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 15.7.5). Medium-high. Mère Agnès notes: "the conversation was too long, I could not take everything, nor word for word."
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: a small image of the communion of saints.

### E-P6-170 — 15 July 1897, infirmary — She refuses to die after Communion on Our Lady of Mount Carmel
- What happened: On 15 July 1897 someone said she might die the next day, feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, after Communion. She protested: that would be too beautiful, and would not fit her little way. Little souls could not imitate that.
- Cards: F-EW-1471
- Best details:
  - "too beautiful" and not her little way (F-EW-1471).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-171 — July-Sept 1897 — The community rarely visited; she could not bear noise
- What happened: The community seldom visited her in the infirmary so as not to tire her; she was very weak and could not bear the least noise. Marthe, as her novice, went rarely; in the last weeks, on kitchen duty, a little more often. Marthe says she knew almost nothing of the illness and did not attend the agony.
- Cards: F-EW-1619
- Best details:
  - Marthe did not attend the agony (F-EW-1619).
- Evidence: Marthe; Marie des Anges (notes). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-172 — 16 July 1897 (Pri 20) — Prayers Thérèse composed for Marthe
- What happened: Thérèse wrote several prayer formulas for Marthe (MJ). One is an evening prayer before the tabernacle, confessing the day's faults (Pri 7). Another is a prayer to obtain humility (Pri 20), given for Marthe's birthday on 16 July 1897. Pri 20 is dated on the page "16 juillet 1897". It is a prayer beginning "O Jesus, when you were a Traveller on earth you said: Learn from me that I am meek and humble of heart." She asks to submit her will to her Sisters "without asking whether they have the right to command me", as Jesus obeyed even his executioners. She asks "that my sisters always put me in the last place and that I be truly convinced this place is mine", and asks Jesus "to send me a humiliation each time I try to raise myself above others". She notes the daily failure: "each morning I take the resolution... and in the evening I see I have committed many faults of pride; I am tempted to be discouraged, but... discouragement is also pride." She repeats: "O Jesus, meek and humble of heart, make my heart like yours." LT 256 (16 Jul 1897) tells Sr Marthe she will give her "the prayer on humility, not quite finished being copied".
- Cards: F-EW-1472, F-OW-582
- Best details:
  - Pri 20 for a birthday, 16 July 1897 (F-EW-1472).
  - « le découragement est aussi de l'orgueil » (F-OW-582).
  - « je veux, afin de répondre à votre amour, désirer que mes soeurs me mettent toujours à la dernière place » (F-OW-582).
- Evidence: Marthe (recipient; PO 15 with long quotations). High. | Her own writing (Pri 20; LT 256). High. Absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: The dedication to Sr Marthe is our link from LT 256 and the same date; the prayer page itself names no recipient.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-173 — July-Sept 1897, Lisieux Carmel — The feasts that mark her last months
- What happened: The file ties events to feasts: 16 July (Our Lady of Mount Carmel), when Pauline suggested she might die after Communion. 6 Aug (Transfiguration), for which the Sisters placed the Holy Face picture by her bed. 19 Aug (St Hyacinth in the Carmelite calendar), her last Communion, offered for Hyacinthe Loyson. 24 Sept, anniversary of her taking the veil, for which Pauline had a Mass said. Laveille adds: on 24 Sep, anniversary of her veiling, Mass was said for her and she said, "My good… that, without doubt, is to suffer." From 25 Sep she could not move alone and voices near her bed were torture. She held a small crucifix "its Figure worn by her caresses".
- Cards: F-EW-1583, F-LF-236
- Best details:
  - 24 Sept: anniversary of her taking the veil, Mass said by Pauline's arrangement (F-EW-1583).
  - A crucifix with its Figure worn by her caresses (F-LF-236).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; NV f. 262r, 272r, 216r, 288r). High. | Laveille (pp. 388-396). High.
- Conflicts: 15 July (F-EW-1471) versus 16 July here for the Carmel feast.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-174 — 16 July 1897 (Our Lady of Mount Carmel), Lisieux Carmel — The Communion of Our Lady of Mount Carmel and the farewell letter to the Guérins
- What happened: For her Communion on the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, 16 July 1897, she composed verses the night before. The cloisters were strewn with wild flowers and rose petals. A young priest saying his first Mass in the Carmel chapel brought the Viaticum. Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (her cousin Marie Guérin, "exceptionally sweet" voice) sang, before Communion, a small verse Thérèse had composed ("You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me! Come into my heart, O white Host I love..."). After Communion she sang a verse of "Vivre d'amour": "To die of love is a sweet martyrdom." Thérèse wrote her farewell letter to her adoptive parents that day, on the Communion "made so touching, or rather so triumphant, by your flowered sheaves". She said of Marie's singing: "I cannot tell you how high and beautiful her voice was; she had promised not to cry, to please me; my hopes were far exceeded." She also promised the childless cousin Jeanne a "cherub" or else "mother of many children" (souls); "True I am like a lark except when the fever attacks me; happily, it only comes at night, when larks sleep." LT 255 (16 Jul 1897, to her uncle and aunt Guérin) in her own words: she is glad to show that "your little Thérèse has not yet left exile", but "with a trembling hand". "It is true that I am like a finch except when I have the fever; fortunately it usually comes to visit me only in the evening, at the hour when finches sleep, head hidden under the wing." "One day I think I am at the door of Heaven because of the dismayed look of Mr de Cornière, and the next day he goes off joyful, saying 'You are on the way to recovery'... What I judge (I, little baby at the milk) is that I will not recover, but that I could drag on for a long time yet." She promises Jeanne (La Néele), who lacks a child, "the most beautiful cherub in Heaven", so that Jeanne may have "a great pontiff and a great saint".
- Cards: F-EW-2626, F-EW-2104, F-LF-233, F-LF-234, F-OW-506
- Best details:
  - « To die of love is a sweet martyrdom » sung after Communion (F-EW-2626).
  - "she had promised not to cry, to please me" (F-EW-2626).
  - "like a lark except when the fever attacks me; happily, it only comes at night, when larks sleep" (F-LF-234).
  - « je suis comme un pinson excepté quand j'ai la fièvre ; heureusement elle ne vient ordinairement me visiter que le soir » (F-OW-506).
  - « Ce que je juge, moi (petit bébé au lolo), c'est que je ne guérirai pas mais que je pourrais traîner longtemps encore » (F-OW-506).
- Evidence: Piat (1967, letter quoted); Prioress's Epilogue (D6 p. 209); Laveille (Summarium 1919). High. Communion verse is one of her last poems. | Her own writing (LT 255). High. Absent from the 1911 letters ("de Cornière", "pinson excepté quand j'ai la fièvre").
- Conflicts: Verse composed the night before 16 July here, versus the night of 12-13 July in Pauline's notebook. Communion from 16 August to death not possible because of vomiting (D6 p. 208; LAV p. 368): see E-P6-314. | Evening fever here, versus 3 o'clock in LT 222 bis. The letter does not say who wrote the verse Marie sang first (Thérèse composed it "for this morning").
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-175 — 16 July 1897, infirmary — A saying scratched out by Mère Agnès
- What happened: With "the air of a child with a nice trick in mind": "I'd like to give you a proof of love no one has ever given you..." The next words were scratched out by Mère Agnès ("Mère Agnès a gratté la suite !", Carmel editors' note). On the same day Thérèse said "You alone know all the folds of my little soul...", which Mère Agnès later used to claim authority over what would be written about her.
- Cards: F-OW-263
- Best details:
  - « Je voudrais vous donner un témoignage d'amour que personne ne vous ait jamais donné... » (F-OW-263).
  - The erased continuation (F-OW-263).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 16.7.4-5; "Remarque importante" at the end of cj-septembre-1897); Carmel editors' note on the erasure. High that the text was erased; its content is unknown. Direct evidence of Mère Agnès editing her own record.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-176 — 16 July 1897, infirmary — "Do not worry in advance": persecutions and massacres
- What happened: Mère Agnès said to Thérèse, "I fear that in dying you will suffer a great deal". Thérèse answered: "Why are you afraid in advance? Wait at least until it happens to be sad. Do you see me torment myself thinking that if persecutions and massacres come, as is foretold, they may tear out your eyes!"
- Cards: F-OW-521
- Best details:
  - « Voyez-vous que je me mette à me tourmenter en pensant que s'il survient des persécutions et des massacres, comme c'est prédit, on vous arrachera peut-être les yeux ! » (F-OW-521).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 16.7.1). High (contemporary notebook). Keyword check: no "massacre" in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none. Compare LT 192 (16 Jul 1896) on Abbé Lechêne's sermon about a new persecution.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: the community's fear of persecution enters the infirmary.

### E-P6-177 — 16 July 1897, infirmary — Céline's entry as proof that "all my great desires will be granted"
- What happened: She said she had completely given up Céline but could not say she no longer wanted her. "Often in summer, in the hour of silence before Matins, sitting on the terrace, I said to myself: Ah! if my Céline were here beside me! But no! that would be too great a happiness for earth!" It seemed an impossible dream. She desired it "not by nature" but for Céline's soul. When she saw Céline enter, "and not only enter but given to me completely to teach her everything", "I understood what immensity of love God has for me". She concludes: "if a desire barely expressed is thus fulfilled, then it is impossible that all my great desires... will not be completely fulfilled."
- Cards: F-OW-522
- Best details:
  - « si un désir à peine exprimé est ainsi comblé, il est donc impossible que tous mes grands désirs dont je parle si souvent au bon Dieu ne soient pas complètement exaucés. » (F-OW-522).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 16.7.2). High. Céline's entry (14 Sep 1894) was given to her as a formation charge.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-178 — 16 July 1897, infirmary — "The saints of the last times will surpass the first as cedars surpass other trees"
- What happened: She repeated "with a convinced air" a sentence she had read in the book "Les Petites Fleurs" by Abbé Bourbon: "The saints of the last times will surpass those of the first as much as the cedars surpass the other trees."
- Cards: F-OW-523
- Best details:
  - « Les saints des derniers temps surpasseront autant ceux des premiers que les cèdres surpassent les autres arbres. » (F-OW-523).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 16.7.3). High for the saying; the notebook gives the author only as "l'Abbé Bourb."; the work is not identified.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-179 — 17 July 1897 (letter); 3 June 1897 (image) — Her last letter to Léonie, in pencil
- What happened: Léonie keeps Thérèse's last letter to her, written in pencil on 17 July 1897 (LT 257). An image of 3 June 1897, sent when Léonie was thinking of a secular life, reads that one day they would follow the Lamb together for all eternity (LT 238). Laveille adds the letter of 17 Jul 1897 to Léonie: "I do not wish to enter heaven a minute sooner by my own will… If you wish to be a saint, that will be easy for you, because in your heart the world is nothing to you." LT 257 (17 Jul 1897) in full: "I am very happy to be able to talk with you still; a few days ago I no longer thought I would have this consolation on earth, but the good God seems to want to prolong my exile a little. I am not sad about it, for I would not enter Heaven one minute sooner by my own will." "The only happiness on earth is to apply oneself to find delightful always the part that Jesus gives us." She tells Léonie "the world is nothing to you", so she can be a saint. She promises: "You want me in Heaven to pray the Sacred Heart for you; be sure I will not forget to make your commissions to him and to claim all you need to become a great saint." She adds a note: "I will write you at more length another time; I cannot now, baby needing to go to sleep."
- Cards: F-EW-1434, F-LF-234, F-OW-507
- Best details:
  - Best line among letters Léonie quotes: "picking up a pin out of love can convert a soul" (LT 164) (F-EW-1434 notes).
  - "I do not wish to enter heaven a minute sooner by my own will" (F-LF-234).
  - « je ne voudrais point entrer au Ciel une minute plus tôt par ma propre volonté » (F-OW-507).
  - « je pourrai t'aimer encore davantage » (F-OW-507).
- Evidence: Léonie, holder of the letters (PO-07, PA-11). High. | Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 951 § 2780). High. Laveille printed 15 letters to the Guérins in his Appendix (p. 448-460). | Her own writing (LT 257). High. The 1911 edition has the passage on "une minute plus tôt"; the closing lines about "baby" and the Sacred Heart commissions are absent.
- Conflicts: A rare day documented twice (her letter and the Carnet jaune). In the letter she talks of Heaven as a place from which she can "love more" and "make commissions", not of doing good on earth.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-180 — 17 July 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary — "I will spend my heaven in doing good upon earth"
- What happened: On 17 Jul she told Mère Agnès: "I will spend my heaven in doing good upon earth." Céline heard her tell Agnès she wanted to spend her heaven doing good on earth. It is the line later put on her cross. The full Carnet jaune entry (Saturday 17 July 1897, after she spat blood at 2 a.m.): "I feel that I am going to enter into rest... But above all I feel that my mission is about to begin, my mission to make the good God loved as I love him, to give my little way to souls. If the good God grants my desires, my Heaven will pass on earth until the end of the world. Yes, I want to spend my Heaven doing good on earth. That is not impossible, since even in the very bosom of the beatific vision the Angels watch over us."
- Cards: F-LF-233, F-EW-1479, F-OW-525
- Best details:
  - « JE VEUX PASSER MON CIEL À FAIRE DU BIEN SUR LA TERRE » (F-LF-233, in capitals in Laveille's text).
  - « ma mission de faire aimer le bon Dieu comme je l'aime, de donner ma petite voie aux âmes » (F-OW-525).
  - "until the end of the world" is in the notebook, not only in later editions (F-OW-525).
- Evidence: Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 893 § 2585); Céline (heard). High. The baseline quotes the "heaven" line. | Mère Agnès (CJ 17.7, dated the same day). High for the notebook text; the wording differs slightly in later published versions. The baseline has the shorter form; Novissima Verba has this saying.
- Conflicts: Céline dates 17 July; other sources give 13 July for related plans in heaven (see E-P6-163). | Her letters of the same week say the idea differently: LT 253 (13 Jul), LT 254 (14 Jul), LT 257 (17 Jul). Langlois discusses the wording in the process versions.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-181 — 17 July 1897, infirmary — "I cannot rest as long as there are souls to be saved"
- What happened: The same day she said: "I cannot make a feast of enjoying, I cannot rest as long as there are souls to be saved... But when the Angel says 'Time is no more!' then I will rest, I will be able to enjoy, because the number of the elect will be complete and all will have entered into joy and rest. My heart leaps at this thought..."
- Cards: F-OW-526
- Best details:
  - « Je ne puis pas me faire une fête de jouir, je ne peux pas me reposer tant qu'il y aura des âmes à sauver... » (F-OW-526).
