- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 17.7, second paragraph). High. The phrase "Le temps n'est plus" is from the Apocalypse (Rev 10:6, not quoted by her).
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-182 — 11 July to 11 Sept 1897 — Dated sayings recorded by Marie Guérin
- What happened: Marie de l'Eucharistie recorded sayings with dates. On 11 July 1897 she read the Imitation chapter on bearing others' faults. On 18 July: in heaven "I will do great things" and "I will follow you close" like a guardian angel, letting nothing pass. In July: reasoning about the prioress's words hurts God, even in one's heart. On 2 Aug: she takes no natural pleasure in being loved, but a very great one in being humiliated. On 11 Sept: be gentle; yesterday you hurt Sr xx, then another Sister did, and she cried. Piat gives the fuller wording of the 18 July saying to Marie. Marie asked Thérèse to obtain great graces for her once she was in eternity. Thérèse said: "Oh, when I am in Heaven I will do many things, great things... it is impossible that it is not God himself who gives me this desire; I am sure he will grant it!" And: "When I am up there I shall be after you closely." The timid Marie ventured: "Perhaps you will frighten me." Thérèse: "Does your guardian angel frighten you? Yet he follows you all the time; well, I will follow you the same, and closely. I shall let nothing pass." The 2 Aug saying is given in full: "I find no natural pleasure in being loved and petted, but I find a very great one in being humiliated. When I have done a foolish thing that humbles me and shows me what I am, oh! then I have a natural pleasure." The 11 Sept warning in full: "You must become very gentle. Never hard words, never a hard tone... If you had not treated her harshly she would have accepted the second pain better." Her last message to her father, relayed on 17 Sep: "If I thought I was well loved, I would never have believed they loved me so much!" Marie de l'Eucharistie's own notes (Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs) add a Sept saying: to Marie, who promised to become a saint once Thérèse was gone: "Don't wait for that... Believe me, never wait till tomorrow to start becoming a saint." In the same record, "it's me who'll be right on your heels!" for "I'll follow you closely".
- Cards: F-EW-1615, F-EW-2627, F-EW-2638, F-EW-2639, F-OW-264
- Best details:
  - 18 July: "I will follow you close" like a guardian angel (F-EW-1615).
  - 2 Aug: no natural pleasure in being loved, very great pleasure in being humiliated (F-EW-1615).
  - "Does your guardian angel frighten you? ... I shall let nothing pass." (F-EW-2627).
  - "I find no natural pleasure in being loved and petted, but I find a very great one in being humiliated" (F-EW-2638).
  - Piat: she stayed "the watcher" looking for her novices' failings and "the little brush" painting the image of the perfect religious on their souls until the end (F-EW-2639).
  - "never wait till tomorrow to start becoming a saint" (F-OW-264).
- Evidence: Marie de l'Eucharistie (heard; copy of a lost 1897 notebook). Medium-high. Some appear in Derniers Entretiens "other sayings". | Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters and notes. Medium-high. | Marie de l'Eucharistie (recorder). Medium.
- Conflicts: "Sister X" (11 Sept) is not identified.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-183 — 18 July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 258 to Bellière: "the thought of heavenly happiness gives me no joy"
- What happened: Bellière wrote that she should not tell him of her joy at approaching happiness. Thérèse writes: "If for a few moments you could read my soul, how surprised you would be! The thought of heavenly happiness, far from causing me any joy, makes me wonder sometimes how it will be possible for me to be happy without suffering. Jesus will surely change my nature; otherwise I would miss suffering and the valley of tears." "I never asked God to die young; that would have seemed cowardice to me; but from childhood he gave me the inner conviction that my course here below would be short. So the only thing that makes me joyful is the thought of doing the Lord's will." She then quotes Jesus at the Last Supper ("I go to my Father... it is to your advantage that I go") and promises: "When I am in port I will teach you how to sail on the stormy sea of the world." She tells him "you are called to rise to God by the elevator of love and not to climb the rough stairs of fear", and says "soon, like St Augustine, you will say: love is the weight that draws me".
- Cards: F-OW-508
- Best details:
  - « La pensée du bonheur céleste, non seulement ne me cause aucune joie, mais encore je me demande parfois comment il me sera possible d'être heureuse sans souffrir » (F-OW-508).
  - « Jamais je n'ai demandé au bon Dieu de mourir jeune, cela m'aurait paru de la lâcheté » (F-OW-508).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 258). High. The 1911 edition prints part of this letter ("jamais je n'ai demandé", "quand je serai au port"); the first paragraph is absent from 1911.
- Conflicts: This letter corrects the impression of a young nun who longed for death. It is dated the day after the "spend my Heaven doing good on earth" saying (17 July, Carnet jaune).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-184 — 18 July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — The parable of the two children and the father (the one who asks to be punished with a kiss)
- What happened: To help Bellière trust Jesus, Thérèse tells this story: "I suppose a father has two mischievous and disobedient children. Coming to punish them, he sees one tremble and move away in terror, though he feels in his heart that he deserves punishment. His brother, on the contrary, throws himself into his father's arms, saying he regrets having hurt him, that he loves him, and that to prove it he will be good from now on. Then the child asks his father to punish him with a kiss. I do not think the happy father's heart could resist the filial confidence of his child, whose sincerity and love he knows. He knows that more than once his son will fall again into the same faults, but he is disposed to forgive him always, if always his son takes him by the heart... I say nothing of the first child, dear little brother; you must understand whether his father can love him as much and treat him with the same indulgence as the other."
- Cards: F-OW-509
- Best details:
  - « cet enfant demande à son père de le punir par un baiser » (F-OW-509).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 258). High. Absent from the 1911 edition. LT 191 (12 Jul 1896) tells a one-child version.
- Conflicts: none. The two-children version makes a contrast between the fearful child and the trusting child, among the clearest short statements of the "little way" for a priest.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-185 — 18 July 1897 (photograph taken before, for the prioress's feast) — The photograph "in view of my death" and Mère Agnès who "speaks of my death like a feast"
- What happened: In the same letter (LT 258) Thérèse tells Bellière that Mère Agnès "is twice my sister and served as my mother in my childhood". "Our good Mother" feared Agnès's sensitive nature would make her departure bitter. "The contrary happened: she speaks of my death as a feast, and that is a great consolation to me." She cannot give him a photograph "because I have made the vow of poverty; I cannot even dispose of a holy card; only our Mother can satisfy you". "Just now, in view of my approaching death, a sister photographed me for our Mother's feast. The novices cried out when they saw that I had put on my 'grand air'; it seems I am usually smiling more; but believe, little brother, that if my photograph does not smile at you, my soul will not stop smiling at you when it is near you."
- Cards: F-OW-510
- Best details:
  - « elle parle de ma mort comme d'une fête et c'est une grande consolation pour moi » (F-OW-510).
  - « en vue de ma mort prochaine, une soeur m'a photographiée pour la fête de notre Mère. Les novices se sont écriées en me voyant que j'avais pris mon grand air » (F-OW-510).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 258). High. Absent from the 1911 edition.
- Conflicts: The photographer is not named in the letter.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-186 — 18 July 1897, infirmary — "God would not give me this desire unless he meant to fulfil it"; "only inconveniences"
- What happened: On 18 July Mère Agnès recorded: "The good God would not give me this desire to do good on earth after my death if he did not want to fulfil it; he would rather give me the desire to rest in him." And: "I have only inconveniences to bear, not sufferings."
- Cards: F-OW-527
- Best details:
  - « Je n'ai que des incommodités à supporter, pas des souffrances. » (F-OW-527).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 18.7.1-2). High. A dated "before the big sufferings" mark: the "great sufferings" are dated from 28 July.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-187 — 19 July 1897, infirmary — She will not ask what the chaplain said about her
- What happened: When Marie came back from a parlour with the chaplain, Thérèse wanted to ask what he said about her condition. She judged it curiosity and steered the conversation away so that Marie would not tell her. The Carnet jaune adds her reason: "since the good God does not allow her to tell me of her own accord, it is a sign he does not want me to know". She avoided bringing up the subject "for fear that Sr Marie would tell me as if forced". The notebook does not say what Youf said about her state.
- Cards: F-EW-1473, F-EW-1474, F-OW-528
- Best details:
  - She steered the talk away so that her sister would not be led to tell her (F-EW-1474).
  - « puisque le bon Dieu ne permet pas qu'elle me le dise d'elle-même, c'est signe qu'il ne veut pas que je le sache » (F-OW-528).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 19.7). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-188 — 19 July 1897, infirmary — "I am a little seed; no one knows yet what will come out of it"
- What happened: At the start of recreation Thérèse said "I am going to water this evening", and Mère Agnès said "but you should water me too!". Asked "What are you?", she answered "I am a little seed; one does not yet know what will come out of it." She also confessed that she had sought herself by wiping her face once more than necessary, "so that Sr Marie would see that I was sweating a great deal".
- Cards: F-OW-528
- Best details:
  - « Je suis une petite graine, on ne sait pas encore ce qui en sortira... » (F-OW-528).
  - Wiping her face once more than needed so Marie would see her sweat (F-OW-528).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 19.7.1-3). High. No "seed" in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-189 — 20 July 1897 (DE), infirmary — "My last look will be for Mother Marie de Gonzague"
- What happened: On her deathbed Thérèse told Mère Agnès not to think that her last look would be for her. It would be for Mother Marie de Gonzague and for those to whom it might be useful. She told Agnès not to be hurt, for she wanted to do only what is supernatural.
- Cards: F-EW-1320
- Best details:
  - « ne croyez pas, ma petite mère, que mon dernier regard sera pour vous; ce sera pour mère Marie de Gonzague » (F-EW-1320).
- Evidence: MdA, heard from Mère Agnès (second-hand); date from DE. Medium. Also in Last Conversations.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-190 — 20 July 1897 (13 July in one card), infirmary — "Happy to die in Gonzague's arms": no "human side"
- What happened: Thérèse told Mother Agnès she would be happy to die in the arms of Mother Marie de Gonzague, who represents God. With Agnès there would have been a human side, and she preferred only the divine. The occasion: Agnès was no longer prioress. Asked what she would have done if one of her sisters were ill in her place, she said she would have gone straight to recreation. Pauline adds, in parenthesis, that Thérèse had suffered much from this prioress. The Carnet jaune (20 July, after she spat blood at 3 a.m.): Mère Agnès asked: "What would you have done if one of us had been ill in your place? Would you have come to the infirmary during recreations?" She answered: "I would have gone straight to recreation without asking any news, but I would have done it quite simply so that nobody would notice my sacrifice. If I had come to the infirmary, I would have done it to please, never to satisfy myself." Then: "The good God has himself represented by whom he wishes, but that does not matter... With you there would have been a human side; I prefer that there be only the divine. Yes, I say it from the bottom of my heart, I am happy to die in the arms of our Mother, because she represents God."
- Cards: F-EW-1476, F-EW-1477, F-EW-1478, F-EW-1468, F-OW-529, F-OW-530
- Best details:
  - « avec vous, ma petite mère, il y aurait eu un côté humain, j'aime mieux qu'il n'y ait que du divin » (F-EW-1478).
  - « (Elle avait beaucoup souffert de cette mère prieure) » (F-EW-1476), Pauline's parenthesis.
  - Asked what she would have done if a Sister were ill in her place: gone straight to recreation (F-EW-1476).
  - « J'aurais été tout droit à la récréation sans demander aucune nouvelle, mais j'aurais fait cela bien simplement pour que personne ne s'aperçoive de mon sacrifice. » (F-OW-529).
  - « je suis heureuse de mourir entre les bras de Notre Mère, parce qu'elle représente le bon Dieu. » (F-OW-530).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915); Marie (heard, 1910, 1915); Geneviève gives the same idea. High. A rare sworn comment on Gonzague's treatment of Thérèse. | Mère Agnès (CJ 20.7.1-4). High.
- Conflicts: Date: 20 July (F-EW-1476, F-EW-1477, F-EW-1478) versus 13 July DE (F-EW-1468). The judgment "she had suffered a great deal" is Pauline's own. Céline adds the same theme for her last look (F-EW-1669). | "Notre Mère" may be Mère Marie de Gonzague; the notebook does not say. The Carnet jaune dates the arms remark to 20 July, as do the process cards.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: she prefers to die in the arms of the prioress who wounded her.

### E-P6-191 — 20 July 1897, infirmary — "They harass me with questions, like Joan of Arc before her judges"
- What happened: The Sisters, wanting to profit from her last days, questioned her about everything. She said it made her think of Joan of Arc before her tribunal, and she felt she answered with the same sincerity. The Carnet jaune (20.7.6) gives the same comparison: "I am harassed with questions; it makes me think of Joan of Arc before her tribunal! It seems to me I answer with the same sincerity." The context (who questioned her, and about what) is not given. Novissima Verba also has "tribunal".
- Cards: F-EW-1475, F-OW-531
- Best details:
  - « On me harcèle de questions, cela me fait penser à Jeanne d'Arc devant son tribunal. » (F-EW-1475).
  - « On me harcèle de questions, cela me fait penser à Jeanne d'Arc devant son tribunal ! » (F-OW-531).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 264v-265r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 20.7.6). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-192 — 20 July 1897, Lisieux Carmel (letter to Céline Maudelonde) — "It is not an extraordinary sanctity; it is love"
- What happened: On 20 Jul 1897 Marie wrote to her cousin Céline Maudelonde: "It is not an extraordinary sanctity, not a love of extraordinary penances, no, it is love of God. People of the world can imitate her holiness, for she strove only to do everything out of love and to accept all the small contrarieties, all the small sacrifices that come at every moment, as from God's hand. She sees God in everything and does all her actions as perfectly as possible. Always duty before all! And pleasure she knew how to sanctify, while tasting it, by offering it to God." She added: "I asked her the other day: Have you ever refused God anything? She answered: I do not remember. Even when I was very small, from the age of three, I began to refuse God nothing of what he asked."
- Cards: F-EW-2628
- Best details:
  - "pleasure she knew how to sanctify, while tasting it, by offering it to God" (F-EW-2628).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letter. Medium-high. A contemporary reading of Thérèse from the novice room within weeks of her death; the "from age three" claim is Thérèse's, told by Marie.
- Conflicts: "I do not remember. Even when I was very small..." differs from Marie's later "never" (F-EW-1347).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-193 — 20 July 1897, Lisieux Carmel — Thérèse smiles at the doctor's forecast; the community becomes resigned
- What happened: Marie wrote that Thérèse now got up for an hour each day, but haemoptysis threatened. "When Dr de Cornière says she cannot go on many months like this, she cannot help smiling and has trouble hiding her satisfaction; one sees her face light up with happiness." Marie added on 20 Jul to her parents: "Now we are resigned and ready for the sacrifice... It is a consolation for us not to see her suffer more and to see the joy with which she leaves for Heaven." Piat says the expected end was then postponed beyond all forecasts, and that the drama lasted over two more months, explained by Thérèse's desire to co-operate in the redemption of souls.
- Cards: F-EW-2629
- Best details:
  - "one sees her face light up with happiness" at the doctor's forecast (F-EW-2629).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-194 — 1897 (undated) — A capacity for suffering, a small one for joy
- What happened: Recorded in Céline's "Varia 3": "I had a very great capacity for suffering and a very small one for enjoying; I couldn't bear joy. Joy, for example, took away all my appetite, while on days I had much sorrow I ate like four. It was the opposite of everyone else." Also: "How sorry I'd be to have read all those books!" in front of a bookcase: "I'd have broken my head, I'd have lost precious time I could have used simply to love God." And: "I'm counting on not staying idle in Heaven; my desire is to work for the Church and souls" (from a letter). The Carnet jaune (29 July) adds: "I am weary of the earth! They pay compliments when one does not deserve them and reproaches when one does not deserve them either." "What causes our humiliation at the moment causes our glory afterwards, even in this life." "I have no capacity for enjoyment, I have always been like that; but I have a very great one for suffering. In the past, when I had great pain, I felt appetite in the refectory, but when I had joy it was the opposite: impossible to eat."
- Cards: F-OW-248, F-OW-554
- Best details:
  - « les jours où j'avais beaucoup de peine je mangeais comme quatre » (F-OW-248).
  - « Je suis lasse de la terre ! On fait des compliments quand on n'en mérite pas et des reproches quand on n'en mérite pas non plus. » (F-OW-554).
- Evidence: An unnamed recorder (Varia collection). Medium. | Mère Agnès (CJ 29.7.10-14). High. A body fact: pain made her hungry, joy took away her appetite.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-195 — 20 July 1897, infirmary — "Mortal sin would not take away my trust"
- What happened: Mère Agnès recorded: "... Mortal sin would not take away my trust... Do not forget to tell the story of the sinner above all! It is that which will prove I am not mistaken." Mère Agnès told her she feared for her "the anguish of death". She answered that in those who had died before her eyes she had never seen the terrible sufferings that show as frightening signs; "Mère Geneviève had them for the soul, but not for the body."
- Cards: F-OW-530
- Best details:
  - « Le péché mortel ne m'enlèverait pas la confiance. » (F-OW-530).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 20.7.2-4). High. The "story of the sinner" is the parable of the sinner who is forgiven (the notebook does not say which story). Novissima Verba has "mortal sin".
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-196 — 21 July 1897, infirmary — "If God scolds me a little I will not weep; if he does not scold at all, I will"; Pilate; the instrument
- What happened: "If the good God scolds me, even a very little, I will not weep from tenderness... but if he does not scold at all, if he receives me with a smile, I will weep..." "Oh! I would like to know in Heaven the history of all the saints; but no one must tell it to me, because it would take too long. On meeting a saint I will have to know his name and his whole life at one glance." "I never acted like Pilate, who refused to hear the truth. I always said to God: O my God, I really want to hear you; I beg you, answer me when I humbly say: What is truth? Let me see things as they are; let nothing throw dust in my eyes." When the Sisters said she was fortunate to be chosen by God to show souls the way of trust: "What does it matter to me whether it is I or another who gives this way to souls; so long as it is shown, what does the instrument matter!"
- Cards: F-OW-532
- Best details:
  - « Qu'est-ce que cela me fait que ce soit moi ou une autre qui donne cette voie aux âmes ; pourvu qu'elle soit montrée, qu'importe l'instrument ! » (F-OW-532).
  - "if he does not scold me at all... I will weep" (F-OW-532; no "scold" in Novissima Verba).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 21.7.2-5). High. Novissima Verba has the Pilate and "instrument" sayings.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial for the whole; yes for "if he does not scold me at all"
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-197 — 22 July 1897, infirmary — "That is not what attracts me: it is love"
- What happened: On 22 July 1897, as Céline read her a passage on the happiness of heaven, she interrupted: that is not what draws her. Céline asked what, then. She said: love, to love, be loved, and come back to earth to make Love loved. Céline also heard her tell Agnès (17 July) that she wanted to spend her heaven doing good on earth. Céline says her desire for heaven was calm, tempered by the trial of faith, "those terrible doubts about the existence of another life". Céline wrote to her aunt Mme Guérin on 22 Jul 1897 with the same saying: "Oh! it's Love! To love, to be loved, and to come back to earth." Also "In Heaven you'll sit next to me!" (21 Jul), and she would fasten Céline's "azure wings" herself, "because you'd put them on too low or too high!"
- Cards: F-EW-1479, F-EW-1480, F-OW-265
- Best details:
  - « aimer, être aimée, et revenir sur la terre, pour faire aimer l'Amour » (F-EW-1479).
  - "those terrible doubts about the existence of another life" (F-EW-1480).
  - "azure wings" fastened by Thérèse herself (F-OW-265).
- Evidence: Céline (heard, present; 1910, 1915). High. | Céline (dated letter of 22 Jul 1897). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-198 — 22 July 1897 (feast of St Mary Magdalene), Carmel of Lisieux — "Let the just man strike me": LT 259 to Céline
- What happened: On 22 Jul 1897 she copies for Céline a verse from Psalm 140 as a prayer: "Let the just man strike me out of compassion for sinners; let the oil with which one perfumes the head not soften mine." She explains: "I can be broken and tried only by the just, since all my Sisters are pleasing to God. It is less bitter to be broken by a sinner than by a just man, but out of compassion for sinners, to obtain their conversion, I ask you, my God, to be broken for them by the just souls around me." She also asks that "the oil of praise, so sweet to nature, not soften my head, that is my mind, by making me think I have virtues I have hardly practised several times". LT 256 (16 (?) Jul): to Sr Marthe, "your little twin", she says she forgot her birthday and will give her "the prayer on humility". LT 260 (24-25 (?) Jul): to the Guérins, she sends flowers (thoughts for her uncle and aunt, two rose buds for Jeanne and Francis, a lone bud for Léonie) with "the fruits of the Holy Spirit, particularly that of Joy".
- Cards: F-OW-511
- Best details:
  - « Que le juste me brise par compassion pour les pécheurs, que l'huile dont on parfume la tête n'amollisse pas la mienne. » (F-OW-511).
  - « Votre petite jumelle qui ne pourrait dormir si elle ne vous envoyait ce petit mot. » (F-OW-511).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 259, 256, 260). High. The 1911 edition prints the Psalm-140 note in Céline's testimony; the explanation of "broken by the just", LT 256 and LT 260 are absent.
- Conflicts: "Petite jumelle" (little twin) is her name for Sr Marthe; the note does not explain it. The "prayer on humility" is not identified in the note (Pri 20; see E-P6-172).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-199 — 22 July 1897, infirmary — Her nursing as "a flash in my darkness"; "if the angels swept Heaven, the dust would be diamonds"
- What happened: When Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur said "Go on! you are cared for with much love...", Thérèse answered: "Yes, I see it well... It is an image of the love that the good God has for me. I only ever gave him love, so he gives me back love, and it is not finished, he will give me more soon. I am much touched, it is like a ray or rather a flash in the middle of my darkness... but only like a flash!" Marie also recorded the words of M. Youf after her confession: "If the angels swept Heaven, the dust would be made of diamonds."
- Cards: F-OW-533
- Best details:
  - « c'est comme un rayon ou plutôt un éclair au milieu de mes ténèbres... mais seulement comme un éclair ! » (F-OW-533).
  - « Si les anges balayaient le Ciel, la poussière serait faite de diamants. » (F-OW-533), attributed to Fr Youf.
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 22.7.1-2). High. "Diamonds" and "flash" not found in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-200 — 23 July 1897, infirmary — "So near Heaven, all this seems sad"; "It is as if you were singing!"
- What happened: On 23 July she was told of associations (pious societies): "I am so near Heaven that all this seems sad to me." When a Sister who thought she had consoled her in her "great trial" by reading to her asked "Isn't it true your trial has stopped for a moment?", Thérèse answered: "No! It is as if you were singing!" Later, when Mère Agnès told her again of her fear that she would suffer still more, she said: "We who run in the way of Love, I find we must not think of what painful things may happen to us in the future, for that is a lack of trust and it is like meddling in creation."
- Cards: F-OW-534
- Best details:
  - « Non ! C'est comme si vous chantiez ! » (F-OW-534).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 23.7.1-3). High. A dated record that on 23 July the trial of faith had not lifted even when a Sister read to her.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes ("It is as if you were singing")
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-201 — told 23 July 1897; event 1888-89 — "I feel I can still suffer more": Sr Marie des Anges remembers her as a postulant
- What happened: She said: "At the time of Papa's trials I had a violent desire for suffering... One evening when I knew he was worse, Sr Marie des Anges, seeing me very sad, consoled me as best she could; but I said to her: 'O my Sr Marie des Anges, I feel I can still suffer more!' She looked at me astonished and often reminded me of it later." Mère Agnès adds Sr Marie des Anges's own account: the little saint, still a postulant, was about to go to bed, sitting on her straw mattress in her nightdress with her beautiful hair on her shoulders; "her look and her whole person had something so noble, so beautiful, that I thought I saw a virgin from Heaven."
- Cards: F-OW-535
- Best details:
  - « O ma Sr M. des Anges, je sens que je puis encore souffrir davantage! » (F-OW-535).
  - The postulant in her nightdress on her straw mattress with her hair down (F-OW-535).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 23.7.4), with a second testimony from Sr Marie des Anges (later account, not written in 1897). High for the sayings.
- Conflicts: none. A rare physical picture of the postulant (1888-89).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-202 — told 23 July 1897; events 1889-90 — Mme Swetchine on "union and unity"; the prayer for her father's cure on the day of her profession
- What happened: She recalled a day at the height of the family's troubles when she met Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur after sweeping the dormitory stairs. She told her she was "so possessed" by a saying of Mme Swetchine that she was "as if on fire": "Resignation is still distinct from the will of God; there is the same difference as between union and unity. In union one is still two; in unity one is more than one." She adds "I do not know if this is quite textual". Then: "They obliged me to ask for papa's cure on the day of my profession; but it was impossible for me to say anything but: My God, I beg you, may it be your will that papa be cured!" And: "'In te Domine speravi!' At the time of our great pains, how happy I was to say this verse in the choir!"
- Cards: F-OW-536
- Best details:
  - « La résignation est encore distincte de la volonté du bon Dieu; il y a la même différence qu'entre l'union et l'unité. » (F-OW-536).
  - « Mon Dieu, je vous en supplie, que ce soit votre volonté que papa guérisse ! » (F-OW-536).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 23.7.5-7). High; she says the quotation may not be word for word. The Swetchine sentence is in the 1911 edition in one of her letters; the profession-day prayer was not checked.
- Conflicts: none. Shows her reading (Anne-Sophie Swetchine, named only as "Mme Swetchine"); at her profession (8 Sep 1890) the family wanted her to ask for her father's cure.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-203 — 24 July 1897, infirmary — Fruit for the Holy Family; milk with rum for St Joseph
- What happened: Sent beautiful fruit she could not eat, she took them one by one as if offering them. St Joseph and the Child Jesus each got a peach and two plums, and the Virgin got her share. When given milk with a little rum she offered it to St Joseph. In the refectory she used to give the sweet to the Child Jesus and the strong to St Joseph. The Carnet jaune (24 July) adds: "The Holy Family has been well served. St Joseph and the Child Jesus each had a peach and two plums." Half-voiced, she asked whether it was wrong that she touched them "with satisfaction": "It gives me much pleasure to touch the fruit, above all the peaches, and to see them close." In the refectory she always saw to whom she should give: the sweet was for the Child Jesus, the strong for St Joseph; when she lacked something, for example when they forgot to pass her the sauce or salad, she was happier because she was giving "for real". Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur once placed her plate of salad so close to Sr Marie de l'Incarnation that she could no longer regard it as hers and did not touch it. "And what a 'slipper' of an omelette they served me in my life! They thought I liked it all dried up. After my death, take care not to give this filth to the poor Sisters."
- Cards: F-EW-1481, F-OW-537
- Best details:
  - « Oh! que cela va faire de bien au pauvre Saint Joseph! » (F-EW-1481).
  - « quelle «savate» d'omelette on m'a servie dans ma vie ! On croyait que je l'aimais ainsi toute desséchée. » (F-OW-537).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 265v). High. Shows her playfulness and that she was given milk with rum. | Mère Agnès (CJ 24.7.1-2). High. Novissima Verba has the peaches-and-plums passage; the omelette remark is not there.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: fruit shared out to the Holy Family.

### E-P6-204 — 23-25 July 1897 — The last letter to the Guérins and Uncle Isidore's tears
- What happened: Mme Guérin wondered how Thérèse, so weak, could write such a fine letter with such well-formed handwriting. The family kept it. Isidore Guérin's reply of 25 July calls it perhaps "the last song" of the swan and calls her a "poor little blue bird". He signs as her "second father". He wrote that the letter brought tears to his eyes: pride in his adoptive daughter, admiration, and sadness at a separation. He called her the late-born "little pearl" of her mother and "little queen" of her father. Mme Guérin's letter says he could not hold back tears at the passage on her entry into heaven. Isidore Guérin's letter of 24 July from La Musse, to his daughter Marie (per Piat): "You were the little pearl, late come, of your good Mother; you were the Little Queen of your old father, and you are the most beautiful flower of this crown of lilies that surrounds me." Laveille gives Isidore's reply of 25 July to Thérèse: "You were your good mother's precious pearl, last in coming, your venerated father's 'little queen'… It is said that the swan, though silent during its whole life, breaks into a sublime song as death approaches. Your letter… is, without doubt, your last song for us… He who has perhaps some small claim to be called your second father embraces you with deep affection."
- Cards: F-EW-1482, F-EW-1485, F-EW-2630, F-LF-234
- Best details:
  - « mais encore l'écriture très bien formée » (F-EW-1482).
  - "poor little blue bird"; "little pearl" and "little queen" (F-EW-1482, F-EW-1485).
  - The swan's last song (F-LF-234).
- Evidence: Letters of Mme Guérin (24 July 1897) and Isidore Guérin (25 July 1897). High. | Piat (1967); Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 961). High for the existence of the letters.
- Conflicts: Piat says "little pearl" and "Little Queen" in the 24 July letter are Marie (his pet name for her), and that Thérèse herself is called "Petite Reine" in the same bulletins. Laveille and F-EW-1485 apply the words to Thérèse (Louis Martin's "little queen"). The passages may be one letter or two.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-205 — 25 July 1897, infirmary — Uncle Guérin's grapes: "I don't like what comes from my family"
- What happened: Her uncle Isidore Guérin sent grapes. She ate some and said they were good, but she did not like what came from her family. Formerly she refused bouquets he sent for her Child Jesus statue until sure the prioress had approved. The Carnet jaune (25 July) adds: she ate a little and said: "How good this grape is! But I do not like what comes to me from my family... In the past, when they brought me bouquets for my little Jesus from them, I would never take them without being sure that Our Mother had said so." She also confessed some small things that she blamed herself for; Mère Agnès answered these were not sins. Thérèse said: "When I heard you, I remembered Father Alexis: your words penetrated as deep into my heart," and she wept; Mère Agnès wiped her tears with a fine cloth ("Sr Geneviève keeps this relic"). "Never throw faded little flowers, only fresh-opened ones." "No boys' games! No little girls' games either; only games of little angels." She looked at a grape: "It is pretty, it looks good; then I eat one; that one I do not give to the little Jesus, he gives it to me."
- Cards: F-EW-1483, F-OW-539
- Best details:
  - "I don't like what comes from my family" (F-EW-1483).
  - « Pas de jeux de garçons !.. Pas de jeux de petites filles non plus ; seulement des jeux de petits anges. » (F-OW-539).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 266r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 25.7.3, 7-10). High. The tears kept as a relic are Mère Agnès's note: the recorder is already making relics.
- Conflicts: Compare F-EW-1273 (no costly remedies from her family). | In the Carnet jaune Céline (Sr Geneviève) keeps the tear-cloth; in Pauline's 1915 testimony Pauline herself collected the tears (F-EW-1238). "Père Alexis" is not identified in the notebook.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-206 — 25 July 1897, infirmary window — The golden sunset
- What happened: On the evening of 25 July 1897, leaning a little, she saw through the window the setting sun gilding the tops of the trees. She told Marie that is why she seems all golden: she stands in the sun of Love, and would stop being so at once if she left it.
- Cards: F-EW-1484
- Best details:
  - « C'est pour cela que je parais toute dorée; en réalité je ne le suis pas » (F-EW-1484).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-207 — 25 July 1897, infirmary — "It is the face I kiss"; "The Thief is in my heart"; the Virgin with her dead Jesus
- What happened: On 25 July Mère Agnès held the crucifix out to be kissed as usual. Thérèse said: "But I kiss the face!" Looking at the picture of the Child Jesus that Sr Marie de la Trinité brought from the Carmel of Messina, she said: "This little Jesus seems to say to me: 'You will come to Heaven, I tell you so!'" When Mère Agnès asked "Where is the Thief now? no one speaks of him", she put her hand on her heart: "He is there! He is in my heart." When Mère Agnès said death was sad to look at and that she would have great pain seeing her dead, she answered in a moved voice: "The Blessed Virgin held her dead Jesus on her knees, disfigured, bloody! It was something else than what you will see! Ah! I do not know how she did it!... Suppose they bring me back to you in this state, what would become of you? Responde mihi?..."
- Cards: F-OW-538
- Best details:
  - « La Sainte Vierge a bien tenu son Jésus mort sur ses genoux, défiguré, sanglant ! C'était autre chose que ce que vous verrez ! » (F-OW-538).
  - « Il est là ! Il est dans mon coeur. » (F-OW-538).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 25.7.4-6). High. No match in Novissima Verba. "Responde mihi" is Latin for "answer me"; she leaves the question open.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-208 — told 25 July 1897; event after her clothing (1889) — "Fat face" and "wasting away": two opposite opinions on the same novice
- What happened: In a story she told on 25 July, Thérèse said: "One day, after my clothing, Sr Saint-Vincent-de-Paul saw me at Our Mother's and cried: 'Oh, what a face of prosperity! How strong she is, this big girl! how fat she is!' I was going off quite humbled by the compliment when Sr Madeleine stopped me in front of the kitchen and said: 'What is becoming of you, poor little Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus! You are getting thin before our eyes! If you go on at this rate, with that face that makes one tremble, you will not follow the rule for long!' I could not get over hearing one after the other two such opposite opinions. From that moment I never gave importance to the opinion of creatures, and this has so grown that now blame and compliments glide off me without leaving the slightest trace."
- Cards: F-OW-540
- Best details:
  - « Oh! quelle figure de prospérité! Est-elle forte cette grande fille! est-elle grosse! » (F-OW-540).
  - « Vous maigrissez à vue d'oeil! Si vous y allez de ce train là, avec cette mine qui fait trembler, vous ne suivrez pas longtemps la règle! » (F-OW-540).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 25.7.15). High. Neither remark is in the 1911 edition. One of the few outside remarks about her looks in the novitiate.
- Conflicts: Sr Saint-Vincent-de-Paul, who here calls her "fat", is the Sister who later called her "not even a good religious" and who was cured at her feet (see E-P6-395).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-209 — 25 July 1897, infirmary — On spiritual direction: "the guards took my cloak"
- What happened: About spiritual directors she said: "I think we must take care not to seek ourselves, for then the heart is wounded afterwards and one could say with truth: 'The guards took my cloak, they wounded me... it was only after passing them a little that I found my Beloved.'" She thought that if the soul had humbly asked the guards where her Beloved was, "they would have told her where he was, but wanting to be admired she fell into trouble; she lost simplicity of heart."
- Cards: F-OW-541
- Best details:
  - « Les gardes m'ont enlevé mon manteau, ils m'ont blessée... ce n'est qu'après les avoir dépassés un peu que j'ai trouvé mon Bien-Aimé » (F-OW-541).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 25.7.13). High. The text is from the Song of Songs (5:7), which she does not cite in the notebook. Her only recorded remark in these pages on directors.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-210 — 26 July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux — LT 261 to Bellière: "the onions of Egypt" and the hidden conversation in Heaven
- What happened: Bellière wrote that he is resigned to losing her "presence, sensible action". Thérèse promises: "after my departure for eternal life I will let you taste what happiness there is in feeling a friendly soul near you. It will not be this more or less distant correspondence, always incomplete, that you seem to regret, but a fraternal conversation that will charm the angels, a conversation creatures cannot blame since it will be hidden from them." She jokes: "How good it will seem to be freed from this mortal shell, which, if by impossible I found myself with several people in the presence of my dear little brother, would oblige me to look at him as a stranger, an indifferent!" She tells him not to imitate the Hebrews who "regretted the onions of Egypt" ("I have served you those vegetables that make you cry when you bring them near your eyes uncooked"). She tells him she will not need "to put my hand on Jesus's mouth"; he "has long since forgotten your unfaithfulnesses". Jesus is more saddened by the many small "indelicacies" of his friends than by the grave faults of people of the world, but only when the friends make a habit of them and do not ask forgiveness does Jesus say "These wounds I received in the house of those who loved me".
- Cards: F-OW-512
- Best details:
  - « un entretien fraternel qui charmera les anges, un entretien que les créatures ne pourront blâmer puisqu'il leur sera caché » (F-OW-512).
  - « je n'aurai pas besoin de «mettre la main sur la bouche de Jésus» ! Il a depuis longtemps oublié vos infidélités » (F-OW-512).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 261). High. "Oignons d'Egypte" and "main sur la bouche de Jésus" are absent from the 1911 edition; the wounds passage is known.
- Conflicts: The letter says Bellière called his sin-list "an indiscretion" and that she had "served" him "onions" (her earlier severe letters).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-211 — 26 July 1897 (about events of the 1860s-1894), Carmel of Lisieux — Her own account of her family to Bellière: four little angels, "three times up the mountain of Carmel"
- What happened: Bellière asked whether in the world his "sister" was called Geneviève (he thought she was Céline). Thérèse gives "details on my family". "The good God gave me a father and a mother more worthy of Heaven than of earth. They asked the Lord to give them many children and take them for himself. This desire was granted: four little angels flew to Heaven, and the 5 children left in the arena took Jesus as spouse." Her father, "like a new Abraham, climbed three times the mountain of Carmel to sacrifice to God what he had dearest". First his two elders; then "the third of his daughters, on the advice of her director and led by our incomparable father, tried a convent of the Visitation" (she came back and "lives in the world as in the cloister"). "Only two children were left, one aged 18, the other 14." The younger, "little Thérèse", asked to fly to Carmel, which "she obtained without difficulty from her good Father, who went as far as taking her first to Bayeux, then to Rome to remove the obstacles that delayed the immolation of her whom he called his queen". At the port he said to the last child: "If you want to follow your sisters' example, I consent; do not worry about me." She signs "not Geneviève but Thérèse of the Child Jesus of the Holy Face".
- Cards: F-OW-513
- Best details:
  - « ils demandèrent au Seigneur de leur donner beaucoup d'enfants et de les prendre pour Lui. Ce désir fut exaucé, quatre petits anges s'envolèrent aux Cieux, et les 5 enfants restées dans l'arène prirent Jésus pour époux » (F-OW-513).
  - « mon père, comme un nouvel Abraham, gravit trois fois la montagne du Carmel » (F-OW-513).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 261). High for the words; a pious retelling in her last summer, not a neutral record. Absent from the 1911 letters.
- Conflicts: The letter says the third daughter (Léonie) "tried" the Visitation and later came back to the world (other cards record that Léonie left the Visitation in July 1895).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-212 — told 26 July 1897; events 1888-1892 — Her father's illness, told to Bellière: "he did not want his healing asked for"
- What happened: In the same letter she says a short time after she left home the father "was seized by an attack of paralysis in the legs, which returned several times, but it could not settle there, the trial would have been too sweet, for the heroic patriarch had offered himself to God as a victim; so the paralysis, changing its course, settled in the venerable head of the victim whom the Lord had accepted". "We had to drink the chalice to the dregs and be separated for three years from our venerated father, entrusting him to religious but foreign hands." "He accepted this trial of which he understood all the humiliation, and carried heroism to the point of not wanting his healing to be asked for." She apologises: "I hope to write you again if the trembling of my hand does not increase, for I had to write my letter in several sittings."
- Cards: F-OW-514
- Best details:
  - « poussa l'héroïsme jusqu'à ne pas vouloir qu'on demandât sa guérison » (F-OW-514).
  - The letter written "in several sittings" with a trembling hand (F-OW-514).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 261). High for her words; her interpretation of his illness, not a medical record. Details ("paralysis in the legs, then head", "he did not want his healing asked for") absent from the 1911 letters.
- Conflicts: She says three years apart (1889-1892); other cards give the Bon-Sauveur dates.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-213 — 26 July 1897, infirmary — A dream of a bazaar with her father; the dated timeline of her charge of the novices
- What happened: On 26 July she said: "I dreamed last night that I was in a bazaar with Papa; I saw pretty little white pincushions that tempted me to put my pins in; but in the end I told myself that they make the same at the Carmel, and I asked for a little music." Later she told Mère Agnès the timeline of her charge of the novices: "Around 8 Dec 1892 she had taken care of Sr Marthe; in 1893 she had helped Mère Marie de Gonzague at the novitiate, and at the last election in 1896 she found herself, so to speak, entirely charged with the novices." And: "Virtue shines naturally; as soon as it is not there, I see it."
- Cards: F-OW-542
- Best details:
  - « vers le 8 Déc.1892 elle s'était occupée de Sr Marthe ; qu'en 1893 elle avait aidé Mère Marie de Gonzague au noviciat et qu'à la dernière élection en 1896 elle s'était vue pour ainsi dire chargée complètement des novices. » (F-OW-542).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 26.7.1-3), the timeline in indirect speech. High. The only dated list in the notebook of when she took on the novices.
- Conflicts: Compare Ms C on her role and Sr Marthe's entry.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-214 — undated; DE 27 July 1897 — The small cross with spikes: "never launch on that road"
- What happened: Once she wore a small cross with points too long. It caused a wound that became infected, and she had to be treated. She said great penances were not for her, since God let her fall ill for so little. Natural satisfaction can mix with the harshest penance. She told Pauline never to launch into that way. The Carnet jaune (27 July) tells the iron cross story in Mère Agnès's words: "She told me how she wore her little iron cross for a long time and that she was made ill by it."
- Cards: F-EW-1486, F-OW-547
- Best details:
  - « ne vous lancez jamais dans cette voie, ce n'est pas celle des toutes petites âmes » (F-EW-1486).
  - « Elle me raconta comment elle avait porté longtemps sa petite croix de fer et qu'elle en avait été malade. » (F-OW-547).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1910). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-215 — 27 July 1897 (evening), infirmary — "Break the web of this sweet encounter"
- What happened: On the evening of 27 July, Thérèse reminded Mère Agnès of a saying of St John of the Cross: "Break the web of this sweet meeting." She said: "I have always applied this saying to the death of love that I desire. Love will not wear out the web of my life, it will break it suddenly." She added other words of St John of the Cross that she had repeated "from the beginning of my religious life": "It is most important that the soul practise Love greatly so that, being consumed quickly, it stays hardly at all here below but arrives quickly to see its God face to face." Repeating these last words "she raised her finger and took a heavenly air".
- Cards: F-OW-543
- Best details:
  - « L'amour n'usera pas la toile de ma vie, il la rompra tout à coup. » (F-OW-543).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7.5). High. Novissima Verba has the "web" saying; the second saying is on her June 1897 farewell card (LT 245). The raised finger is Mère Agnès's note.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-216 — 27 July 1897, infirmary — The doctor's drops, a dream between torches, and the laundry prayer
- What happened: On 27 July, Thérèse told Mère Agnès not to forget the drops of a medicine: "Oh! you must build yourself up; 30 drops this evening, do not forget!" When asked "Do we tire you?" she answered "No, because you are very nice people of the world." She told with a laugh that she had dreamed she was carried to the warming room between two torches for Our Father's feast. And on the day the community was at the laundry: "Towards one o'clock I said to myself: they are very tired at the wash! and I prayed to God that he relieve you all, that the work be done in peace and charity. When I saw myself so ill, I felt joy at having to suffer like you."
- Cards: F-OW-545
- Best details:
  - « Quand je me suis vue si malade, j'ai éprouvé de la joie d'avoir à souffrir comme vous. » (F-OW-545).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7.1-4). High. The "chauffoir" is the Carmel's heated room; "Notre Père" is not identified in the notebook.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-217 — 27 July 1897, infirmary — Baby talk with her sisters: "Little Mother is my telephone"
- What happened: On 27 July she said "I shall soon no longer see your dear little face! I shall see only your little soul." – "It is much more beautiful!" To those who said "when one thinks we are going to lose you!", she answered "But you will not lose me... point fine!..." To Sr Geneviève who was crying she said in a mock-village voice: "E' voit bien qu'c'est ça qui lui pend au bout du nez (la mort) la v'la saisie d'peur maintenant!" Of Mère Agnès: "Little Mother is my telephone; I have only to stretch my little ear when she comes and I know everything!" And after offering a bunch of grapes to the Child Jesus: "I offered him this bunch to make him want to take me, because I believe I am of that kind..." Tasting a grape: "Yes, I am of that kind."
- Cards: F-OW-546
- Best details:
  - « La petite Mère c'est mon téléphone ; je n'ai qu'à tendre ma petite oreille quand é vient, et j'sais tout ! » (F-OW-546).
  - « Je lui ai offert cette grappe là pour lui donner envie de me prendre, parce que je crois que je suis de cette espèce là... » (F-OW-546).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7.7-11). High. The notebook keeps her baby talk and village accent. A telephone in 1897 was a rare object. No match in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-218 — 27 July 1897, infirmary — "I am not selfish"; the iron cross; "curried like a horse"; "from 9 June I was sure I would die soon"
- What happened: A group of sayings of 27 July: "I am not selfish, it is God I love, not myself." "For nature I would rather die, but I rejoice at death only because it is God's will for me." "I never asked God to die young, so I am sure that at this moment he is only accomplishing his will." When she was suffocating and Mère Agnès showed compassion: "Do not be sad, go on! If I suffocate, God will give me strength. I love him! He will never abandon me." She told how she wore "her little iron cross" for a long time and was made ill by it: "it was not God's will for me or for us to throw ourselves into great mortifications; this proved it to me." About the E-P6-121 ordered by the doctor: "Ah! to be 'curried' as I have been is much worse than anything!" And: "From 9 June I was sure I would die soon."
- Cards: F-OW-547
- Best details:
  - « Ah ! d'être «étrillée» comme je l'ai été, c'est bien pire que n'importe quoi ! » (F-OW-547).
  - « A partir du 9 juin, j'ai été sûre de mourir bientôt. » (F-OW-547).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 27.7.12-18). High. The 1911 edition tells of the iron cross that made her ill; "from 9 June I was sure" is not in Novissima Verba. "From 9 June" links to LT 244 and the E-P6-069 saying.
- Conflicts: The iron cross is a penitential object; the notebook does not date it. DE 27 July places the spiked cross saying on the same day.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-219 — c. late July 1897, Lisieux Carmel — "I had a difficult nature... not one day without suffering"
- What happened: TSA heard a Sister say that Thérèse had no merit in practising virtue because she had never had struggles. In 1915 she adds that people "did not fear to say it loudly". About two months before Thérèse's death, TSA asked her directly. Thérèse said: "Oh, I have had them! I had a nature that was not easy; it did not show, but I felt it. Not one day without suffering, not one!" In 1915 TSA adds: "Ah, creatures! when they do not see, they do not believe!" TSA's own record of the exchange: "Tell me if you have had struggles." "Oh! have I had them! I had a nature that was not easy; it didn't show, but I felt it; I can assure you I haven't been one single day without suffering, not one." "But people say you had none." "Ah! the judgments of creatures. Because they don't see, they don't believe." About the doctor: "Today he says I'm lost, tomorrow that I'm better... all that doesn't touch my soul."
- Cards: F-EW-1433, F-OW-221
- Best details:
  - « J'avais une nature pas commode; cela ne paraissait pas; mais moi, je le sentais bien » (F-EW-1433).
  - "Ah, creatures! when they do not see, they do not believe!" (F-EW-1433).
  - « J'avais une nature pas commode, cela ne paraissait pas mais moi je le sentais bien » (F-OW-221).
- Evidence: TSA (heard both the critic and Thérèse; 1911, 1915). High. Type of criticism: belittling judgement, because her constant peace made people think virtue cost her nothing. | TSA (recorder; Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs nn. 3-4). Medium.
- Conflicts: Links with Léonie's remark that Thérèse wrote her nature was proud (F-EW-073). Compare the recreation remark reported on 29 July (F-EW-1228).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the hidden temper, in her own words.

### E-P6-220 — 28 July 1897, infirmary — "How badly one arranges one's little business on earth!"
- What happened: Marie often spent recreation in the infirmary. She told Thérèse that losing recreation would cost her with another patient but was a comfort with her. Thérèse answered that she would have been very happy with a less likeable patient, because one practises more charity that way.
- Cards: F-EW-1487
- Best details:
  - « Ah! qu'on sait mal arranger sa petite affaire sur la terre! » (F-EW-1487).
- Evidence: Marie (heard, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-221 — 29 July 1897 (DE) — "What can you say about someone who has been pampered?"
- What happened: A lay Sister said during Thérèse's illness that she wondered what the prioress could write in Thérèse's circular. Thérèse had been pampered and had not gained virtue through struggle like the rest. She was sweet and good, but it was natural. Thérèse overheard it and told Pauline. This Sister, now dead in 1910, at other times said Thérèse was a saint. TSA says Thérèse's constant peace earned her unfavourable judgments. Some Sisters said aloud that since she never had inner combats, she had no merit in practising virtue. The Carnet jaune (29 July) gives the remark in Mère Agnès's words: "Why do they talk of Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus as a saint?! She practised virtue, it is true, but it was not a virtue acquired by humiliations, and above all by sufferings." Thérèse: "And I who suffered so much from my earliest childhood! Ah! how good it does me to see the opinion of creatures at the moment of death!" Earlier that day: "I would like to go away!" – "Where?" – "Up there, in the blue Heaven!"
- Cards: F-EW-1228, F-EW-1335, F-OW-551
- Best details:
  - « Que voulez-vous dire d'une personne qui a été tout le temps choyée » (F-EW-1228).
  - Thérèse's reply in the NV version (29 July): "And I who have suffered so much since my earliest childhood!" (F-EW-1228 notes).
  - "she had no struggles, so no merit" (F-EW-1335); Thérèse's answer is in I-Q10.
  - « Et moi qui ai tant souffert dès ma plus tendre enfance ! Ah ! que cela me fait de bien de voir l'opinion des créatures au moment de la mort ! » (F-OW-551).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard from Thérèse, who heard it; 1910; NV f. 267v). High. | TSA (heard the talk; Carmel archive notes, section FORCE). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 29.7.1-2). High. Novissima Verba has a similar passage on "acquired virtue/humiliations".
- Conflicts: Process 1910 says Thérèse overheard and told Pauline; NV (29 July) says the remark was made at recreation and reported by a Sister. Whether this is the same Sister as in the kitchen remark is not stated. | The Carnet jaune does not name the Sister who made the remark (an example of dissent in the community).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-222 — 29 July 1897, infirmary — The child's toy and the tearful apology: "How imperfect I am!"
- What happened: On 29 July 1897, very ill in the infirmary, a Sister brought her a small child's toy to amuse her. Surprised, Thérèse accepted it without enthusiasm, saying "What do you want me to do with that?" The Sister, a little hurt, told her she was not being gracious. Thérèse answered: "You are right, oh how imperfect I am!... but I am happy to feel so wretched." Despite exhaustion she then welcomed all visitors with a smile. Pauline says she saw her ask pardon of a Sister with tears and a kind of holy vehemence that day. Soon her face was peaceful again, and she said she felt great joy to be found imperfect and to feel it herself. Céline says she was glad when her imperfections became known: "it was her gain". The Carnet jaune (29 July) gives the ungracious moment: a Sister thought to please her by bringing her an object; "the opposite happened". Thérèse showed displeasure, guessing that somebody had been deprived of the thing, but soon repented and asked pardon with tears: "Oh! I beg your pardon, I acted by nature; pray for me!" A little later: "Oh! how happy I am to see myself imperfect and to have so much need of the good God's mercy at the moment of death!"
- Cards: F-EW-1489, F-EW-1488, F-EW-1490, F-OW-552
- Best details:
  - « Que voulez-vous que je fasse de cela? » (F-EW-1489).
  - "great joy to be found imperfect and to feel it herself" (F-EW-1488, F-EW-1490).
  - « Oh ! je vous demande bien pardon, j'ai agi par nature, priez pour moi ! » (F-OW-552).
- Evidence: Marie (1915); Mère Agnès (saw, 1915); Céline (present, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 29.7.3). High. Like 28 May (LT 230), a scene where she loses patience and turns it to trust.
- Conflicts: The Sister and the cause of the apology are not named (F-EW-1488). F-EW-1490 links to Marie's toy episode, same date. | The object and the giver are not named. Marie's card (F-EW-1489) says a "small child's toy" and quotes "What do you want me to do with that?"
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: scene S-EW-033, a rare case of small ungraciousness caught out.

### E-P6-223 — 29 July 1897, infirmary — A feast-day picture on the eve of Extreme Unction
- What happened: Though very ill, Thérèse did not forget Marthe's feast on 29 July 1897 (St Martha), the day before she received Extreme Unction. She had a small picture with a note in her own hand sent to her.
- Cards: F-EW-1491, F-EW-1492
- Best details:
  - Note in her own hand on a small picture (F-EW-1491).
- Evidence: Marthe (recipient; 1916; notes). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-224 — 29 July 1897, infirmary — Blood at 3 p.m.; "not die at night"; "the novices exhaust me like preachers"; "to be my historian, spare yourself"
- What happened: On 29 July she had spat blood in the morning and at 3 p.m. She was told they feared she would die at night: "I will not die at night, believe me; I wished not to die at night." She said "Two days after Sr Marie de la Trinité's entry they treated my throat... The good God allowed the novices to exhaust me. Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie told me it happened to me as to preachers." To Mère Agnès: "To be my historian, you must spare yourself." And: "Well! 'baby' is going to die then! For 3 days, it is true, I have suffered a lot; tonight I am as if in purgatory." "Very often, when I can, I repeat my offering to Love." To a Sister who had sat her up: "I lean my head on my little Mother's heart."
- Cards: F-OW-553
- Best details:
  - « Pour être mon historien, il faut vous ménager. » (F-OW-553).
  - « Le bon Dieu a permis quel les novices m'épuisent. » (F-OW-553).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 29.7.4-11). High. Novissima Verba has "exhaust" and the offering sayings.
- Conflicts: The "novices exhausted me" remark links her throat to the novitiate: she says the treatment of her throat came two days after Sr Marie de la Trinité entered (16 Jun 1894 in other cards). "Historian" is Mère Agnès's job as first biographer.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-225 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — The flies: "my only enemies"
- What happened: Flies tormented her in her last illness but she would not let them be killed. Sisters wanted to kill them, and she always begged them to spare them. They were her only enemies, she was glad to forgive them, and God said to forgive one's enemies. A later dating and witness. On Sunday 26 Sep 1897, four days before her death, while the community was at Mass, Sr Marie-Élisabeth was told to watch Thérèse. Sure she was with a saint, she stayed the whole hour asking questions and admiring the answers. A painful suffocation crisis followed after she left. She never knew this and kept only a heavenly memory. She was struck that Thérèse would not let her try to kill the flies that troubled her in the heat: she begged her not to harm them because they were her only enemies and she was glad to have a chance to forgive. The Carnet jaune (30 July): the flies tormented her but she would not kill them: "I always spare them. Yet they alone gave me trouble in my illness. I have only them as enemies and since the good God recommended to forgive enemies, I am glad to find this small occasion." When a Sister wanted to kill one: "What are you going to do to it?" – "Kill it." – "Oh! no, please."
- Cards: F-EW-1232, F-EW-1235, F-EW-1281, F-EW-2561, F-OW-557
- Best details:
  - « Je n'ai qu'elles d'ennemies » (F-EW-1232).
  - Céline says she forgave them "to obey Our Lord" (F-EW-1281).
  - She stayed a whole hour asking questions, then a suffocation crisis followed (F-EW-2561).
  - "I have only them as enemies" (F-OW-557).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard, 1910 and 1915; NV 30 July); Céline (present). High. | Later Carmel writers (Agnès, 1935; Marie de la Rédemption, biography). Medium-high. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.7, 9, 15). High. Novissima Verba has the flies passage.
- Conflicts: Pauline's NV dates the flies saying to 30 July; this card dates a flies scene to 26 Sept.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium: humour and gentleness together.

### E-P6-226 — 30 July 1897 (about 6 p.m.); last confession 29 Sept 1897 — Extreme Unction and Viaticum; "this time I'm going to see my Jesus soon"
- What happened: When Maupas first saw her in her last illness she seemed joyful and radiant. He asked why. She said that this time she was soon going to see her Jesus. Canon Maupas gave her Extreme Unction and Viaticum on 30 July 1897 at about 6 p.m. Abbé Faucon heard her last confession on 29 Sept 1897. Her regular extraordinary confessions were at the Ember Days. Details of the anointing. The superior arrived at 6 o'clock, gave her Extreme Unction, then brought her Communion. She said death did not frighten her because she had only ever acted for God. Maupas told her that, if she received it well, her soul would be pure "as on the day of her baptism". Her sister told him these words gave her great joy. He later read in her Life that the words struck her and made her very happy. After the anointing she showed Pauline her hands with respect. She asked the community's pardon in such touching words that the Sisters could not hold back tears. Maupas's first visit was about two months before her death: he found her radiant and smiling, and his general impression was of childlike candour and simple surrender. Fuller accounts of 29-30 July. Marie's letter of 30 July describes an acute crisis with frequent haemorrhages, the back "burning like fire", and oppression so strong that even ether did not stop the suffocation. The day before, the Sisters had thought that Louis Martin would come for his "Little Queen" on the third anniversary of his death (29 Jul). On the doctor's advice Thérèse received Extreme Unction and Viaticum on the 30th at 6 p.m. Marie wrote: "It was touching to see our little patient always with her calm and pure look. When she asked pardon of the whole community, more than one melted into tears... It is impossible to imagine her happiness in dying; it is like a little child who desires with all her heart to go and find her Father... Why would death frighten me? I have never acted except for God." Laveille: she said "The door of my dark prison is half open. I am in great joy, especially since our Father Superior has told me that my soul today resembles that of a little child after baptism." Marie des Anges recalls that after the Anointing she said: "I have found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering, for I suffered much here below; you must tell the souls." Laveille says "a martyrdom of two long months was commencing". The Carnet jaune (30 July): she said to Mère Agnès: "Prepare me for Extreme Unction." With a smile: "I think of nothing! Pray the good God that I receive it as well as one can receive it." She told what "Our Father" (the priest) said to her before the ceremony: "'You will be like a little child who has just received baptism.' Then he spoke to me only of love. Oh! how touched I was!" She showed her hands respectfully after the anointing. In the afternoon, after a short thanksgiving, several Sisters came to talk to her. In the evening: "How people came to disturb me after Communion! They looked under my nose... but so as not to be irritated I thought of Our Lord who withdrew into solitude and could not prevent the crowd from following, and he did not send them away. I wanted to imitate him by receiving the Sisters well." The editors name the priest Canon Maupas. Dr de Cornière's swing: on 30 July "administer today", on 31 July "a month or more".
- Cards: F-EW-1328, F-EW-1329, F-EW-1500, F-EW-1501, F-EW-1503, F-EW-1504, F-EW-1505, F-EW-1435, F-EW-2631, F-LF-235, F-EW-2523, F-OW-559
- Best details:
  - « C'est que, cette fois, je vais bientôt aller voir mon Jésus. » (F-EW-1328).
  - "pure as on the day of your baptism" (F-EW-1504, F-EW-1505); Marie says the priest told her her soul was now like that of a child after baptism (F-EW-1501).
  - "the day of my death will be the greatest feast day for me" (F-EW-1503, letter of 31 July).
  - Her request for pardon made the Sisters weep (F-EW-1500).
  - Maupas says she died of a "tuberculous affection" in the infirmary on 30 September 1897 (F-EW-1435 notes).
  - « il faudra le dire aux âmes » (F-EW-2523).
  - "The door of my dark prison is half open." (F-LF-235).
  - The Sisters thought Louis Martin would come for her on the third anniversary of his death (F-EW-2631).
  - « Vous allez être comme un petit enfant qui vient de recevoir le baptême. » (F-OW-559).
- Evidence: Maupas (heard); Faucon; Marie de l'Eucharistie's letter. High. | Marie de l'Eucharistie (letter dated 31 July); Marie; Mère Agnès (present); Maupas (first-hand, Process 1911). High. | Marie's letter (via Piat); Laveille; Marie des Anges (1915, not the note-taker; the source is Pauline's notebook, "31-7"). Medium-high. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.16-18). High. LT 263 (10 Aug) reports the same day. Novissima Verba has the preparation and the baptism words.
- Conflicts: F-EW-1331 (Godefroy, hearsay) says the ecclesiastical superior gave the last sacraments; its notes say Extreme Unction was given by the chaplain Abbé Youf, and that daily communion stopped in August 1897. F-EW-1329 names Canon Maupas. | Laveille says "our Father Superior" without naming; the name of the superior in 1897 is Maupas (other cards), Delatroëtte having been superior in 1888-1892. Marie des Anges's reference "31-7" as printed against 30 Jul for the Anointing. | The notebook gives the priest as "Notre Père" (the Father Superior); the editors' summary names Maupas.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-227 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — After Viaticum the Sisters crowd in; the lip skin she swallowed
- What happened: On 30 July 1897, after Extreme Unction, she showed Pauline her hands with respect. On other days she let the Sisters take the bits of skin peeling from her fever-dried lips; that day she swallowed them. After Viaticum several Sisters came in to look at her and talk during her thanksgiving. She said Jesus did not send the crowds away either, so she had not sent them away. The Carnet jaune for 30 July says Mère Agnès used to collect the little dry skins of her lips; that day Thérèse said: "I swallow my little skins today because I have received Extreme Unction and holy Viaticum." Several Sisters came in during the thanksgiving.
- Cards: F-EW-1493, F-EW-1497, F-OW-559
- Best details:
  - She swallowed the bits of skin from her lips that day (F-EW-1493).
  - "Jesus did not send away the crowds who followed him into the desert" (F-EW-1497).
  - « J'avale aujourd'hui mes petites peaux parce que j'ai reçu l'Extrême Onction et le saint Viatique. » (F-OW-559).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; NV f. 269r; 1910, 1915). High. Implies the Sisters were already keeping relics while she lived. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.16-18). High. The skins are Mère Agnès's note that she used to gather them: the relic habit already in July 1897.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes (the lip-skin detail)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-228 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — Her straw mattress brought down: "ready for my corpse"
- What happened: Her death seemed so near that her straw mattress was carried down in advance, to lay her out on it after death. She saw it through an open door in the room next to the infirmary and cried out joyfully that it would be all ready for her corpse. The Carnet jaune dates the mattress scene to 31 July: on 31 July people supposed her death might fall on a feast day such as the Transfiguration (6 Aug) or the Assumption (15 Aug). She said: "Do not speak of a date, it will always be a feast!" The straw mattress had been brought down so that her body could be laid out. She saw it through the door and cried with joy: "Ah! There is our mattress! It will be quite ready to put my corpse on... My little nose has always had luck!"
- Cards: F-EW-1494, F-OW-560
- Best details:
  - « Ah! voilà notre paillasse! Elle va se trouver toute prête pour mettre mon cadavre! » (F-EW-1494).
  - « Ah ! Voilà notre paillasse ! Elle va se trouver toute prête pour mettre mon cadavre. » (F-OW-560).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 269r-v). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 31.7.1-4). High. Compare the exposure of the body after 30 Sept.
- Conflicts: 31 July (Carnet jaune) versus 30 July (Pauline's NV, F-EW-1494).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-229 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — "My body always bothered me"
- What happened: She said her body had always bothered her; she never felt at ease in it; as a very little girl she was ashamed of it. Céline says that in adolescence, in her own words, "she was ashamed of her body". Later she was consoled for having one only by thinking of Our Lord becoming man. The Carnet jaune (30 July) records the same saying: "My body has always been in my way; I did not feel at ease in it... even as a very small child I was ashamed of it." The notebook gives no reason for the shame.
- Cards: F-EW-1495, F-EW-1498, F-EW-1502, F-OW-555
- Best details:
  - « Toujours mon corps m'a gênée, je ne me trouvais pas à l'aise dedans... Toute petite, j'en avais honte. » (F-EW-1495).
  - « Toujours mon corps m'a gênée, je ne me trouvais pas à l'aise dedans... toute petite même, j'en avais honte. » (F-OW-555).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915); Céline (1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.1). High. Novissima Verba has it.
- Conflicts: 1910 has "as a very little girl", Céline says "in adolescence". Link with F-EW-453 (eating as "shameful").
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-230 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — The glass of bad medicine that looked like liqueur
- What happened: She showed a glass of very bad medicine that looked like a delicious liqueur. A Sister had said she hoped Thérèse was drinking good liqueur. Thérèse said the glass was the image of her life: to others she seemed to drink fine liqueurs, but it was bitterness, yet she had made joy of all bitterness. The Carnet jaune gives more of the glass saying: "This little glass is the image of my life. Yesterday Sr Thérèse of St Augustine said to me: 'I hope you are drinking good liqueur!' I answered: 'O my Sr Thérèse of St Aug., it is the worst I drink!' Well, my little Mother, that is what appeared to the eyes of creatures. It always seemed to them that I drank exquisite liqueurs and it was bitterness. I say bitterness, but no! for my life was not bitter, because I knew how to make my joy and sweetness of all bitterness." The glass held a very bad medicine that looked like a delicious currant liqueur.
- Cards: F-EW-1496, F-OW-557
- Best details:
  - "the image of her life": bitterness that looks like liqueur (F-EW-1496).
  - « Il leur a toujours semblé que je buvais des liqueurs exquises et c'était de l'amertume. Je dis, de l'amertume, mais non ! car ma vie n'a pas été amère, parce que j'ai su faire ma joie et ma douceur de toute amertume. » (F-OW-557).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 268v). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.7, 9, 15). High.
- Conflicts: The Sister who wished her "good liqueur" is named here as Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (said "yesterday", 29 July); the NV card (F-EW-1496) says only "a Sister". The glass is a real object.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-231 — 30 July 1897 — A painted image as thanks for Dr de Cornière
- What happened: Asked what memento to give Dr de Cornière after her death, she said to paint him an image with the words "What you did to the least of mine, you did to me". The Carnet jaune (30 July): she asked Mère Agnès to make Dr de Cornière a picture with the words "What you have done to the least of mine, you have done to me." M. de Cornière had said to give her 5 or 6 spoons of Tisserand water; she asked Sr Geneviève to give only 5, then, turning to Mère Agnès: "Always the least, isn't it, Mamma?"
- Cards: F-EW-1499, F-OW-558
- Best details:
  - "What you did to the least of mine, you did to me" (F-EW-1499).
  - « Toujours le moins, est-ce pas, maman ? » (F-OW-558).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910 and 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.10-13). High. The doctor's dose ("5 or 6 spoons of Tisserand water") is a treatment detail.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-232 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — The photographs of Bellière and Roulland: "I am nicer than they are"
- What happened: On 30 July, looking at the photograph of "the Fathers Bellière and Roulland", she said: "I am nicer than they are!" When they promised to buy her "little Chinese", she said: "It is not Chinese I want, it is negroes!" (the notebook's word). Novissima Verba, on another day, has her asking that the money spent on a gift be used "to ransom some poor little Negroes from slavery".
- Cards: F-OW-556
- Best details:
  - « Je suis plus gentille qu'eux ! » (F-OW-556).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7.4-5). High for the words. The word "nègres" is a term of 1897; the sense is children to adopt or buy (the "little Chinese" are toys or figurines: the notebook does not say). Handle the wording with care in the biography.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes ("I am nicer than they are"); partial for the "negroes" line
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-233 — 30 July 1897, infirmary — The fan from Saigon: "I fan the saints in my place"
- What happened: On 30 July she had been given a fan (an "écran") that came from the Carmel of Saigon; she used it to chase the flies. As it was very hot she turned toward the pictures pinned to the bed curtain and fanned them: "I fan the saints in my place; I fan you to do you good and because you are saints too." She said: "Do not tell M. Ducellier I have only a few days left; I am not yet weak to die, and after that when one lives one is quite 'capot' (fool)."
- Cards: F-OW-558
- Best details:
  - « J'évente les saints en place de moi ; je vous évente pour vous faire du bien et parce que vous êtes des saintes aussi. » (F-OW-558).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 30.7). High. Novissima Verba has the Saigon fan and the picture for Dr de Cornière. The word "capot" is left untranslated; its sense is uncertain. Abbé Ducellier is her confessor.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-234 — end of July 1897, worst of the illness, infirmary — The meat juice: "not even a good nun"
- What happened: At the worst of her illness a lay Sister offered her meat juice. Thérèse gently refused, saying it would make her vomit, and humbly asked pardon.
