- Evidence: Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline, 16 août). Medium-high. The 1911 edited text (HA ch. XII) prints a version as told to "l'infirmière".
- Conflicts: Céline's PA 1915 account (F-EW-1606) puts the devil's night "in late September"; Marie's holy-water scene is 28 Sept ("for another... and he does not want"); this card dates it 16 August and says the "other" is Céline herself.
- New to English readers: partial (in Taylor via HA)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-308 — Aug-Sept 1897, infirmary — A month without a doctor: who refused Dr La Néele?
- What happened: About a month before her death Thérèse went through a very painful crisis. The community doctor, Dr de Cornière, was on holiday. The Martin sisters asked the prioress to let Dr La Néele, their cousin by marriage, come in. Mère Marie de Gonzague refused, and for a month Thérèse suffered "the cruellest tortures", without relief. When her sisters complained, Thérèse told them not to murmur: it was God who allowed their Mother to give her no relief. Céline says that when the tuberculosis reached the intestines and caused gangrene, she was without doctors for a month. Céline later says Gonzague was jealous to see Thérèse in her family's hands.
- Cards: F-EW-1523, F-EW-1575, F-EW-1591, F-EW-1595, F-EW-1598, F-EW-1601
- Best details:
  - « Mes petites sœurs il ne faut pas murmurer contre la volonté du Bon Dieu » (F-EW-1523).
  - « C'est lui qui permet que notre mère ne me donne pas de soulagement. » (F-EW-1591).
  - Céline: "she was deprived of doctors for a month" (F-EW-1595).
  - Thérèse did not complain and checked their indignation (F-EW-1598).
- Evidence: Marie du Sacré-Cœur (participant in the request; notes); Marie (heard; 1910, 1915); Céline (present; 1910, 1915). High for the refusal as the Sisters experienced it; medium for the motive.
- Conflicts: Reason: none given (Marie, F-EW-1591); jealousy of the family (Céline, F-EW-1598). Marie des Anges instead blames the doctor for not thinking of palliatives (F-EW-1338). TSA says the prioress thought she should refuse the means to relieve her (F-EW-1612). The editors of Geneviève's deposition say claims about Gonzague should be checked against Guy Gaucher. Neither Marie nor Céline names Dr de Cornière. Pauline (F-EW-1514) says La Néele was let in three times.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: a month of pain, a refused doctor, and "do not murmur".

### E-P6-309 — 17-30 Aug 1897 — La Néele finds both lungs taken
- What happened: On 17 August Dr La Néele found both lungs affected and gave her only a few days to live. From 17 to 30 August she saw no doctor despite serious complications. Piat's account: on 15 Aug 1897 a violent left-side pain and swollen legs (heart failure signs) caused new fear. On 16 Aug Dr Francis La Néele, passing through Lisieux, was allowed to examine his young cousin. Marie wrote: "This visit moved him. He found Thérèse very ill and gives her hardly more than fifteen days... he said tuberculosis had reached its last degree... he found our little patient wonderfully cared for, and said that with all the care Dr de Cornière had given, if she had not recovered it was because God wanted to take her."
- Cards: F-EW-1573, F-EW-2633
- Best details:
  - "From 17 to 30 August she saw no doctor" (F-EW-1573).
  - "gives her hardly more than fifteen days" (F-EW-2633).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (1915). High. | Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Compare F-EW-1514 (La Néele let in only three times). | Date 16 Aug (Piat) versus 17 Aug (Pauline). Pauline says "only a few days" and that she saw no doctor 17-30 Aug; Piat has La Néele finding her "wonderfully cared for".
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-310 — 17 Aug 1897, infirmary — "I am at peace, that is why I am happy"
- What happened: On 17 Aug 1897 Thérèse told Marie Guérin that if told she would recover she would not be "caught out". She would be as content as to die. She was happy because she was in great peace, not from a rush of joy. Marie's letter (via Piat) records the same saying on 16 Aug: "If they told me I would get well, do not think I would be caught out. I would be as content as to die. I have a great desire for Heaven but above all I am happy because I am in great peace; to feel an immense joy as sometimes when the heart beats with happiness, oh no!... I am in peace; that is why I am happy."
- Cards: F-EW-1550, F-EW-2633
- Best details:
  - « je suis en paix, voilà pourquoi je suis heureuse » (F-EW-1550).
  - "to feel an immense joy... oh no!... I am in peace" (F-EW-2633). The card notes a quiet statement of what she felt, contradicting the picture of ecstatic joy.
- Evidence: Marie de l'Eucharistie (heard; letter). High. | Marie Guérin, letter (via Piat). High.
- Conflicts: 16 Aug (Piat) versus 17 Aug (letter cited in F-EW-1550).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-311 — 17 August 1897, infirmary — "The remedies hurt me"; a little linen
- What happened: "I feel God wants me to suffer. The remedies that should do me good and that relieve other patients hurt me." When they hesitated to give her a small cloth she asked for: "People ought to believe me when I ask for something, because I'm a very good 'little girl'" (meaning she asks only for what is essential). "I'm a very sick 'little girl', yes, very sick!"
- Cards: F-OW-290
- Best details:
  - « Les remèdes qui devraient me faire du bien et qui soulagent les autres malades, me font du mal à moi. » (F-OW-290).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 17.8.1-2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-312 — 18 Aug 1897, Vichy — Isidore's letter from Vichy: the Casino against the Carmel
- What happened: From Vichy on 18 Aug 1897, Isidore Guérin wrote to his niece, contrasting the spa town, with its Casino, Eden theatre and ball, with the modest Carmel "without gilding or sculpture, from which rises the murmur of prayer". "On a bed of pain lies a pure young girl, already consumed by the fires of that divine love to which she aspires to be entirely and forever joined. She does not desire death but loves it as a liberator. She asks for suffering to be more like her Master, and offers all... not in expiation of the sins she has committed but for those of that crowd that runs dancing, to the sound of infernal violins, to throw itself gaily into hell." He thanked God for "such angels".
- Cards: F-EW-2634
- Best details:
  - The Casino, Eden theatre and ball set against "the bare cross" of the Carmel (F-EW-2634).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting letters. Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Isidore says "my five little girls"; Piat does not explain whom he means. Thérèse wanted to reply and could not.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-313 — 18 August 1897, infirmary — "My merit is perhaps going to be lost, since you're writing it down"
- What happened: When Mère Agnès took up her pencil to note a remark, Thérèse asked: "My merit is perhaps going to be lost, since I told you and you're writing it down?" "So you want to gain merits?" "Yes, but not for me; for poor sinners, for the needs of the whole Church, to throw flowers to everyone, just and sinners." Told she was very patient: "I haven't yet had one minute of patience. It's not my patience!... People always get it wrong!" "If I saw a big spider on our bed" she wonders what she would do.
- Cards: F-OW-291
- Best details:
  - « Mon mérite va être perdu peut-être, puisque je vous l'ai dit et que vous l'écrivez ? » (F-OW-291).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 18.8.3-4, 18.8.7). Medium-high. Langlois cites this saying as proof Thérèse knew she was being recorded.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-314 — 19 August 1897, infirmary — The last communion: constant vomiting
- What happened: From 19 August she could no longer receive Communion because of constant vomiting. Her last Communion, on 19 August (feast of St Hyacinth in Carmel), was offered for the conversion of Fr Hyacinthe Loyson, the ex-Carmelite. She often spoke of him and made many sacrifices for him. That day she nearly fainted hearing the Miserere recited even quietly before Communion. Until her death she could not receive Communion again because of vomiting. She had asked TSA to join her prayers for him. More on the last Communion. Piat also gives 19 Aug 1897 as the last Communion, offered for the conversion of the apostate Carmelite Père Hyacinthe Loyson. From then on she was obliged "to deprive herself of the happiness of receiving God". Laveille says that from 17 Aug she vomited so often that Communion became impossible, "the most cruel trial of all". The Prioress's Epilogue says no Communion from 16 Aug to her death, because of vomiting.
- Cards: F-EW-1506, F-EW-1552, F-EW-1554, F-EW-1555, F-EW-2636, F-LF-236, F-EW-2104
- Best details:
  - From 19 August 1897 no more Communion (F-EW-1506).
  - "poor Père Hyacinthe" (F-EW-1554).
  - She nearly fainted at the Miserere said quietly (F-EW-1552).
  - "the most cruel trial of all" (F-LF-236).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; Process 1910, f. 245v). High. | Mère Agnès (saw and heard; 1910, 1915); TSA and Marie des Anges (notes). High. | Piat; Laveille; Prioress's Epilogue (D6 p. 208). High.
- Conflicts: Godefroy's hearsay says she received Communion daily as long as she could (F-EW-1331); the standard record says daily Communion stopped in August 1897. | Last Communion on 19 Aug (Pauline, Piat) versus no Communion from 16 Aug (D6) versus vomiting from 17 Aug (Laveille).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: her last Communion offered for a priest who had left the Order.

### E-P6-315 — 19 Aug 1897, infirmary — To the novices, still: "Don't sit crooked on chairs. It's written."
- What happened: Even on 19 August she watched over the novices and told one not to sit sideways on chairs, because it is written (in the customs). On 9 Aug she says she "used her sword" with a novice the evening before.
- Cards: F-EW-1551
- Best details:
  - « Il ne faut pas s'asseoir ainsi de travers sur les chaises. C'est écrit. » (F-EW-1551).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 281r). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-316 — 19 August 1897, infirmary — "I'm afraid I'll lose my mind"; the dead Christ
- What happened: She nearly fainted before Communion when the Miserere was recited, even softly. "I may lose my ideas. Oh, if people knew what this weakness is!... Last night I couldn't go on; I asked the Blessed Virgin to take my head in her hands so I could bear it." Looking at a crucifix with bowed head: "He is dead! I prefer that he be shown dead, because I think he doesn't suffer any more." "Everything has been said, hasn't it?" "Yes!" "I suffer only one instant. It's because we think of the past and the future that we lose heart and despair."
- Cards: F-OW-292
- Best details:
  - « Il est mort, Lui ! J'aime mieux qu'on le représente mort, parce que je pense qu'il ne souffre plus. » (F-OW-292).
  - "I suffer only one instant" (F-OW-292).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 19.8). Medium-high. Last Communion was 19 Aug 1897.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-317 — August 1897, infirmary — Burning thirst: "like pouring fire on fire"
- What happened: She suffered much in the side, the shoulder, and from a burning thirst that nothing could quench. She said that when she drank, it was like pouring fire on fire.
- Cards: F-EW-1515
- Best details:
  - "like pouring fire on fire" (F-EW-1515).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-318 — 20 Aug 1897 (DE), infirmary — "How happy I would have been if I had been asked": the difficult infirm Sister
- What happened: A Sister in the infirmary tried everyone's patience with her many odd habits. She was a neurasthenic nun whose incurable boredom was torture for whoever kept her company. When the others showed how tiresome it was to keep her company, Thérèse said she would have been happy to be asked to do it. It might have cost her nature, but she would have done it with love, recalling "I was sick and you visited me". She said the infirmarian's post would please her most, but she would not ask for it for fear of presumption. She urged Céline to nurse the sick with love, as if serving God Himself. The Carnet jaune (20 Aug, 3 Sep) names the difficult patient as the elderly Mère Cœur de Jésus: "Oh, how I would have liked to be infirmarian, not by nature but 'by an attraction of grace'. And it seems to me I would have made Mère Cœur de Jésus happy!" (again 3 Sep: "Ah, how drawn I would have been to all that!")
- Cards: F-EW-1517, F-EW-1558, F-EW-1561, F-EW-1560, F-OW-295
- Best details:
  - « Que j'aurais été heureuse si on m'avait demandé cela! » (F-EW-1517, F-EW-1561).
  - Grace would have spoken louder than nature (F-EW-1558).
  - « L'emploi d'infirmière est celui qui me plairait le mieux » (F-EW-1560).
  - "by an attraction of grace" (F-OW-295).
- Evidence: Marie (heard; 1910, 1915); Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 281r); Céline (heard). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.3; 3.9.3). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Dates: DEA 20-8 (F-EW-1517) versus 28 July for a related saying about a less likeable patient (F-EW-1487). The Sister is not named. | The other cards leave the Sister unnamed; this one names Mère Cœur de Jésus (probably Mère Hermance du Cœur de Jésus, the same nun as in the Corbinelli story; the card does not say so).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-319 — 20 Aug 1897, infirmary — Pauline gets her to drink the milk
- What happened: She could no longer drink milk, which disgusted her. Pauline asked if she would drink the cup to save Pauline's life. She said yes. Pauline asked: then would she not take it for the love of God? She drank the cup in one go. The Carnet jaune gives the same milk exchange: "Would you drink this cup to save my life?" "Oh yes!... Well then, and I wouldn't take it for the love of God?" and she drank it in one go.
- Cards: F-EW-1556, F-OW-294
- Best details:
  - The cup drunk "in one go" (F-EW-1556).
  - "Would you drink this cup to save my life?" (F-OW-294).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; NV f. 281v). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-320 — 20 Aug 1897, infirmary — The Holy Family as ordinary people; "no useless miracles"
- What happened: She imagined the Holy Family living an ordinary life. Joseph planed wood and wiped his brow, on the sly so as not to grieve Mary; he was sometimes not paid and even reproached. Village women chatted with Mary and asked to take the child Jesus to play. She rejected legends like Jesus breathing life into clay birds: he did no useless miracles. In her last illness Pauline saw her throw flowers to St Joseph's statue. The Carnet jaune (20 Aug) adds: "It will be nice to know in Heaven all that happened in the Holy Family!" She imagined little Jesus wanting to fast like his mother; Joseph could not fast because of his work: "I see him planing, then wiping his forehead from time to time. Oh, how sorry I feel for him!" "What does me good when I think of the Holy Family is to imagine a quite ordinary life. Not all they tell us... that the Child Jesus, after making clay birds, blew on them and gave them life. Ah no, little Jesus did not do useless miracles like that." "How many times did people refuse to pay him for his work!"
- Cards: F-EW-1557, F-EW-1559, F-OW-296
- Best details:
  - « le petit Jésus ne faisait pas de miracles inutiles comme ça! » (F-EW-1557).
  - Joseph "wiping sweat from his face on the sly so as not to grieve Mary" (F-EW-1559).
  - She prayed daily "O saint Joseph, père et protecteur des vierges" (F-EW-1559 notes).
  - « Je le vois raboter, puis s'essuyer le front de temps en temps. » (F-OW-296).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, saw; NV f. 281v-282v; 1915, Pauline wrote it down at once). High. Same day she rejected a priest's claim about Mary (see E-P6-263). | Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.14). Medium-high. Her rejection of apocryphal miracles is a sign of her taste for realism.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-321 — summer 1897 (witness says "a few days before" death), cloister walk — The E-P6-321 shoot she begged to spare
- What happened: A few days before her death (per the witness) her bed was rolled out under the cloister. Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, the garth gardener, said she would pull up a dying E-P6-321 shoot. Thérèse, in a plaintive voice, begged: for me who am going to die, let this poor E-P6-321 live. She had to insist, but it was spared.
- Cards: F-EW-1620
- Best details:
  - "for me who am going to die, let this poor E-P6-321 live" (F-EW-1620).
  - The shoot came from Marie des Anges's own family (F-EW-1620).
- Evidence: Marie des Anges (present or told). Medium: the timing is doubtful.
- Conflicts: "A few days before death" is doubtful, since outings under the cloister were in July-August (card note). See I-M08.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-322 — 20 August 1897, infirmary — A day of anguish behind the fly screen
- What happened: On 20 Aug, feeling she could not receive Communion any more, and hearing many remarks about it, she had "a day of anguish and temptations" that Mère Agnès "guessed were terrible". She asked for silence and not to be looked at: "I'd cry too much if I told you my troubles now, and I'm so short of breath I'd certainly suffocate." After at least an hour she spoke, holding in front of her eyes the screen she had been given against flies.
- Cards: F-OW-293
- Best details:
  - « Je pleurerais trop si je vous racontais tout de suite mes peines » (F-OW-293).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.10). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-323 — 20 August 1897, infirmary — "Agne! Agne!" and "a cooking recipe"
- What happened: To Céline: "You know you're nursing a 'baby' who's dying... Put something good in the big glass, because 'baby' has a taste of rot in her mouth." She asked to be kissed little because breath tired her: "hands don't breathe". To relieve breathlessness she gave a small regular cry: "Oh là là!" or "Agne! Agne!"; "that's not nice, it displeases me; now I'll say: Anne! Anne!" — "We'll put that in your obituary circular." — "It would look like a cooking recipe!" She coughed at her glass of Botot water: "It's not for drinking, I tell you!"
- Cards: F-OW-294
- Best details:
  - « Ça ferait l'effet d'une recette de cuisine ! » (F-OW-294).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.1-2, 20.8.5-6, 20.8.12). Medium-high. Humour at the edge of death.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-324 — 20 August 1897 — "Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur made me do many mortifications"
- What happened: "No one suspects that Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, as provisor [in charge of food], made me do many mortifications. She loves me so much that I seemed very spoiled; but the mortification is greater that way... She looked after me according to her tastes, absolutely opposite to mine." When she received absolution: "He's put a nice white little dress on me and changed my smock."
- Cards: F-OW-297
- Best details:
  - « Elle me soignait selon ses goûts absolument opposés aux miens. » (F-OW-297).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 20.8.17-18). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-325 — 21-23 Aug 1897, infirmary — Most sermons on Mary left her cold
- What happened: She told Pauline that most sermons she had heard on the Virgin did not touch her. Preachers should show that one can imitate Mary's virtues; if one must say "Ah! Ah!" all the time, one soon has enough. Priests should show her imitable virtues, not only her privileges; "Mary prefers imitation to admiration". If she had been a priest she would have spoken well of Mary: "more mother than queen". The Carnet jaune (21, 23 Aug): "How I would have liked to be a priest to preach on the Blessed Virgin! Once would have been enough." Preachers should not say improbable things, e.g. that Mary at three went to the Temple "with burning, quite extraordinary feelings... whereas she perhaps went simply to obey her parents". "They show her unapproachable; they should show her imitable... say she lived by faith like us." "She is more Mother than Queen." "Everything I would preach about her, I said in my hymn 'Why I love you, O Mary!'" (PN 54).
- Cards: F-EW-1562, F-EW-1567, F-OW-298
- Best details:
  - « Elle aime mieux l'imitation que l'admiration. » (F-EW-1567).
  - "more mother than queen" (F-EW-1562).
  - « On la montre inabordable, il faudrait la montrer imitable » (F-OW-298).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; 1910 NV f. 283v; 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 21.8.3; 23.8.9). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-326 — 21 Aug 1897, infirmary — "Each time I say 'I suffer', answer 'So much the better'"
- What happened: On 21 Aug, groaning and breathing with difficulty, she said "I suffer! I suffer!" with each breath, which seemed to help her breathe. She told Céline to answer "So much the better!" each time. That is what she would like to say to complete her thought, but she lacked the strength.
- Cards: F-EW-1563, F-EW-1564
- Best details:
  - « Chaque fois que je dirai 'je souffre', vous répondrez: tant mieux! » (F-EW-1564).
- Evidence: Céline (heard, involved; 1910, 1915). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-327 — 22 Aug 1897 on — Bowel pain: "sitting on iron spikes"
- What happened: On 22 August atrocious intestinal pains began. She felt them most when they tried to sit her up to ease her breathing during coughing fits that lasted hours. She said she was "sitting on iron spikes" and begged them to pray for her. The Carnet jaune (23 Aug) adds: "Pray for me, my little Sisters, but not on your knees, sitting down." (they were kneeling).
- Cards: F-EW-1565, F-OW-299
- Best details:
  - « assise sur des fers pointus » (F-EW-1565).
  - "not on your knees, sitting down" (F-OW-299).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard, 1915). High. The intestinal pains fit tuberculous spread to the gut (the witness does not say this). | Mère Agnès (CJ 23.8.10). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-328 — 22 Aug 1897 (Marie Guérin's birthday), infirmary — Thérèse cannot answer her uncle; "What a good man my Uncle is"
- What happened: On 22 Aug 1897 (Marie's birthday) Thérèse gave Marie a picture she wished to sign, which took all her strength; she thought she could not finish. She could not answer her uncle's letter, which grieved her: "You will not be able to express all I feel; you will not tell them enough how I love them and how touched I am by their affection." She never tired of hearing the letter; Marie had to read it several times. "Oh, how good my Uncle is! What a great soul he has!" To say those few words she had to stop a minute between each word because of oppression. When the Eucharist was brought, the Sisters entered chanting the Miserere. The last time she was so weak that hearing it made her suffer "martyrdom". LT 265 (22 Aug 1897, to Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie, "souvenir of her 27 years"): only the dedication "Souvenir of her 27 years – Th. of the Child Jesus". The image she signed with all her strength that day.
- Cards: F-EW-2635, F-OW-519
- Best details:
  - A minute's pause between each word, because of oppression (F-EW-2635).
  - "Souvenir of her 27 years" (F-OW-519).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters. Medium-high. The image signed on 22 August is a lead for the objects list (not identified). | Her own writing (LT 265). High. The image may be the one Piat says she signed on 22 August.
- Conflicts: Miserere at Communion: the "last Communion" was on 19 Aug (see E-P6-314); Piat places the Eucharist scene on 22 Aug.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-329 — 23 Aug 1897, infirmary — A tempted seminarian's letter
- What happened: She offered her sufferings for a young seminarian who was tempted. He learned of it and wrote a very humble, moving letter. It consoled her: she saw her small sufferings bore fruit.
- Cards: F-EW-1568
- Best details:
  - The seminarian is not named (F-EW-1568).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 283r-v). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-330 — Aug 1897 (entry 25 Aug), infirmary — A Sister stands at the foot of her bed every evening, laughing
- What happened: A Sister came into the infirmary every evening, stood at the foot of the bed and looked at her laughing for a long time. Thérèse smiled back. Pauline felt this indiscreet visit tired her. Thérèse said she thought of Christ on the cross being looked at by people shaking their heads, but that it is very painful to be looked at laughing when you suffer. The Carnet jaune names the Sister who laughed at the foot of the bed as Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix; Thérèse's reflection: "Yes, it's very painful to be looked at laughing when one suffers. But I think Our Lord on the cross was looked at like that in his sufferings... it was even worse, for they really mocked him."
- Cards: F-EW-1509, F-OW-301
- Best details:
  - « c'est très pénible d'être regardée en riant quand on souffre » (F-EW-1509).
  - "it was even worse, for they really mocked him" (F-OW-301).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 284r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 25.8.1). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix is the older nun who later wrote a daily prayer to Thérèse (see E-P6-094); the same Sister here laughs at her bedside.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-331 — 25 August 1897 (feast of St Louis), infirmary — "How we must pray for the dying!"; a failed prayer to her father
- What happened: "Oh, how we must pray for the dying! If people knew!" "I believe the devil asked God's permission to tempt me by extreme suffering, to make me fail in patience and faith." On 25 Aug, feast of St Louis, she prayed fervently to her father "and was not heard"; "despite what I felt at first, I told God again that I loved him more, and all the saints too." "I'll suffocate!"
- Cards: F-OW-302
- Best details:
  - She prayed to her father on his feast "and was not heard" (F-OW-302).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 25.8.6-7, 25.8.9); Céline records the same words (Dernières paroles à Céline, Septembre n. 3). High (two recorders).
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-332 — 25 August 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary), five weeks before her death — Her last letter: "I cannot fear a God who made himself so small for me"
- What happened: Her last letter in the corpus is a holy card sent to Bellière on 25 Aug 1897. The front (recto) reads: "I cannot fear a God who made himself so small for me... I love him!... for he is only love and mercy!" The back (verso): "Last souvenir of a soul sister of yours, Th. de E. J."
- Cards: F-OW-520
- Best details:
  - « Je ne puis craindre un Dieu qui s'est fait pour moi si petit... je l'aime !... car Il n'est qu'amour et miséricorde ! » (F-OW-520).
- Evidence: Her own writing (LT 266). High. The Carmel site lists LT 266 as the last letter in its series.
- Conflicts: The text does not say whether the words were written on a printed card. Other last writings (the prayer of 8 Sep 1897) are still to be checked.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-333 — late Aug 1897 (letter of 27 Aug), infirmary — She cannot bear the rustle of paper
- What happened: By 27 Aug 1897 she was weaker and could not stand the least noise near her, even paper rustling or whispered words. Her state had changed much since the Assumption (15 Aug). The Sisters came to wish for her release, as she suffered "martyrdom". The oppression (breathlessness) made her suffer greatly.
- Cards: F-EW-1522
- Best details:
  - « même le froissement du papier, ou quelques paroles dites à voix basse » (F-EW-1522).
- Evidence: Marie de l'Eucharistie, contemporary letter of 27 Aug 1897. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-334 — 26-27 Aug 1897, infirmary — Iced water: "Oh, how I long for it!"
- What happened: She suffered constantly from thirst. When Marie offered iced water, she let slip how much she wanted it. Marie reminded her the prioress had ordered her to ask for all she needed. She said she asked only for the necessary, not for relief: when she had no grapes, she did not ask for any. Even at the height of fever she did not ask for cool drinks unless offered. Later, urged to drink more, she said her tongue was not dry enough. Marie's 1910 account puts a version "three days before death": tortured by fever, she did not ask for water with a little ice in it, nor for grapes when they forgot to put them within reach. Marie offered iced water; Thérèse said she longed for it.
- Cards: F-EW-1570, F-EW-1571, F-EW-1640
- Best details:
  - « Oh! j'en ai une envie! » (F-EW-1570).
  - « Je demande ce qui m'est nécessaire, mais non ce qui me fait plaisir » (F-EW-1571).
  - "three days before her death" (F-EW-1640).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 285r); Marie (heard, 1915). High. | Marie (saw and heard, 1910). Medium on date.
- Conflicts: Marie says "three days before", but the cited notebook date is 27 August. Date conflict inside the source.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-335 — 27 August 1897, infirmary — The bishop's visit dreaded; "if it were only St Nicholas"
- What happened: "How I'd like the bishop not to come and see me... Still, a bishop's blessing is a grace." Laughing: "If it were only St Nicholas, who raised three little children!" (Mgr Hugonin was in Lisieux.) "How odd it is to be afraid of dying! ...When you're married, with a husband and children, one understands; but I who have nothing!..." Told she had eaten nothing: "Nothing! I've had two cups of milk, I'm stuffed." Thirsty, she asked only for "the necessary"; "when I have no grapes I don't ask for any".
- Cards: F-OW-304
- Best details:
  - « Si c'était seulement St Nicolas qui a ressuscité trois petits enfants ! » (F-OW-304).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 27.8). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-336 — 28 Aug 1897, infirmary — The black hole by the chestnut trees
- What happened: Pointing through the window to a dark spot in the garden beside the chestnut trees, where nothing could be seen, she said she was in a hole like that for body and soul, in darkness, but in peace. Laveille has the words: "As regards both soul and body, I am in a place like that. What darkness! Yet I am in peace there." The Carnet jaune (28 Aug) adds: the bed was turned towards the window. At midday silence: "Look, do you see down there the black hole (under the chestnut trees near the cemetery) where you can't make out anything any more? It's in a hole like that that I am, for soul and body. Ah yes, what darkness! But I'm at peace there." Told the Sisters found her pretty: "What does that matter to me! ...it bores me." "I love flowers very much, roses, red flowers and beautiful pink daisies." "The little flowers tremble with me, I like that" (forget-me-nots around her pictures). Of the statue through a slit in the curtain: "Look, she's watching me!"
- Cards: F-EW-1572, F-LF-236, F-OW-305
- Best details:
  - "a hole like that for body and soul, in darkness, but in peace" (F-EW-1572).
  - "What darkness! Yet I am in peace there." (F-LF-236).
  - « c'est dans un trou comme cela que je suis pour l'âme et pour le corps » (F-OW-305).
  - "The little flowers tremble with me" (F-OW-305).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 285r-v). High. | Laveille. High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 28.8). Medium-high. The black hole is in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-337 — 29 August 1897 — Nine pears on the tree
- What happened: She called Mère Agnès: "I've just counted 9 pears on the pear tree by the window. There must be many others. I'm glad; you'll eat them. Fruit is so good!" Reading the Good Samaritan: "I'm like that poor traveller, 'semivivo', half dead, half alive."
- Cards: F-OW-306
- Best details:
  - « Je viens de compter 9 poires au poirier près de la fenêtre. » (F-OW-306).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 29.8). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-338 — 30 Aug 1897 — Telegram to Dr La Néele at Caen
- What happened: With Dr de Cornière still away, they telegraphed Dr La Néele at Caen on 30 August. He said what she endured was horrible and admired her patience.
- Cards: F-EW-1574
- Best details:
  - He called it "horrible" and admired her patience (F-EW-1574).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (1915). High.
- Conflicts: The file says La Néele was at Caen; elsewhere (miracle cards) he is "of Lisieux" by 1908.

### E-P6-339 — Aug-Sept 1897, infirmary — "Promise me a Memorare every day"
- What happened: A few weeks before her death Thérèse had Marthe called and said she would not be at peace about her. Marthe must promise to say a "Souvenez-vous" (Memorare) to the Virgin every day. Marthe promised and kept it. Marthe's own notes (AMOUR DE DIEU) repeat that she kept the promise. Marthe's record: weeks before death, Thérèse made her promise to say a Memorare every day ("I won't be at peace about you").
- Cards: F-EW-1609, F-EW-1616, F-OW-328
- Best details:

  - Marthe promised and kept it (F-EW-1616).
  - "I won't be at peace about you" (F-OW-328).
- Evidence: Marthe (PA 18 p. 1062). High. | Marthe (notes). High. | Sr Marthe de Jésus (recorder). Medium.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-340 — about a month before death (Jun-Sep 1897) — The red balloon and the spinning top
- What happened: Sr Marie de la Trinité (novice) recorded that about a month before her death, seeing a big red balloon at the foot of the bed, she wanted to play. Thérèse told her to play behind her while she shut her eyes, and peeked and laughed. Then she "ordered" her to take her Christmas top and play for an hour in the novitiate attic: "if anyone notices, say I told you to." She made her go and claim the forgotten dessert as a penance. A week before death she made her promise to cry "in the shell" (a novice rule). The novice was forbidden to speak to Thérèse "on the pretext that, being young, I might catch her illness" (Thérèse had told her no one would catch it, she had asked God).
- Cards: F-OW-288
- Best details:
  - « Mettez-vous derrière moi pendant qu'il n'y a personne, et puis jouez avec, je vais fermer les yeux » (F-OW-288).
  - "if anyone notices, say I told you to" (F-OW-288).
- Evidence: Marie de la Trinité (recorder; Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs nn. 1-5). Medium.
- Conflicts: The card notes to check the "coquille" (shell), a small shell used by novices to catch tears, per other sources.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-341 — 30 August 1897, cloister of the Carmel — Wheeled to the choir door; the last photograph; the doctor's verdict
- What happened: She was put on a folding bed and rolled to the choir door under the cloister, left alone a long while, prayed looking at the grille, and threw rose petals on it. "She was photographed before going back in." Dr La Néele said: "It's for soon, my little sister, I'm sure." She smiled with happiness. The chaplain Youf told her: "You have suffered more than you will suffer now... We are finishing our ministry together, you as a Carmelite, I as a priest."
- Cards: F-OW-307
- Best details:
  - « On l'a photographiée avant de rentrer. » (F-OW-307).
  - Youf: "We are finishing our ministry together, you as a Carmelite, I as a priest." (F-OW-307).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 30.8.3). Medium-high. The photograph itself is not free to use (plan 01 § 9); the photographer (Sr Geneviève) is not named here.
- Conflicts: Pauline (1915, F-EW-1574) says La Néele was telegraphed at Caen on 30 August; this card has him at her bedside that day. Youf died 15 days after her (F-EW-1381).
- New to English readers: yes (the circumstance of the last photograph)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-342 — late August 1897, infirmary — "Don't leave poison near patients in such pain"
- What happened: She said that if she had no faith she would despair. She understood well why people without faith kill themselves when they suffer so much. She told Pauline: when you have patients in such violent pain, be careful not to leave near them remedies that are poison. It takes only a moment, when suffering like this, to lose one's reason.
- Cards: F-EW-1516
- Best details:
  - "it takes only a moment, when suffering like this, to lose one's reason" (F-EW-1516).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard, 1915). High. The card notes no "poison" advice in the 1910 deposition.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial (Last Conversations)
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-343 — 31 August 1897, infirmary — "Who could have invented the Blessed Virgin?"
- What happened: After looking long at the statue: "Who could have invented the Blessed Virgin?" "How much courage I need to make a sign of the cross!" "Ah! If you love me, how I love you too!" At lunch: "I wanted to have a real dinner, so I took a grape and a little sip of wine, which I offered to the Blessed Virgin. Then I did the same for the Child Jesus, and my little dinner was over." Told the bed would soon be empty: "Ah, in your place how happy I'd be!" "It's incredible how all my hopes have been fulfilled... I begged God to... consume me quickly in love, and I've been heard!"
- Cards: F-OW-308
- Best details:
  - « Qui est-ce qui aurait pu inventer la Sainte Vierge ? » (F-OW-308).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 31.8). Medium-high. Not found in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-344 — 1897, last illness — No delirium, ever
- What happened: Asked if she was delirious in her last illness, Pauline said Thérèse did not lose her presence of mind for a single moment and became calmer as death approached. The judge also asked about a "neuropathic" temperament; Pauline said no: a calm, balanced, unimaginative child.
- Cards: F-EW-1231
- Best details:
  - Presence of mind never lost, calmer near death (F-EW-1231).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw, 1910). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-345 — 1897; Hermance died 1898 — Father Corbinelli and little Aloysius
- What happened: During refectory reading of the Life of St Aloysius, old Mère Hermance du Cœur de Jésus called Thérèse "little Aloysius" and herself "old Father Corbinelli". Near death Thérèse sent word that she had seen Corbinelli's grave close to Aloysius's. Hermance took it as a prophecy. She died one year later, and the two graves lie side by side. TSA says Thérèse told Mère Hermance du Cœur de Jésus that she would die soon, and the nun died a year later. TSA also says Thérèse sometimes answered people's unspoken thoughts. The longer account: during Thérèse's last illness the life of St Aloysius Gonzaga was read in the refectory. Mère Hermance, struck by his friendship with the old Fr Corbinelli, told Thérèse: you are little Louis, I am old Corbinelli; remember me in heaven. Thérèse offered to come for her soon; Hermance said not yet, she had not suffered enough, and she needed authority's sanction for so grave a matter. In the last days, thanking her for flowers, Thérèse sent word that at Mass that morning she had seen Corbinelli's grave very close to little Louis's. Hermance said she had understood. Their graves are indeed side by side in Lisieux cemetery. Sr Hermance du Cœur de Jésus's own account (Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs): the old Mère Cœur de Jésus told Thérèse: "You are little Louis and I am old Father Corbinelli; when you're in Heaven, remember me!" Thérèse offered to come and fetch her soon. She said she needed permission from authority; the prioress said yes without attaching importance. In her last days Thérèse sent her a message: "Tell Mère Cœur de Jésus that this morning, during Mass, I saw the tomb of Father Corbinelli very close to that of little Louis." The account calls the word "truly prophetic".
- Cards: F-EW-1222, F-EW-1317, F-EW-1621, F-OW-326
- Best details:
  - Hermance's nicknames: "little Aloysius" and "old Father Corbinelli" (F-EW-1222).
  - The graves lie side by side (F-EW-1222).
  - Prediction of a death within the year; no detail (F-EW-1317).
  - "you are little Louis, I am old Corbinelli; remember me in heaven" (F-EW-1621).
  - Hermance: not yet, "she had not suffered enough" (F-EW-1621).
  - « j'ai vu la tombe du Père Corbinelli tout près de celle du petit Louis » (F-OW-326).
- Evidence: Prioress's Epilogue (edited account). Medium. | TSA (1915); she does not say she heard it. Low-medium. Not found in other witnesses of this batch. | Editors (source not given); Sr Geneviève (PA) on Hermance's many oral messages. Medium. The graves are a checkable fact (an old photo is shown). | Sr Hermance du Cœur de Jésus (Dernières paroles). Low-medium: one account; its meaning depends on her later death; it does not state when she died.
- Conflicts: F-EW-1222 tells it as the graves of Corbinelli and Aloysius lying side by side; F-EW-1317 says only that she told Hermance she would die soon. | The messenger: the infirmarian, Sr Geneviève per her PA text. | In F-EW-1621 Hermance said "not yet, I have not suffered enough" and needed authority's sanction; here the prioress said yes. Check the death date of Mère Hermance in other parts (1898 per F-EW-1222).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-346 — 1897, late in the illness, infirmary — Helping change her bed: a look of thanks; an angelic face
- What happened: Aimée (AJ) left the post of infirmarian at the start of Thérèse's illness and saw her little. Only once did she help change her bed. Thérèse could no longer speak but thanked her with a heavenly look full of gratitude and affection. During sharp sufferings her face kept an angelic expression of happiness. A second account of Aimée's help. In the last days of September 1897, when Thérèse could no longer move and her bed had to be remade, the infirmarians feared hurting her. Thérèse said Sr Aimée would easily take her in her arms: "she is tall and strong, and very gentle with the sick". Aimée lifted her like a light burden without a jolt. Thérèse, arms round her neck, thanked her with a smile of grateful affection she never forgot.
- Cards: F-EW-1307, F-EW-1308, F-EW-1627
- Best details:
  - A "heavenly look" of thanks from a Sister who could no longer speak (F-EW-1307).
  - « elle est grande et forte, et très douce autour des malades » (F-EW-1627).
  - Arms round Aimée's neck, a smile of grateful affection (F-EW-1627).
- Evidence: AJ (experienced it, 1915). High for the bed; medium for the face (she seldom saw her). | Aimée (deposition and later account; Carmel archive circular of 17 Jan 1930). High.
- Conflicts: F-EW-1307 says Aimée helped change the bed only once and Thérèse could no longer speak but thanked her with a look; F-EW-1627 says Thérèse named Aimée herself and thanked her with a smile. Probably the same occasion.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: Aimée lifting her like a light burden.

### E-P6-347 — 1897, shortly before dying — Advice to prioresses: make the sick ask for what they need

### E-P6-352 — 2 Sept 1897, infirmary — "I'll go, I'll go soon" to Indochina
- What happened: In early September 1897 Sr Geneviève, seeing her dying, said: to think they still wait for you in Indochina! Thérèse answered she would go soon, and would quickly make her round. The Carnet jaune (2 Sep) gives the Saigon version: to "They're still expecting you at Saigon!": "I'll go, I'll go soon; if you knew how fast I'll do my round!"
