- Best details:
  - "would quickly make her round" (F-EW-1629). The Hanoi Carmel had asked for her (F-EW-889).
  - "how fast I'll do my round" (F-OW-310).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (present or told; 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 2.9). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Pauline (1915) has "Indochina" and Céline as the speaker (F-EW-1629); the Carnet jaune has "Saigon".
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-353 — 2 September 1897, infirmary — Opening her soul to the novice mistress; mortifications in the refectory; "Tom Thumb"
- What happened: "Perhaps two years after I came here God ended my trial with regard to Sr Marie des Anges, and I could open my soul to her... in the end she really comforted me." "It cost me a lot to ask to do mortifications in the refectory, because I was shy, I blushed, but I was faithful to my two times a week. When that trial of shyness passed, I paid less attention and must have forgotten more than once." To the Sisters calling her "the leader of the band": she held her hands a little apart, "This high in the family!" and mimed sowing: "Tom Thumb!"
- Cards: F-OW-310
- Best details:
  - "This high in the family!" and "Tom Thumb!" (F-OW-310).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 2.9.2-5). Medium-high. Compare Ms A 70v on her difficulty opening her soul.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes (Tostain; refectory mortifications)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-354 — 3 September 1897, infirmary — Not dazzled by the Tsar's visit to France
- What happened: Told of the honours paid to the Tsar of Russia in France: "Ah! all that doesn't dazzle me! Talk to me about God, the example of the saints, everything that is truth..." "When you think we're nursing a little saint!" "Well, so much the better! but I'd like God to say so."
- Cards: F-OW-311
- Best details:
  - « Ah ! ça ne m'éblouit pas tout ça ! » (F-OW-311).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 3.9.1-2). Medium-high. Nicholas II's visit was Aug-Sep 1897 (outside fact). The Tsar remark is in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-355 — 4 September 1897, infirmary — Her list of loves at the Angelus
- What happened: At the Angelus, too weak to say it, told just to say "Virgin Mary!", she said: "Virgin Mary, I love you with all my heart." When Céline asked her to add "for me too", she whispered: "for 'Mlle Lili', for Mama, for Godmother, for Léonie, little Marie, my Uncle, my Aunt, Jeanne, Francis, 'Maurice', 'little Roulland' and all those I love." She was annoyed that her craving for a simple dish had been reported to her uncle: "It's very odd that they let that be known in the world!" then: "It's offered to God. Let them think what they like!"
- Cards: F-OW-312
- Best details:
  - « pour «Mlle Lili», pour Maman, pour Marraine, pour Léonie... » (F-OW-312). Names "Maurice" (Bellière) and "little Roulland".
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 4.9.4-5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-356 — early September 1897 — Morphine: injections refused, syrup given by stratagem
- What happened: Dr de Cornière came back in early September and visited often. He spoke of morphine injections, but the prioress did not allow it. They gave her only small, rare doses of morphine syrup, because the prioress still had prejudices against the painkiller. Céline says the Sisters had to use a stratagem to give her the syrup, because Gonzague held that relieving a Carmelite that way was shameful, and she never agreed to injections.
- Cards: F-EW-1589, F-EW-1599
- Best details:
  - « parla de piqûres de morphine, mais la mère prieure ne le permit pas » (F-EW-1589).
  - « Il fallait aussi user de stratagème pour donner du sirop de morphine » (F-EW-1599).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (1915, absent in 1910); Céline (1915, flagged by editors). High for the fact; medium on the motive.
- Conflicts: Links to F-EW-2120 (Gonzague: "a sin to treat" new illnesses). Editors say Gaucher's work discusses the morphine.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-357 — 5 September 1897, infirmary — "There is one, perhaps... and even it's certain!"
- What happened: "So you're not sad to leave 'Mama'?" (childlike) "No!... If there were no eternal life, oh yes!... but there is one, perhaps... and even it's certain!" If she recovered: "Gentlemen, I'm very glad to be cured to serve God on earth still... I've suffered as if I were going to die; well, I'll start again another time!" After a drink with ice: "I drank without being thirsty! I'm a little 'drink-without-thirst'." Dr La Néele, who had said she might die turning over in bed, now said: "You're like a ship that neither advances nor goes back."
- Cards: F-OW-313
- Best details:
  - « mais il y en a une peut-être... et même c'est sûr ! » (F-OW-313).
  - "You're like a ship that neither advances nor goes back" (F-OW-313).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 5.9.1-4). Medium-high. The "peut-être" line is not found in Novissima Verba. It shows the trial of faith even in September.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-358 — 1897; relic 6 Sept — The novena to Théophane Vénard for Hanoi; his relic kissed
- What happened: Wishing to answer the Carmel of Hanoi's call for her, she began a novena to Théophane Vénard for her cure. It marked a worse phase of the illness. On 6 Sept 1897 the prioress brought her a relic of Vénard, which she kissed repeatedly.
- Cards: F-EW-2107
- Best details:
  - The relic kissed repeatedly (F-EW-2107).
- Evidence: Prioress's Epilogue (D6 pp. 201, 216). High.
- Conflicts: Pauline's NV records her asking for the relics of Anne de Jésus and of Vénard to kiss on 11 Sept (see E-P6-361).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-359 — 8 Sept 1897, infirmary — Her last written lines
- What happened: She could no longer look at the Virgin's statue without crying. On 8 September 1897 she asked to see again her image of Our Lady of Victories, on which she had stuck the little flower her father gave her when he let her enter Carmel. On the back, with a trembling hand, she wrote: "O Mary, if I were Queen of heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you would be Queen of heaven." These were the last lines she wrote. The Carmel editors' note on Pri 21 says: "Last text in Thérèse's hand." The text is dated 8 Sep 1897 (the feast of the Nativity of Mary, and the seventh anniversary of her profession): "O Mary, if I were the Queen of Heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you might be the Queen of Heaven!" On the back is a picture of Our Lady of Victories, on which Thérèse had stuck the little saxifrage flower her father gave her when she asked to enter Carmel; the editors say the flower was moved from the Imitation (Ms A 50v) to this picture.
- Cards: F-EW-1630, F-EW-1631, F-EW-1632, F-OW-583
- Best details:
  - « O Marie, si j'étais la reine du ciel et que vous fussiez Thérèse, je voudrais être Thérèse pour que vous fussiez la Reine du ciel » (F-EW-1632).
  - The image carried the little flower her father gave her at her entry into Carmel (F-EW-1631).
  - « [Dernier texte de la main de Thérèse. Le verso est une image de Notre Dame des Victoires sur laquelle Thérèse a collé la petite fleur de saxifrage] » (F-OW-583).
- Evidence: Céline (saw; 1910, 1915); Mère Agnès (1915). High. | Her own writing (Pri 21); note on "last text" and the flower from Carmel editors (facts only). High for the text and date; the "last text" claim is the editors'. Compare LT 266 (25 Aug 1897).
- Conflicts: Ms A says she put the flower in her Imitation of Christ (the stem broke near the root); by 1897 it was on the picture (see E-P6-286).
- New to English readers: yes (the flower on the image and the "last text" status); partial for the words
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-360 — 8-9 September 1897, infirmary — Seventh anniversary of profession: robin, Léonie's music box, wild flowers
- What happened: On 8 Sep a robin hopped onto her bed. Léonie sent a music box (kept by the Carmel) whose tunes, though secular, she enjoyed. A sheaf of wild flowers came for her profession anniversary; she cried with gratitude: "outwardly I'm loaded with them, and yet inwardly I'm still in the trial... but also in peace." On 9 Sep the box was overwound; the gardener Auguste repaired it, but it lost its prettiest note.
- Cards: F-OW-314
- Best details:
  - « à l'intérieur je suis toujours dans l'épreuve... mais aussi dans la paix » (F-OW-314).
  - The music box lost its prettiest note (F-OW-314).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 8.9, 9.9.1). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-361 — 26 Aug and 11 Sept 1897, infirmary — Final acts: crucifix, holy water, relics, blessed candle
- What happened: On 11 Sept she asked for her crucifix to kiss after the act of contrition to gain the plenary indulgence for the souls in purgatory ("it's all I give them now"). She asked for holy water, and for the relics of Venerable Mère Anne de Jésus and of Théophane Vénard to kiss. A blessed candle was left burning all night on 26 Aug; she credited it with a less bad night. The Carnet jaune adds that on 11 Sept she kissed her crucifix for the plenary indulgence for the souls in purgatory ("I give them nothing but that now") and the relics of Anne of Jesus and Vénard.
- Cards: F-EW-1633, F-OW-315
- Best details:
  - "it's all I give them now" (F-EW-1633).
  - "I give them nothing but that now" (F-OW-315).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 284v, 287r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 11.9). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-362 — 11 September 1897, infirmary — "A kiss that makes a noise"; fear of the separation of soul and body
- What happened: "Give me a kiss, a kiss that makes a noise; the lips must go 'pit!'" "You are a lyre, a song to me... much more than a music box." "I'm afraid I was afraid of death... But I'm not afraid of what comes after, of course! And I don't regret life, oh no. It's only telling myself: what is this mysterious separation of soul and body? It's the first time I've felt that." Unable to sleep: "It's the devil's malice; he's furious because I didn't forget my little devotions."
- Cards: F-OW-315
- Best details:
  - « J'ai peur d'avoir eu peur de la mort... » (F-OW-315).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 11.9.2, 11.9.4-5). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-363 — 13 Sept 1897, infirmary — Violets: she signed to ask if she could smell them
- What happened: When E-P6-363 were brought she exclaimed at their scent. Then she signed to Pauline to ask whether she could smell them without breaking mortification. The Carnet jaune (13 Sep) adds: her feet had swollen the day before; any movement of the bed or touch hurt her. Marie and Agnès took her pulse at length; she said nothing so as not to hurt them, then cried. "I'd like... not to hurt my little Sisters any more, and for that to go away quickly." Trying to shift her cushion herself: "Wait, I'll push myself to the end of the bed, making the movements of a little grasshopper."
- Cards: F-EW-1634, F-OW-316
- Best details:
  - She signed to ask permission to enjoy the scent (F-EW-1634).
  - « en faisant les mouvements d'une petite sauterelle » (F-OW-316).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; NV f. 287r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 13.9.1-2). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-364 — September 1897, infirmary — Her face reflected in a chalice
- What happened: In her last illness she was shown the chalice of a young priest who had just said his First Mass. She looked inside and said that as sacristan she liked to see herself reflected in chalices. She thought Christ's blood would later fall where her face had been. The Carnet jaune (18-19 Sep) names the priest: Abbé Denis, newly ordained. After his first Mass she asked to see his chalice and looked long at the bottom of the cup: "Because I'm reflected in it. In the sacristy I liked doing that. I was glad to say: My features have been reflected where the blood of Jesus has rested and will come down again. How often too I've thought that in Rome my face was reproduced in the Holy Father's eyes." On 18 Sep she admired Céline's altar cloth for the oratory and Abbé Denis's vestment.
- Cards: F-EW-1585, F-OW-318
- Best details:
  - "Christ's blood would later fall where her face had been" (F-EW-1585).
  - "in Rome my face was reproduced in the Holy Father's eyes" (F-OW-318).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (present, 1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 18.9.3, 19.9). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Date 18-19 Sept (Carnet jaune) versus "September 1897" (F-EW-1585).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-365 — c. 18 Sept 1897, infirmary — "Do not weep like those who have no hope"
- What happened: One day in the last illness Marie felt grief close to despair at the thought of her death. She wept in secret and showed nothing. Much later that day she came into the infirmary without any sign of emotion, and Thérèse greeted her with a gentle reproach.
- Cards: F-EW-1636
- Best details:
  - « Il ne faut pas pleurer comme ceux qui n'ont pas d'espérance » (F-EW-1636).
- Evidence: Marie (her own experience; 1910). High. Presented as a sign of supernatural knowledge.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-366 — one of the last nights, Sept 1897, infirmary — "I say nothing to Him, I love Him"
- What happened: On one of the last nights Céline found her with hands joined and eyes raised. Céline told her to try to sleep. She said she could not, so she was praying. Asked what she said to Jesus, she said: "I say nothing to Him, I love Him." Céline's Dernières paroles (Sept) confirm: "I can't [sleep], I'm suffering too much, so I pray..." "And what do you say to Jesus?" "I don't say anything to him, I love him!"
- Cards: F-EW-1597, F-EW-1603, F-OW-309
- Best details:
  - « Je ne lui dis rien, je l'aime » (F-EW-1597).
  - "I can't [sleep], I'm suffering too much, so I pray" (F-OW-309).
- Evidence: Céline (saw and heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline). Medium.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-367 — late Sept 1897 — The stitched pleats of the habit
- What happened: For convenience the novice (Marie de la Trinité) had back-stitched the pleats of her habit that should be formed by hand each morning under the belt. Some days before her death Thérèse told her to unpick them as it was not the custom. She delayed. (See K-M03 for the sequel.)
- Cards: F-EW-1626
- Best details:
  - She was told to unpick the stitching as it was not the custom (F-EW-1626).
- Evidence: Marie de la Trinité (circular of 20 Feb 1944). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-368 — 14 and 20 Sept 1897, infirmary — "The doctor lies": she denies being patient
- What happened: When the doctor praised her heroic patience, she said it was a lie. She moaned, sighed and cried out all the time "My God, I can't go on! Have pity on me!" Earlier (14 Sept) the doctor had told her she would have no agony. She said she would accept one anyway. The Carnet jaune (14 and 20 Sep): Dr La Néele (14 Sep) had told her she would have no agony; "They told me I'd have no agony!... But after all, I'm willing to have one." "Would you choose?" "I'd choose nothing!" On 20 Sep, Dr de Cornière said she must be suffering "a real martyrdom" and praised her "heroic patience". Thérèse: "How can he say I'm patient! It's a lie! I never stop moaning, I sigh, I cry out all the time: Oh là là! and then: My God, I can't go on! Have pity, have pity on me!" When her tunic was changed they were struck by her thinness, though her face was unchanged; the prioress came late and said, "What's this, such a thin little girl?" — "A skeleton!" ("Un quelette!", mimicking a child).
- Cards: F-EW-1637, F-OW-317, F-OW-319
- Best details:
  - « Comment peut-il dire que je suis patiente! Mais c'est mentir! » (F-EW-1637).
  - "Un quelette!" mimicking a child (F-OW-319).
  - « Comment peut-il dire que je suis patiente ! Mais c'est mentir ! » (F-OW-319).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 287v-288r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 14.9.3; 20.9.1-2). Medium-high. "C'est mentir" not found in Novissima Verba.
- Conflicts: Who said she would have no agony: Dr La Néele (Carnet jaune, 14 Sept) versus "the doctor" (F-EW-1637, NV f. 287v-288r).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-369 — 21 September 1897, infirmary — The spittoon and the unspoken thought; eyelashes "for the earth"; Bellière
- What happened: Mère Agnès silently emptied her spittoon, thinking how happy she would be if Thérèse said she would repay it in Heaven; at once Thérèse said: "In Heaven I'll repay you for that." When some eyelashes came out as she wiped her eyes: "Take these eyelashes, my little Sr Geneviève; we must give as little as possible to the earth," and punned on the name of a workman, Père Alaterre ("to the earth"): "Poor old fellow, though, if it gives him pleasure!" Of Bellière: "He calls me his little Thérèse!... I'm 'fed up' with little Thérèse! ...Not fed up with little Pauline, oh no!" "What is agony? It seems to me I'm in it all the time!"
- Cards: F-OW-320
- Best details:
  - « il faut qu'on en donne le moins possible à la terre » (F-OW-320).
  - The answer to an unspoken thought (listed also as a M-OW answer per the recorder) (F-OW-320).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 21.9.1, 21.9.3, 21.9.6-7). Medium-high. M. Alaterre was the workman, brother of Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-370 — 22 September 1897, infirmary — "If I had not had faith, I would have killed myself"
- What happened: "What a terrible illness, and how much you've suffered!" — "Yes!!! What a grace to have faith! If I had not had faith, I would have killed myself without hesitating a single instant..." Earlier that day: "It would not have been necessary to pity me so much, I assure you... I came out strengthened by humiliations; there was no one braver than me under fire." About the saints: "They want to see... how far I'll push my trust." "It seems to me it was yesterday that I followed the Community, that I wrote the notebook."
- Cards: F-OW-321
- Best details:
  - « Si je n'avais pas eu la foi, je me serais donné la mort sans hésiter un seul instant... » (F-OW-321).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 22.9.1, 22.9.3-6). Medium-high: it is not the kind of line she would invent. Novissima Verba (English 1952) does not contain this line.
- Conflicts: Hard, important and sensitive. The biography must quote it with its context: the next words praise the grace of faith. Compare Ms C 5v-7v on the trial, and the earlier "poison" advice (F-EW-1516) and the atheists remark (F-EW-1641).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-371 — 23 September 1897, infirmary — "It's you who raised me"; the bird of prey
- What happened: After a long silence, to Marie and Agnès alone: "My little sisters, it's you who brought me up!..." and her eyes filled with tears. At night a cooing was heard at the window; one Sister said a turtle-dove, another a bird of prey: "Well, if it's a bird of prey, too bad! Birds of prey came to eat the martyrs." "Many times I've given beautiful smiles, lost, to 'Bobonne' and others."
- Cards: F-OW-322
- Best details:
  - « Mes petites soeurs, c'est vous qui m'avez élevée !... » (F-OW-322).
  - "Birds of prey came to eat the martyrs" (F-OW-322).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 23.9.3-4, 23.9.6). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: The cooing at the window is 23 Sept here; other cards give the turtle-dove on 29 Sept at 9 p.m.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-372 — 1897 (DE 24 Sept), infirmary — Who withheld pain relief? Dr de Cornière's "a soul not of this earth"
- What happened: MdA and Marie des Anges say God permitted that "our so good and devoted doctor", who came often, did not think of calming her pain with the relief science had found. So she bore the pain at full intensity to the end. In 1915 MdA says de Cornière "could not" give such relief. The doctor, edified, said: "If you knew what she suffers you would not want to keep her on earth. I cannot cure her; she is a soul not of this earth." TSA quotes him: "I have never seen anyone suffer so much with such an expression of supernatural joy; she is an angel." More versions of the doctor's remark. The doctor said he had never seen so much suffering borne with such supernatural joy: "She is an angel". If they knew what she suffered they would not try to keep her; "I shall not be able to cure her; she is a soul not made for earth." Several independent reports give it: De Teil's Articles, Mère Agnès to Dolan, the Prioress's Epilogue, and Laveille's Summarium quotation. TSA repeats it dated 24 Sept. Pauline (1915) dates another version to 24 Sept 1897: the community doctor praised her patience: do not wish to keep her in this state; what she endures is frightful; but what an angel, and what a smile he always saw on her. Marie des Anges's deposition (PA 10 [899]-[900]) repeats that Dr de Cornière could not give Thérèse the relief that would have lightened her suffering, and that she bore it to the end at full strength. The doctor was much edified and said: "Oh, if you knew what she suffers, you would not want to keep her on earth. I cannot cure her; she is a soul not of this earth."
- Cards: F-EW-1321, F-EW-1338, F-EW-1576, F-EW-1610, F-EW-1638, F-EW-2524
- Best details:
  - « Je ne pourrai la guérir, c'est une âme qui n'est pas de la terre » (F-EW-1321).
  - "I have never seen anyone suffer so much with such an expression of supernatural joy; she is an angel" (F-EW-1338, TSA's quote).
  - "She is an angel" (F-EW-1576, F-EW-1610).
  - "what an angel, and what a smile he always saw on her" (F-EW-1638).
  - « c'est une âme qui n'est pas de la terre » (F-EW-2524).
- Evidence: MdA (reported, 1911 and 1915); Marie des Anges (heard the doctor's remark; notes). Medium. | Multiple independent reports. High (several), medium for TSA's version. | Mère Agnès (heard; 1910, 1915). High. | Marie des Anges (reporting the doctor; the edition points to the DEA note of 24 Sep). Medium: second-hand. The Carmel site gives Dr de Cornière's dates as 1841-1922.
- Conflicts: MdA and Marie des Anges say the doctor did not think of / could not give relief. TSA and Marie du Sacré-Cœur blame the prioress (see F-EW-1314). MdA's two versions differ ("did not think of it" versus "could not"). See gaps. | MdA gives a different wording (F-EW-1321). | Compare F-EW-1321.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-373 — 24 Sept 1897 (DE), infirmary — "Intuitions! If you knew how poor I am"
- What happened: Asked by Mother Agnès if she had an intuition of her near death, she said: E-P6-373! If you knew my poverty! I know nothing but what you know; I guess nothing except by what I see and feel. Céline adds that her early talk of dying was a deduction from what was happening in her soul, not a revelation. The Carnet jaune (24 Sep) adds: "So you have no intuition of the day of your death?" — "Ah, Mother, E-P6-373! If you knew what poverty I'm in! I know nothing but what you know; I guess nothing except by what I see and feel. But my soul, despite its darkness, is in astonishing peace."
- Cards: F-EW-1639, F-OW-324
- Best details:
  - "E-P6-373! If you knew my poverty!" (F-EW-1639).
  - « Si vous saviez dans quelle pauvreté je suis ! » (F-OW-324).
- Evidence: Céline (1915). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 24.9.10). Medium-high. Against legend-building: she denies any foreknowledge.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-374 — 24 September 1897, infirmary — The chestnut trees pruned; Dr de Cornière: "an angel"; the seraphim's wings
- What happened: She told of an old sorrow: one year the chestnut trees were pruned far too late; "I loved the shade so much! ...the branches already green lay in bundles on the ground". Then she thought: "If I were in another Carmel, what would it matter to me if they cut down even all the chestnut trees of the Carmel of Lisieux!" and felt peace. Dr de Cornière told the prioress: "She's an angel! Her face isn't altered despite her great sufferings. I've never seen that. With her general emaciation, it's supernatural." Thérèse: "I wanted to tell M. de Cornière: I'm laughing because you couldn't stop me going to Heaven after all; but for your trouble, when I'm there I'll stop you going there too soon." "If I go among the Seraphim I won't do as they do... all cover themselves with their wings before God; I'll take good care not to cover myself with my wings."
- Cards: F-OW-323
- Best details:
  - « quand j'y serai je vous empêcherai d'y aller si tôt » (F-OW-323).
  - "I'll take good care not to cover myself with my wings" (F-OW-323).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 24.9.2-3, 24.9.5, 24.9.7). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-375 — Sept 1897, infirmary (25 Sept) — "Writing fine things about suffering is nothing"
- What happened: On a day of great pain she told Pauline that writing beautiful things about suffering is nothing; you must be in it to know. Pauline was troubled. On 25 Sept Thérèse, as if remembering, told her she now felt that all she had said and written was true. The Carnet jaune (25 Sep) adds: "I feel now that what I said and wrote is true on every point... It's true I wanted to suffer much for God, and it's true that I still want it." Also: "If I had died at 80, if I had been in China, everywhere, I would have died, I feel sure, as little as today." "For the little ones, they will be judged with extreme gentleness."
- Cards: F-EW-1582, F-OW-325
- Best details:
  - « qu'est-ce que c'est que d'écrire de belles choses sur la souffrance! Ce n'est rien, rien! » (F-EW-1582).
  - 25 Sept: she now felt that all she had said and written was true (F-EW-1582).
  - "For the little ones, they will be judged with extreme gentleness" (F-OW-325).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (heard; NV f. 289r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 25.9.1-3). Medium: Agnès says she was "painfully" struck by the first saying and records the second as its correction; the pairing may be her arrangement.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes (the first saying)
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-376 — Sept 1897 (a few days before death); fulfilment 1905-1914 — "After me there will be a harvest of the young"
- What happened: A few days before her death she told Céline, "I am absolutely sure of it", that after her there would be "a harvest of the young" in the Carmel. In the following years only old nuns died. Céline believes it came true from 1905. She names Marie de l'Eucharistie (d. 14 April 1905, aged 34), Mother Marie-Ange of the Child Jesus (prioress, d. aged 28 after starting the diocesan process) and Mother Isabelle du Sacré-Cœur (subprioress, d. aged 32). Many young recruits entered.
- Cards: F-EW-1600
- Best details:
  - « Après moi, il y aura une moisson de jeunes, au Carmel » (F-EW-1600).
- Evidence: Céline (heard; 1915). High for the saying; its meaning is ambiguous (deaths or vocations).
- Conflicts: Ambiguity of "harvest": deaths or vocations (card).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-377 — 26-27 Sept 1897; 30 Sept 1897 — The dead leaf on a spider's thread
- What happened: Looking out of the window at a dead leaf hanging in the air on a thin thread: "Look, it's my image; my life hangs by no more than a light thread." Mère Agnès adds that on the evening of 30 Sep, after her death, the leaf fell and she picked it up "with its spider's thread still attached". On 27 Sep, between two and three o'clock, offered a drink, she asked for Lourdes water: "Until 3 o'clock I prefer Lourdes water, it's more pious."
- Cards: F-OW-327
- Best details:
  - « Voyez, c'est mon image, ma vie ne tient plus qu'à un léger fil. » (F-OW-327).
  - The leaf fell on the evening of 30 Sep and was picked up "with its spider's thread still attached" (F-OW-327).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (CJ 26.9, 27.9). Medium: the falling leaf is Mère Agnès's report.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-378 — c. 27 Sept 1897, infirmary — "I am surprised there are not more atheists who take their own lives"
- What happened: Three days before her death MT found her in such suffering that it broke her heart. Thérèse forced a smile and, breathless, said: without faith she could never bear such suffering. She was surprised there were not more atheists who killed themselves.
- Cards: F-EW-1641
- Best details:
  - « Je suis étonnée qu'il n'y en ait pas davantage parmi les athées qui se donnent la mort. » (F-EW-1641).
- Evidence: MT (heard; PO 17 f. 1096r; PA 21). High. See E-Q10; the Last Conversations record similar words. Compare E-P6-342 (late Aug).
- Conflicts: Pauline dates a similar saying to late August (F-EW-1516).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-379 — late Sept 1897 (28 Sept for the holy-water scene), infirmary — One night of the devil's assaults; the blessed candle
- What happened: She had no outward attacks of the devil, except once, a whole night, which she revealed to Céline. In the morning Céline found her pale and disfigured by suffering and anguish. Our Lord had asked her to suffer for a soul designated to her, and the devil wanted to oppose it. Deeply struck, Céline lit the blessed candle. Soon calm returned, though the new physical pain was not taken away. Marie's account, two days before death (28 Sept): she asked for holy water, saying she suffered so much she could not make the slightest movement, as if held by a hand of iron. She asked for prayer and said she thought the devil was increasing her pain to make her despair. She was not suffering for herself but for another, "and he does not want". Other dated versions of the same theme. In August 1897, feeling the devil near, she asked the infirmarian to sprinkle holy water on her bed and quoted the Compline hymn "Procul recedant somnia et noctium phantasmata". The infirmarian lit a blessed candle. Laveille has: "The demon is near me; I do not see him, but I feel his presence… he increases my pain in order to lead me to despair… And I cannot pray. I can only look at the Blessed Virgin and say 'Jesus.'" The infirmarian lit a blessed candle and the presence "fled".
- Cards: F-EW-1606, F-EW-1642, F-EW-2105, F-LF-235
- Best details:
  - Céline lit the blessed candle (F-EW-1606).
  - « Ce n'est pas pour moi que je souffre, c'est pour un autre... et il ne veut pas... » (F-EW-1642).
  - "a hand of iron" (F-EW-1642).
  - "I can only look at the Blessed Virgin and say 'Jesus.'" (F-LF-235).
  - The Compline hymn "Procul recedant somnia et noctium phantasmata" (F-EW-2105).
- Evidence: Céline (present in the morning; heard from Thérèse; 1915). Medium-high. | Marie (heard, 1915). Medium-high: "words as reported by Marie", not in the DE record in this form. | De Teil Articles and the Epilogue (D6 p. 206); Laveille. High.
- Conflicts: Parallels Marie's holy-water scene "for another... and he does not want" (F-EW-1642, later chunk). Céline uses a blessed candle, Marie holy water: possibly the same night. | "Another" is not identified (Hyacinthe Loyson is often suggested elsewhere; not in this text). | Dating: August (De Teil, Laveille) versus late September (Céline) versus 28 Sept (Marie). These may be one night or several.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-380 — Sept 1897, infirmary — The confessor: "What an angel! She is confirmed in grace"
- What happened: Canon Faucon, who heard her last confession and stood in for the sick chaplain, entered the infirmary "as if it were a sanctuary". He saw her beautiful and transfigured in pain and said to Mère Agnès: "What an angel! She is confirmed in grace." Her veil was raised that day because of her breathlessness. Faucon heard her last confession on 29 September, the eve of her death, because the ordinary confessor, Abbé Youf, was gravely ill. He entered the infirmary "as a sanctuary". In the middle of her pain she was so beautiful and calm she seemed already in heaven. Leaving the infirmary he said: what a beautiful soul; she seems confirmed in grace. Mme Guérin's letter of 30 Sept adds that she asked his blessing, smiling, and was fully lucid, though the night had been bad.
- Cards: F-EW-1577, F-EW-1645, F-EW-1646, F-EW-1693, F-EW-1694
- Best details:
  - "as if it were a sanctuary" (F-EW-1577).
  - She asked his blessing "with her little smiling look" (F-EW-1693).
  - Her face was still "angelic" and she was fully lucid (F-EW-1694).
- Evidence: Summarium 1919 via Laveille; Mère Agnès to Dolan. High. | Faucon (saw; PA-19); Mère Agnès (heard, 1915; absent in 1910); Mme Guérin's letter of 30 Sept 1897. High.
- Conflicts: Date is only "Sept 1897"; another card gives Faucon's last confession as 29 Sept (F-EW-1329). | Youf died 7 Oct 1897 (not stated in the file). No witness in this batch records the last words or the final hour.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-381 — late Sep 1897; 29 Sep about 9 p.m., infirmary window — The robin and the turtledove
- What happened: A robin came through the window and flew about her bed. On the evening of 29 Sept, about 9 p.m., a turtledove landed on the window sill and cooed a long time. She and Céline recalled "the voice of the turtle is heard in our land". Laveille repeats the turtle-dove on the night of 29 Sep, about 9 p.m., cooing on the sill, and the robin at the window.
- Cards: F-EW-1578, F-LF-236
- Best details:
  - The turtledove at 9 p.m. on 29 Sept, the last evening (F-EW-1578).
  - Robin at the window; turtle-dove at 9 p.m. (F-LF-236).
- Evidence: Céline (Summarium 1919, via Laveille); Prioress's Epilogue mentions the robin. High. | Laveille (Summarium 1919 p. 929). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-382 — 29 Sept 1897; after — A dying nun asked to pray for a church-building
- What happened: Fr Granger, a diocesan missionary who thought her a saint, asked Faucon to request her prayers for two special graces. She promised simply and humbly. Faucon later learned Granger obtained them; he thinks they concerned building the Sacred Heart church at Langannerie.
- Cards: F-EW-1647
- Best details:
  - She promised "simply and humbly" (F-EW-1647). One of her last recorded acts.
- Evidence: Faucon (heard her promise; PA-19 [1087]). Medium on the object of the graces ("I believe").
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-383 — 29 Sept 1897, infirmary — The eve of death: "Mother, is this the agony? I'll never know how to die"
- What happened: On 29 Sept from morning she seemed in agony, with a painful rattle. At noon she asked the prioress: "Mother, is this the agony? How shall I die? I'll never know how to die!" Pauline read her, in French, the office of St Michael and the prayers for the dying. At the mention of demons she made a childlike threatening gesture and smiled. After the doctor's visit she asked "Is it today, Mother?" She was told yes, and that God was joyful, and said "Me too!" In the afternoon: "I can't go on! Let people pray for me!" After Matins she joined her hands: "Yes, my God, I accept everything!" The Carnet jaune (29 Sep) adds details. From the morning she seemed in agony, with a painful rattle; the community said the prayers of the Manual around her bed for about an hour. At noon to the prioress: "Mother, is this the agony?... How am I to die? I'll never know how to die!..." At the prayers for the dying, at the mention of demons she made a childlike threatening gesture and said, smiling, "Oh! Oh!" After the doctor: "Is it today, Mother?" "Yes, my little girl." An insect got into her sleeve: "Leave it, it doesn't matter... I know these little creatures." "Oh Mother, how the nerves hurt!" Late at night, hands joined: "Yes, my God, yes my God, I accept everything!" "Not atrocious, Mother, but much, much... just what I can bear." She asked to be alone at night; the prioress refused; Marie and Céline shared the night watch. Marie de la Trinité was sent away gently.
- Cards: F-EW-1644, F-OW-329
- Best details:
  - « Oui, mon Dieu, oui, mon Dieu, je veux bien tout!!! » (F-EW-1644).
  - The childlike threatening gesture at the mention of demons (F-EW-1644).
  - "Is it today, Mother?" — "Me too!" (F-EW-1644).
  - « Comment fais-je faire pour mourir ? Jamais je ne vais savoir mourir !... » (F-OW-329).
  - The insect in her sleeve: "I know these little creatures." (F-OW-329).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 289v-290r; f. 245v-246r). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 29.9.1-11). Medium-high.
- Conflicts: Pauline's NV puts "Yes, my God, I accept everything" after Matins in the afternoon; the Carnet jaune puts it late at night (F-EW-1644).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-384 — night of 29-30 Sept 1897, infirmary — The last night: watchers allowed, the little glass held
- What happened: On 29 Sept she asked to be left alone at night; the prioress refused. Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur and Sr Geneviève shared the night watch. The Sisters watched her the last night of her life; she kept worrying about their fatigue and tried to help herself so as not to disturb them. On her last night Marie and Céline stayed with her despite her pleas that they rest in the next room as usual. After giving her a drink they dozed off. She stayed holding her little glass in her hand until one of them woke. A hospital nun (Sr Agatha) told Dolan a version in which Marie fell asleep, and Thérèse held the glass for hours rather than wake her.
- Cards: F-EW-1643, F-EW-1667, F-EW-1674, F-EW-1678, F-EW-1650, F-EW-1618, F-EW-1592
- Best details:
  - « elle resta, tenant son petit verre à la main, jusqu'à ce que l'une de nous s'éveillât » (F-EW-1667).
  - She kept worrying about their fatigue (F-EW-1674).
  - Sr Agatha's version: held the glass "for hours" (F-EW-1650).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw, NV f. 289v-290r); Céline (present; 1910, 1915); Marie (present, 1915); Marie du Sacré-Cœur (notes); Sr Agatha to Dolan (hearsay). High for the core; low-medium for the Agatha version.
- Conflicts: Marie (1910, F-EW-1592) and Marie du Sacré-Cœur's notes (F-EW-1618) say she refused watchers even on the eve of death. Céline, Marie (1915) and Pauline say the sisters DID watch the last night. The glass: Céline says both dozed; Sr Agatha says Marie alone, and the three Carmelites confirmed "in order not to disturb Céline" though Marie said she did not remember telling anyone. Céline, not Marie, slept next to the infirmary.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the dying girl holding a glass rather than wake a sleeping sister.

### E-P6-385 — 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary — The last words as Godefroy heard them
- What happened: Fr Godefroy says that in her agony, eyes on the crucifix, she told her prioress "I look and I love!", and died holding the crucifix saying "My God, my God, yes, I love you." He says the ecclesiastical superior gave her the last sacraments and told him how moved he was by her serenity. She received communion daily as long as she could.
- Cards: F-EW-1331
- Best details:
  - « Je regarde et j'aime! » (F-EW-1331).
- Evidence: Godefroy, second-hand ("the Mother Prioress kept me specially informed"). Low: hearsay, some details wrong.
- Conflicts: The standard record: Extreme Unction (30 July) was given by the chaplain Abbé Youf, not the superior; daily communion stopped in August 1897. His form of the last words adds "oui".
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-386 — morning of 30 Sept 1897, infirmary — The last morning: "pure suffering, no consolation"
- What happened: On the morning of 30 September Pauline stayed with her during Mass. She was exhausted and panting. Looking at the statue of the Virgin who had smiled on her as a child, she said she had prayed to her with fervour, but it was agony without any mixture of consolation, "not one". Pauline felt there was nothing bitter in this complaint. Marie says that on the last day her tongue was completely dried, and she suffered so much that the prioress let all three blood sisters stay near her. She seemed abandoned by heaven and earth. Mme Guérin wrote that the night had been bad. Marie's letter of the morning (via Piat and Mme Guérin's letter to Jeanne): "It is really a little victim that God has chosen. In the midst of her sufferings she has always the same face, the same angelic air." Isidore Guérin said of her expected death: "This eventuality that saddens us is the dawn of a triumph." Marie followed the agony in detail and reported it at the parlour grille where her parents came daily for news. Laveille's account of 30 Sept: "it is pure agony without any measure of consolation." The Carnet jaune (30 Sep) has the morning words while she was alone with Mère Agnès during Mass: "Oh! I prayed to her with such fervour! But it is pure agony, without any mixture of consolation." Céline's own record (Dernières paroles à Céline, 30 Sept) covers the same day in two parts.
- Cards: F-EW-1652, F-EW-1656, F-EW-1658, F-EW-1675, F-EW-1676, F-EW-1665, F-EW-2640, F-LF-237, F-OW-330
- Best details:
  - « c'est bien la souffrance toute pure, parce qu'il n'y a pas de consolation, non: pas une » (F-EW-1675).
  - "Oh, I prayed to her so fervently! But it's pure agony, with no consolation." (F-EW-1656).
  - Her tongue was completely dried (F-EW-1676).
  - "the dawn of a triumph" (F-EW-2640).
  - « Si c'est ça l'agonie, qu'est-ce que c'est que la mort ? !... » (F-OW-330).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; 1910, 1915); Marie (present; 1915). High. | Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters; Laveille. Medium-high. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.9); Céline (Dernières paroles). High: two recorders agree in substance.
- Conflicts: Céline's list gives "the vase is full to the brim" where Mère Agnès has "the chalice". Céline also records "I can't go on... and yet I must last... I am... I am reduced...", absent from Mère Agnès's version. Novissima Verba has "the chalice is full to overflowing".
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-387 — 30 Sept 1897, 3 p.m., infirmary — Three o'clock: arms outstretched like the crucified
- What happened: In mid-afternoon she felt strange pains in all her limbs. She put one arm on Mother Agnès's shoulder and gave the other to Céline to hold, and stayed so a few moments. Three o'clock struck, and the Sisters were moved, for she seemed an image of Jesus on the cross. Pauline says she stretched her arms in a cross, and the prioress laid a picture of Our Lady of Mount Carmel on her knees. During the day she said she felt strong and did not think she would die, and cried: "If this is the agony, what is death?" and "the cup is full to the brim". She said: "Mother, present me quickly to the Blessed Virgin; prepare me to die well." She said she would never have believed it possible to suffer so much and could only explain it by her burning desire to save souls; she did not regret giving herself to Love. Pauline notes that from 3 pm "it seemed it was no longer she who suffered". The Carnet jaune, afternoon: she could sit up: "See what strength I have today! No, I'm not going to die! I have months more, perhaps years!" Asked if she would accept it: "I'd have to..." then, falling back: "I accept it!" Mère Agnès wrote down exclamations "impossible to render the tone of": "I don't believe in death for me any more... I believe only in suffering... Well, so much the better!" "If this is the agony, what is death?!" "Oh Mother, I assure you the chalice is full to the brim!" "But God won't abandon me... He has never abandoned me." Given a picture of Our Lady of Mount Carmel: "Mother, present me quickly to the Blessed Virgin; I'm a baby who can't go on!... Prepare me to die well." Told her humility was her preparation: "Yes, it seems to me I have never sought anything but the truth; yes, I have understood humility of heart... It seems to me I am humble." "All I wrote about my desires for suffering, oh! it's true all the same!... And I don't repent of having given myself to Love." Sr Geneviève's own record: in the afternoon, with only Mère Agnès and herself present, Thérèse, trembling, called them to help her; suffering in all her muscles, she put one arm on Mère Agnès's shoulder and the other on Céline's, and stayed "with her arms in a cross". "At that very moment 3 o'clock struck."
- Cards: F-EW-1671, F-EW-1679, F-EW-1652, F-EW-1656, F-EW-1657, F-EW-1659, F-EW-1665, F-EW-1668, F-EW-1680, F-EW-1700, F-EW-1701, F-OW-330, F-OW-331
- Best details:
  - « A ce moment, 3 heures sonnèrent » (F-EW-1671).
  - « 0 ma mère, présentez-moi bien vite à la Sainte Vierge! Préparez-moi à bien mourir. » (F-EW-1656).
  - « Demain, ce sera encore pire! Enfin, tant mieux! » (F-EW-1668).
  - « Non, je n'aurais jamais cru qu'on pouvait tant souffrir...jamais, jamais! » (F-EW-1700). Céline: explained by her desire to save souls.
  - "Yes, it seems to me I never sought anything but the truth. Yes, I understood humility of heart" (F-EW-1657).
  - « elle resta ainsi les bras en Croix. A ce moment-là, même, 3 heures sonnèrent » (F-OW-331).
  - "No, I'm not going to die! I have months more, perhaps years!" then "I accept it!" (F-OW-330).
- Evidence: Céline (saw; 1910, 1915, CS); Mère Agnès (saw and heard; NV f. 290r-291r); Marie (1910). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.9); Céline (Dernières paroles à Céline, 30 Septembre). High (two recorders); medium for the cross-arms detail (Céline's later written account).
- Conflicts: Céline says "vase" (vessel), Marie and Pauline "calice" (cup). Céline and Pauline give speech in the agony; Marie says she did not speak from five o'clock (see the E-P6-390 event). | The arms in a cross are not in Mère Agnès's Carnet jaune entry (F-OW-331 notes); Pauline's NV puts them at about 3 pm with the prioress laying a picture on her knees (F-EW-1652).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-388 — 30 Sept 1897, afternoon, infirmary — MT sees the agony and leaves
- What happened: On the day of her death MT came to the infirmary (PO: after Vespers; PA: about 3 pm). The agony was terrible. Thérèse's hands were all purple. She clasped them in anguish and cried out in a voice made clear and strong by pain, calling on God and Mary. She said the cup was full to the brim and she would never know how to die. The prioress said courage, it is almost over. Thérèse said no, it might last months. Asked if she would accept that, she said "Je le veux bien", and her head fell back on the pillow, calm. MT left, unable to bear it. MT was absent for the last words "My God, I love you".
- Cards: F-EW-1685
- Best details:
  - « Le calice est plein… plein jusqu'au bord!… Jamais je ne vais savoir mourir!… » (F-EW-1685).
  - Purple hands clasped in anguish (F-EW-1685).
- Evidence: MT (saw and heard; PO 17 f. 1096v; PA 21 p. 1279). High. The words are in Histoire d'une âme ch. 12 and Last Conversations; the purple hands are new.
- Conflicts: Time: after Vespers (PO) versus about 3 pm (PA).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-389 — 30 Sept 1897, 3 to 5 p.m., infirmary — The agony begins; the community gathers; the ice on her lips
- What happened: The agony began soon after 3 p.m. (Céline, Marie des Anges, MdA 1915), or about 4:30 p.m. (Pauline 1915, MdA 1911, Prioress's Epilogue), or about 5 p.m. (Pauline 1910). Pauline saw a sudden pallor and the community gathered. She held her crucifix so tightly that it was hard to take it from her after death. Her face and hands, first deathly pale, turned red-violet. For over two hours a terrible rattle tore her chest; her hands were purple, her feet icy, and she shook in all her limbs. Heavy cold sweat soaked her face, clothes, the pillows and the blankets. She thanked the community with a smile. Sr Geneviève came to wipe the sweat from her forehead, and Thérèse thanked her with a smile and a look. Her mouth seemed so dry that Céline put a little piece of ice on her lips. Thérèse gave her an indescribable look and smile, as if to comfort her and say goodbye. At the 6 pm Angelus she raised her eyes to the statue of the Virgin. Marie des Anges says all the little birds gathered at the infirmary window to sing. TSA, towards 4:30 p.m., saw the shadows of death spread over her face and, about 5 p.m., received a farewell look full of tenderness; on the first day of the agony she left the infirmary to hide her tears. Sr Cécile's note says that on 30 Sept Agnès prayed before the Sacred Heart statue that Thérèse be given strength to endure her agony to the end. Laveille and the Carmel's printed account (via Taylor) give more times. Laveille: about 3 p.m. she held out her arms like a cross and the prioress placed an image of Our Lady of Mount Carmel on her knees; about 5 p.m. the bell called the community; for two hours the death-rattle tore her chest and the bedcovers were soaked; Céline cooled her lips with a small piece of ice. The Carmel account (Taylor): at 4.30 pm she entered her agony; at the evening Angelus she looked at the statue of the Virgin. Céline received "a look of infinite tenderness". Sr Geneviève wet her lips with a bit of ice (Laveille). The Carnet jaune (30 Sep): about 5 p.m. her face changed; Mère Agnès understood it was the last agony. The community came in; she greeted them with a smile, holding and constantly looking at her crucifix. For more than two hours "a terrible rattle tore her chest"; her face was congested, hands purple, feet icy; she trembled in every limb; heavy sweat ran down her face; she gave small involuntary cries to breathe. Robins and other birds sang loudly outside the window for a long time; Mère Agnès prayed they would stop. Céline put a piece of ice to her lips; she smiled at her "as a supreme farewell". At the 6 o'clock Angelus she looked long at the statue of the Virgin. Around 5 p.m. Mère Marie de Gonzague knocked down the relics of Vénard and Anne of Jesus pinned to the curtain; they were picked up and she caressed them.
- Cards: F-EW-1651, F-EW-1652, F-EW-1653, F-EW-1660, F-EW-1672, F-EW-1680, F-EW-1697, F-EW-1699, F-EW-1703, F-EW-1657, F-EW-1692, F-LF-237, F-OM-014, F-OW-332
- Best details:
  - A little piece of ice on her lips; "an indescribable look and smile" (F-EW-1653).
  - She held the crucifix so tightly it was hard to take from her after death (F-EW-1660).
  - "all the little birds had gathered at the infirmary window to sing" (F-EW-1697).
  - TSA: a farewell look at about 5 pm (F-EW-1699).
  - Agnès prayed before the Sacred Heart statue given 21 June 1878 by a novice who left (Marie Gahéry) that Thérèse be given strength to the end (F-EW-1703).
  - "a look of infinite tenderness" (F-OM-014).
  - « Pendant plus de deux heures, un râle terrible déchira sa poitrine. » (F-OW-332).
  - Gonzague knocked down the pinned relics of Vénard and Anne of Jesus; Thérèse caressed them when they were picked up (F-OW-332).
  - Birds sang loudly outside; Mère Agnès prayed they would stop (F-OW-332).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; 1910, 1915, Epilogue); Céline (saw); Marie des Anges; TSA (present, medium: personal reading of a look); MdA (saw). High for the core; medium for times. | Laveille (pp. 396-399; eyewitness source type); Carmel account printed by Taylor (PDF 265-266). High for the sequence; medium for exact wording. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.9 and its appendix "Paroles retrouvées"); Céline. High for the main facts (several witnesses); the "last look" is Céline's interpretation.
- Conflicts: Start of the agony: 3 p.m. (Céline, Marie des Anges, MdA 1915), 4:30 p.m. (Pauline 1915, MdA 1911), about 5 p.m. (Pauline 1910). Marie says she did not speak from five o'clock. Birds are Marie des Anges's perception. | Agony start: 4.30 pm (Carmel account via Taylor) versus about 5 p.m. bell (Laveille), see the other times given in this event. | Céline says Thérèse had promised her last look to the prioress "if God leaves me free", but in fact looked at Céline "with a prophetic insistence" a few minutes before death, then lowered her eyes to seek the prioress. Marie des Anges says the birds gathered to sing (Marie des Anges's perception); Pauline says she prayed the birds would stop.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-390 — 30 Sept 1897, from about 7 p.m. to 7:20 p.m., infirmary — "I would not want to suffer less": the community sent away and called back; the last words
- What happened: Just after 7 p.m. the prioress, thinking her state was stable, sent the community away. Céline calls this a cruel trial, since the dying girl saw her release delayed. Thérèse asked whether this was not the agony and whether she was not going to die. Told it might last some hours, she said "Well… come on… come on… Oh, I would not want to suffer less!" Her breathing became weaker and faster. She fell back on the pillow, head leaning to the right. The infirmary bell rang and the prioress hurriedly called the community back. Hardly had the Sisters knelt round the bed when she said clearly, looking at her crucifix, "Oh! I love Him!" and a moment later "My God… I… love you!" These were her last words. Marie says she did not speak from five o'clock and that at 7:15, with a broken voice, she said "My God, I love you". Aimée alone did not hear the bell. Céline adds that in her agony she gave Céline a delightful smile and a long, penetrating look, and that the community "shivered". Thérèse had said her last look would go to the prioress; she then looked for her and lowered her eyes on her with her usual expression. Laveille and the Carmel account give the sequence of the last minutes. About 7 p.m. she asked, "Am I not going to die?" The prioress said yes. She said: "Well then, let it be so. Oh, I would not wish to suffer less." Gazing at the crucifix: "Oh… I LOVE Him! My God, I… LOVE… THEE!" Laveille calls these her last words. The Carmel account adds "fully twelve hours" of agony and gives the last words at a quarter past seven. The papal bull says, "as it is reported" (uti fertur), that she was wounded by a flame of heavenly fire and died repeating "My God, I love you"; the Office lesson has "as she herself relates" (ut ipsa refert). The canonization homily says that shortly before death she could say "that she had given God nothing but love". Piat gives 7.15 p.m. "in a last cry: I love you". Both recorders' texts of the last exchange (Carnet jaune and Céline's list): after the prioress sent the community away (7 p.m. and some minutes), Thérèse sighed: "Mother! Isn't this the agony yet?... Am I not going to die?..." — "Yes, my poor little one, it's the agony, but God perhaps wants to prolong it a few hours." She answered with courage: "Well!... come on!... Come on!... Oh! I would not want to suffer less long..." And looking at her crucifix: "Oh! I love him!... My God... I love you....." Then she fell gently back, head leaning to the right. Mère Agnès's text gives « Oh ! je l'aime !...... Mon Dieu... je vous aime..... ». Sr Geneviève's list gives « Eh bien, allons ! allons ! oh ! je ne voudrais pas moins souffrir !.... Oh ! je l'aime.... « Mon Dieu... je... vous... aime ! » ». The Carmel editors give 7:20 p.m.
- Cards: F-EW-1654, F-EW-1661, F-EW-1662, F-EW-1673, F-EW-1682, F-EW-1669, F-EW-1681, F-EW-1704, F-EW-1651, F-EW-1657, F-EW-1666, F-EW-1677, F-EW-1683, F-EW-1692, F-LF-237, F-OM-013, F-OM-014, F-EW-2640, F-OW-333
- Best details:
  - « Oh! je ne voudrais pas moins souffrir! » (F-EW-1673).
  - « Oh!... je l'aime!... Mon... Dieu! je... vous... aime!! » (F-EW-1657).
  - The prioress sent the community away and had to call them back by a loud bell (F-EW-1654, F-EW-1662, F-EW-1692).
  - « elle fixa sur moi un long et pénétrant regard. La communauté, qui était présente, eut un frémissement. » (F-EW-1669).
  - Aimée regretted all her life that she alone did not hear the bell (F-EW-1704).
  - Bull wording: « Tunc, uti fertur, caelestis ignis flamma vulnerata est … ferventissime ingeminans "Deus meus, Te diligo" » (F-OM-013).
  - "that she had given God nothing but love" (F-OM-014, AAS 17 p. 213).
  - « Oh ! je l'aime !...... Mon Dieu... je vous aime..... » (F-OW-333, Mère Agnès).
  - « Eh bien, allons ! allons ! oh ! je ne voudrais pas moins souffrir !.... » (F-OW-333, Céline).
  - "Yes, my poor little one, it's the agony, but God perhaps wants to prolong it a few hours." (F-OW-333).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw and heard; 1910, 1915, Epilogue); Céline (saw; 1910, 1915); Marie (present); MdA (saw). High for the words; medium for Céline's "last look" detail. | Laveille; the Carmel's printed account via Taylor; papal documents (official). High for the wording. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.9); Céline (Dernières paroles); Carmel editors' health note (19h20). High (two independent recorders in the community).
- Conflicts: Time of death: 7:15 (Marie), about seven (Pauline 1910, MT), 7:20 (Céline 1915, Pauline 1915), 7:30 (TSA). Marie says she did not speak from five; Céline and Pauline record speech in the agony. Céline's "last look for me, not the prioress" is self-favouring and told only by Céline (C-F193); Marie does not mention it. Scene S-EW-042 / S-EW-034 in the cards. | Time: 7.15 (Carmel account, Piat) versus about 7.20 (Laveille). The bull's careful "uti fertur" / "ut ipsa refert" signals it does not claim eyewitness wording (F-OM-013 notes). | Céline's version marks a pause ("My God... I... love you!"). "I would not want to suffer less long" versus "I would not want to suffer less".
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-391 — 30 Sept 1897, about 7:20 p.m., infirmary — The last gaze: "for the space of a Credo"; the candle held to her eyes
- What happened: After "My God, I love you" she suddenly sank, head to the right. Then she straightened as if called, opened her eyes and fixed them, radiant, a little above the Virgin's statue, for about the time it takes to say a Creed slowly. Her face, purple and distorted during the agony, recovered its lily colour. Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (or, in Marie's account, an unnamed Sister) held a candle close to her eyes to see her gaze; her eyelids did not move. Marie says the look reminded her of the 1883 cure. Céline read in the look beatitude, great astonishment and a noble assurance, and thought they had witnessed her judgment. TSA says she could not keep looking at her; the look was not frightening but radiant. MM, MT and MJ describe the same gaze. MdA thought she saw Our Lord (1911) or the Virgin coming for her at the opening of the month of the Rosary (1915). Then she closed her eyes, sighed several times, and died. Laveille and the Carmel account: she sank back, raised herself as if called, opened her eyes with "heavenly peace and indescribable joy", and gazed above the statue "for a space sufficient to recite a Credo". Then her head fell back. She died about 7.20 p.m. on 30 Sep 1897, aged 24 years and 9 months.
- Cards: F-EW-1655, F-EW-1663, F-EW-1666, F-EW-1670, F-EW-1677, F-EW-1683, F-EW-1686, F-EW-1688, F-EW-1690, F-EW-1691, F-EW-1692, F-EW-1698, F-LF-237, F-OM-014
- Best details:
  - « le temps de réciter lentement un credo » (F-EW-1670).
  - « pendant l'espace d'un Credo, l'extase transfigura ses traits » (F-EW-1691).
  - « elle redressa la tête avec une force tout à fait étrange » (F-EW-1692).
  - « une sœur mit un flambeau tout près de ses yeux, mais son regard resta aussi limpide » (F-EW-1666).
  - MM, who had assisted many dying Sisters, never saw anything like it (F-EW-1688).
  - MJ was not at the agony but present when, at the last breath, Thérèse opened her eyes and fixed them on something invisible (F-EW-1690).
  - "a space sufficient to recite a Credo" (F-LF-237).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw; 1910, 1915); Céline (saw; interpretation her own); Marie (saw); MdA (saw); TSA; MT; MM; MJ; Marie des Anges. High for what was seen; the "ecstasy" is interpretation. Pauline calls it an ecstasy that broke her chain. | Laveille; Carmel account via Taylor. High for the sequence; medium for exact wording.
- Conflicts: Who held the candle: Marie de l'Eucharistie (Pauline's NV notebook, 1915) versus "a sister" in the oral account and in Marie's account; Céline does not mention the candle. Eyes "opened wide" (MdA 1911) or "half-opened" (MdA 1915); Our Lord versus the Virgin. Pauline (oral 1910): she saw her close her eyes, sigh several times, and die. Time of death: see the E-P6-390 event.
- New to English readers: partial (the candle test and Marie Guérin's name)
- Scene potential: high: the candle passes before her eyes and the lids do not move.

### E-P6-392 — evening of 30 Sept 1897, infirmary — The face after death; laying out; the worn toque
- What happened: After death a reflection of bliss marked her face; she had a heavenly smile. Céline says that from her lowered eyelids radiated such intensity of life and happiness that it was no longer death at all; she never saw this again on any other dead person. MT had never seen her so beautiful in life. No one had to close her eyes; she closed them herself after the ecstasy. The prioress sent the community out. Sr Aimée de Jésus, Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur and Pauline laid out the body. Her face looked like a child's; she seemed about 12. Dressed and laid on her straw mattress (Carmel custom), she had her crucifix, rosary and a palm in her hand, and nearby on a small table the statue of the miraculous Virgin. Her head still leaned to the right, and her smile was so marked she seemed only asleep. She died holding the small crucifix Mère Agnès had given her at her profession in place of her larger one. Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste, who had mended worn wimples for her "intimate friends" the lay Sisters, brought out a worn toque for the laying-out. Agnès nearly cried, but Thérèse's smile in death seemed to say it did not matter. The Carnet jaune (30 Sep): the prioress rang the infirmary bell to call back the community, saying "Open all the doors." The Sisters knelt and, per Mère Agnès, saw "the ecstasy": her face regained its lily colour, her eyes were fixed upward "shining with peace and joy". She made "certain beautiful movements of the head, as if Someone had wounded her divinely with an arrow of love". Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie brought a candle close: no movement of the eyelids. It lasted "about the space of a Creed", and she breathed her last. Afterwards she kept "a heavenly smile"; she held the crucifix so tightly it had to be pulled from her hands. Marie, Agnès and Sr Aimée de Jésus laid her out and noted she looked no more than 12 or 13. Her limbs stayed supple until burial on Monday 4 Oct 1897.
- Cards: F-EW-1684, F-EW-1687, F-EW-1664, F-EW-1702, F-EW-1705, F-OW-334
- Best details:
  - "from her lowered eyelids radiated such intensity of life and happiness that it was no longer death at all" (F-EW-1684).
  - Her face looked like a child's; she seemed about 12 (F-EW-1664).
  - The worn toque, from a Sister whom she had asked to count her among her intimate friends: « considérez-moi, je vous en prie, comme du nombre de vos intimes amies ! » (F-EW-1705).
  - Crucifix, rosary and palm in her hand; the miraculous Virgin's statue on a small table (F-EW-1664).
  - « Cette extase dura à peu près l'espace d'un Credo, et elle rendit le dernier soupir. » (F-OW-334).
  - "as if Someone had wounded her divinely with an arrow of love" (F-OW-334).
  - "Open all the doors." (F-OW-334).
- Evidence: Céline (saw); MT (saw); Mère Agnès (did it; PA 6 [513]-[514]; also PA 485, Cahiers verts 125). High. | Mère Agnès (CJ 30.9). Medium: interpretive language ("ecstasy"); recorded 1904-1923 in its final form. Novissima Verba reports the "twelve or thirteen" detail and "Open all the doors".
- Conflicts: Sr Aimée de Jésus was not close to the Martins; she testified herself (PA 17). Compare Marie F-EW-1729 (later).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-393 — 1897–1910 — Perfumes smelled by the nuns
- What happened: Almost all the nuns smelled mysterious perfumes at various times after her death. Marie herself did four or five times, always when not thinking of it. She says she attaches less importance to these than to an inner grace.
- Cards: F-EW-1246
- Best details:
  - « je ne me préoccupais pas du tout de ces phénomènes » (F-EW-1246).
- Evidence: Marie (own experience, 1910). High as testimony.
- Conflicts: Pauline calls the perfumes "secondary and hard to prove" (1915) after saying nearly all nuns had perceived them (1910), see F-EW-1243.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-394 — after 30 Sept 1897, Carmel (October 1897) — The patched rope sandals burned
- What happened: Thérèse mended her alpargates "until extinction". The Sister in charge of the rope sandals gave her the worst; the soles had patch on patch and became so heavy nobody else would wear them. Thérèse herself darned the canvas so much the original cloth could not be seen. After her death two pairs were found so worn and patched that no Sister would have worn them. Marie wanted to keep them as relics, but a lay Sister (Marthe/MJ) snatched them, said "You won't keep that filth!", and threw them in the fire. She later regretted it. MJ says they would have shown her poverty to those who did not know her and "are not obliged to believe all we say".
- Cards: F-EW-1689, F-EW-1696, F-EW-1709
- Best details:
  - « Vous ne garderez pas ces saletés-là! » (F-EW-1709).
  - « combien je regrette de les avoir brûlées » (F-EW-1689).
- Evidence: Marie (present, 1915); MJ (her own act; PO 15); Marthe (notes). High. Shows relics were not yet collected systematically, and that not all saw her as a saint just after death (scene S-EW-032).
- Conflicts: The burner is called MJ (F-EW-1689) and Marthe (F-EW-1696); Marie says "a lay Sister" (F-EW-1709). Marie's F-EW-1277 claims no opposing opinion; this story sits awkwardly with it (F-EW-1709 is the alpargates story).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the sandals thrown on the fire.

### E-P6-395 — 30 Sept-Oct 1897 — Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul asks pardon at the dead Thérèse's feet
- What happened: Sr Saint-Vincent-de-Paul, a lay Sister (d. 1905), often hurt Thérèse with cutting remarks and said loudly enough for her to hear: what will anyone find to say about Sr Thérèse after her death? Once a lay Sister said crossly that Thérèse was putting her friends' big bouquets forward and hiding the poor people's; Thérèse smiled and put the servants' flowers in front; the lay Sister accused herself to the prioress. Right after Thérèse died, this Sister asked to be freed of a long cerebral anaemia, put her forehead on Thérèse's feet, begged pardon, and said she was cured. She then collected discarded photographs of Thérèse everywhere, "before her outside reputation had influenced opinions". She told all the Sisters this herself. TSA (1915) says the nun who said "not even a good religious" is dead; she was the Sister who, when Thérèse's body was exposed, came to ask pardon for having misjudged her, laid her forehead on Thérèse's feet, and was instantly cured of a "cerebral anaemia" of several years. TSA calls her one who had behaved "hurtfully". In her 1911 deposition TSA did not mention this cure. Laveille and Taylor report the same cure.
- Cards: F-EW-1707, F-EW-1708, F-EW-1719, F-LF-238, F-OM-015
- Best details:
  - « Qu'est-ce qu'on pourra bien dire de sœur Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus après sa mort? » (F-EW-1708).
  - She put her head on the dead nun's feet, asked pardon, and said she was cured (F-EW-1707, F-EW-1708).
  - Instant cure of several years' cerebral anaemia (F-EW-1719).
- Evidence: Céline (heard from the Sister; PO 4 f. 406r-v); Prioress's Epilogue (D6 p. 197). High (Céline); medium (Epilogue). | TSA (whether she saw is not stated); Laveille; Taylor. Medium: identification by TSA only; the source does not name the nun. See miracle F-M01.
- Conflicts: The reason for her hostility is not given. Miracle entry M-EW-032; see also C-M13. The remark is in the 1898 ch. 12; the cure and photos are new. | Laveille and Taylor identify the Sister with the wreaths scene of 1891; TSA and Céline (F-EW-1708) identify Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high: the sharp-tongued lay Sister at the feet of the dead nun.

### E-P6-397 — 1 October 1897, Carmel — Marie feels Thérèse's soul come near
- What happened: Marie says she omitted at the 1910 process, as too intimate a grace, that on the day after the death, after an act of charity, she felt Thérèse's soul come near hers with an inexpressible joy.
