### E-P6-398 — Fri 1 to Sun 3 Oct 1897, Carmel choir grille — The body exposed at the choir grille; the crowd; what was and was not seen
- What happened: On Friday afternoon (1 Oct) she was carried to the choir and exposed at the grille until Sunday evening. Her forehead was crowned with roses "as is the custom". On Saturday and Sunday many people came to pray, and they touched pious objects to her, "even jewels", rosaries, medals and crucifixes. Léonie saw the face of extraordinary beauty and the chapel full up to the altar steps, with "certainly far fewer" at the death of other Carmelites. She heard behind her: "How beautiful she is! One can hardly pray for her; one feels forced to invoke her." Marie des Anges says she was beautiful at the grille, though "not very like herself", but far more radiant at the lifting of the body ("levée du corps") at the infirmary door, so alive that she would not have been surprised to see her smile at the Child Jesus statue; she looked like a virgin martyr on her shrine. Marie saw the face grow graver at the grille. Pauline, Céline, Marie, Jeanne, TSA and Maupas saw nothing extraordinary and explain the crowd by the family living in Lisieux and by the custom of touching objects to a dead Carmelite. Pauline adds it happens at the death of other Carmelites too.
- Cards: F-EW-1715, F-EW-1716, F-EW-1717, F-EW-1718, F-EW-1720, F-EW-1721, F-EW-1722, F-EW-1729, F-EW-1730, F-EW-1736, F-EW-1740, F-EW-1742, F-EW-1744, F-LF-238
- Best details:
  - « on a peine à prier pour elle, on se sent comme forcé de l'invoquer elle-même » (F-EW-1720).
  - She looked like a virgin martyr on her reliquary rather than a poor Carmelite on her coffin (F-EW-1715, F-EW-1716).
  - She was "quoique peu ressemblante" at the grille (F-EW-1744).
  - She held her crucifix so tightly it had to be pulled from her hands; her limbs stayed supple until burial (F-EW-1722).
  - The body looked 12 or 13, then graver at the grille, then child-like again when the coffin was closed (F-EW-1729).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (present; 1910, 1915); Céline; Marie; Léonie (saw and heard); Jeanne; TSA; MdA; Marie des Anges (deposition and circular); Maupas; Godefroy (second-hand); Laveille. High for the sober accounts; medium for impressions.
- Conflicts: Léonie says many more came than for other Carmelites (F-EW-1720); Pauline, Céline, Marie, Jeanne and TSA say the crowd was ordinary and explained by family fame (F-EW-1717, F-EW-1718, F-EW-1721, F-EW-1730, F-EW-1736). Pauline's 1915 version is a deliberate deflation of any claim of early cult (F-EW-1717 notes).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the crowd at the grille, "one feels forced to invoke her".

### E-P6-399 — 30 Sept-3 Oct 1897, Carmel — Signs reported by the Sisters: perfumes, a palm, a light
- What happened: Laveille and the Carmel's account (Taylor) report that she was clothed in her habit with a crown of white roses and a palm in her hand, and laid on the straw mattress brought down to the infirmary. Nuns noticed a perfume of lilies and E-P6-363 where no flowers grew. Taylor adds: one nun smelled E-P6-363, one felt a kiss, one saw a light in the sky, one saw a luminous crown rise and vanish. A child of ten smelled lilies though only artificial lilies were there. The palm was found intact at the exhumation of 1910. Laveille says the Sister who had rebuked her over the wreaths at Mère Geneviève's coffin suffered from "cerebral anaemia" and, repentant, leant her forehead on Thérèse's feet and "felt herself suddenly cured".
- Cards: F-LF-238, F-OM-015
- Best details:
  - A child of ten smelled lilies, though only artificial lilies were in the choir (F-OM-015).
  - Palm found intact at the exhumation of 1910 (F-LF-238).
- Evidence: Laveille and Taylor, reporting the Carmel's account. Medium (community report; see M-OM-005, M-LF-012, M-LF-013).
- Conflicts: Laveille identifies the lay Sister as the one who scolded Thérèse over the wreaths in 1891 (F-OM-015); other cards (F-EW-1708) name Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul. Pauline (1915) calls the perfumes "secondary and hard to prove".
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-400 — Sun 3 / Mon 4 Oct 1897, Carmel — Signs of decay and the coffin closed
- What happened: On Sunday evening 3 Oct the coffin was closed after "some signs of decomposition" appeared (Pauline, 1910). Pauline (1915) puts it on Monday morning, 4 Oct: the forehead veins were swollen and the fingertips blackish, though she was still beautiful. They were not surprised: when novices told her she would be preserved from corruption, she said the opposite, and wanted her body to dissolve "so that little souls would have nothing to envy her". Marie says that on the morning of 4 October, when the coffin was closed, despite "certain signs of decomposition beginning to appear", she took on again the child-like look of the infirmary. Before the coffin was closed the prioress replaced the crucifix in her hands with a small wooden cross. They left the palm, the paper with her vow formula, and a copy of her Act of Oblation.
- Cards: F-EW-1723, F-EW-1724, F-EW-1725, F-EW-1729
- Best details:
  - « malgré certains indices de décomposition qui commençaient déjà à paraître » (F-EW-1729).
  - Small wooden cross in her hands; palm, vow paper and a copy of the Act of Oblation left in (F-EW-1725).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (saw, in the enclosure; 1910, 1915); Marie (saw). High. Candid detail.
- Conflicts: Sunday evening (F-EW-1723) versus Monday morning (F-EW-1724, F-EW-1729). Pauline reports the crucifix grip and the decay without comment.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-401 — Monday 4 Oct 1897 (feast of St Francis), Carmel chapel and Lisieux cemetery — The funeral and burial
- What happened: The funeral was in the Carmel chapel on Monday 4 October 1897, with a goodly number of priests. The procession to the town cemetery was small and modest, and only a small number of the faithful went. She was buried in the plot her uncle Isidore Guérin had just bought for the Carmelites (the old concession had no vacant place), the first grave, at the back, in the right corner as one enters. Pauline gives a depth of 3.5 m, so that two more coffins could go above later. Piat gives 3.20 m. Only liturgical prayers were said, the body was quickly covered with clay, and the few mourners withdrew. A few days later a wooden cross bore "Soeur Thérèse de l'Enfant-Jésus, 1873–1897" and "Je veux passer mon ciel à faire du bien sur la terre". Pauline had painted the cross and first wrote a line of her poem, but a workman carrying it while the paint was fresh smudged it. Agnès saw a sign from heaven and replaced it with the "heaven" line, which she had not dared put first out of discretion. Abbé Rohée, parish priest and dean of Saint-Pierre, said in Domin's hearing: "Here is a burial at which nobody weeps." TSA sang the Laudes antiphon that morning and reports an inner experience during it (see I-M15). Marie-Élisabeth followed the cortege.
- Cards: F-EW-1726, F-EW-1727, F-EW-1731, F-EW-1732, F-EW-1734, F-EW-1735, F-EW-1737, F-EW-1738, F-EW-1739, F-EW-1740, F-EW-1741, F-EW-1743, F-EW-2054, F-EW-2562, F-EW-2641, F-LF-239, F-OM-016
- Best details:
  - « Voilà une inhumation à laquelle on ne pleure pas » (F-EW-1739).
  - The cross smudged by a workman with fresh paint, and the replacement inscription (F-EW-1732).
  - The grave 3.5 m deep to hold two more coffins, never used (F-EW-1726).
  - Taylor's note: "Divine Providence made use of the bigotry of men" to let pilgrims reach the tomb; he does not explain (F-OM-016 notes).
  - Isidore Guérin, ill, could not attend, but had prepared her resting place (F-EW-2641).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (from reports, she was enclosed); Marie; Céline (hearsay for the cemetery); Léonie (saw); Domin (saw); Maupas (presided); MT (not present); Laveille; Piat. High.
- Conflicts: Crowd: "very large gathering" (Aimée, F-EW-1735, medium); "numerous" (Marie, F-EW-1730); "small and unassuming" (Céline, Laveille, De Teil art. 106). Enclosed nuns did not attend, so MT was absent and Aimée may mean the chapel (F-EW-1735). Depth 3.5 m (Pauline) versus 3.20 m (Piat). The plot: "recently bought by the Carmelites" (Laveille) versus bought by Isidore Guérin (Pauline, Piat). Godefroy's cross bears a wrong date (see E-P6-418).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-402 — October 1897, Lisieux — Gonzague mentions the notes when the obituary circular is drafted
- What happened: When the usual obituary circular was being drafted after her death, Gonzague told Maupas she had very precious notes on Thérèse's life, written by Thérèse on her order.
- Cards: F-EW-1713
- Best details:
  - "very precious notes" written by Thérèse on her order (F-EW-1713).
- Evidence: Maupas (heard; PO-25 f. 1230r). High. Fits Gonzague's letter "only the superior knows" (F-EW-1745).
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-403 — late 1897 — Three "premature" deaths in a year
- What happened: Aimée wrote to her family that three "premature" deaths had struck within a year. They were a young extern Sister aged 36 (Sr Marie-Antoinette), the youngest Sister (Thérèse, died 30 Sept at 24), and the chaplain Abbé Youf, about 50, who died 15 days after Thérèse.
- Cards: F-EW-1381
- Best details:
  - Youf died 15 days after Thérèse (F-EW-1381).
- Evidence: Aimée's letter. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-404 — notes from Oct 1897; asked 1911 and 1916 — MT wrote most of "Conseils et Souvenirs"
- What happened: The judges asked why MT's answer matched word for word the "Conseils et souvenirs" printed in Histoire d'une âme (1899 edition). MT said most of that section came from notes she herself wrote from memory, and she used the same notes for her deposition. In PA she says she wrote them right after Thérèse's death, for her own edification, well before any process. She knows them by heart and "cannot say them otherwise".
- Cards: F-EW-1710
- Best details:
  - "cannot say them otherwise" (F-EW-1710).
- Evidence: MT (about her own writing; PO 17 f. 1070v; PA 21 p. 1235). High. Important for source criticism: many MT "quotations" are recitations of her 1897 notes, already published; both tribunals noticed.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-405 — Oct 1897, cloister, Lisieux Carmel (fifteen days after the death, eve of the feast of the Maternity of the Virgin) — Céline's flame from heaven: "It is Thérèse"
- What happened: Céline said her greatest inner grace came in Oct 1897, only fifteen days after Thérèse's death, on the eve of the feast of the Maternity of the Virgin, while she made the Way of the Cross in the cloister. She saw "a flame that seemed to come from the depths of heaven", felt a supernatural impression and cried "It is Thérèse". In a flash she received the answer to difficulties that had often saddened her, and the "little way" of trust, abandonment, humility and childhood became clear to her.
- Cards: F-EW-2483
- Best details:
  - « C'est Thérèse » (F-EW-2483).
- Evidence: Céline (PO 4 f. 409v-410r, 27 Sep 1910). High that she testified it; private experience, no other witness. Part of the material for the rise of the cult inside the house.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: high.

### E-P6-406 — 29 October 1897, Lisieux — Gonzague's secret plan to publish
- What happened: On 29 October 1897 Gonzague wrote to Godefroy that Thérèse's death left her almost inert and with a void never to be filled. By obedience Thérèse had left "delightful pages" which she was copying out with Mère Agnès. She asked him to correct them. She added that no one knew, even in the community, except the superior.
- Cards: F-EW-1745
- Best details:
  - « Personne ne le sait, même dans la communauté; il n'y a que monsieur le supérieur qui m'a permis. » (F-EW-1745).
- Evidence: Letter read to the tribunal by Godefroy (PO-24 f. 1208r). High: contemporary letter.
- Conflicts: PA-26 (f. 23v) quotes a different wording from memory ("Our angel, before flying to heaven, has left us a treasure…"); the PO letter is the more reliable.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-407 — 1897–1904 (Gonzague d. 1904) — Gonzague weeps before Thérèse's portrait: "all she reproached me for"
- What happened: Mère Marie de Gonzague told Céline of a grace received before a portrait of Thérèse as a child. She could not look at it without weeping. Céline saw this often. She said only that she knew what she owed her: "Oh, what she told me!... All she reproached me for!... but so gently!" In her last years she improved noticeably "under Thérèse's gentle impulse". Céline adds that Sisters who had been hostile changed to veneration after the death. Laveille also says that after the death Marie de Gonzague, moved by the last agony, became more even and gentle. He cites Summarium 1919 p. 994 § 2854 (Céline): the signal grace before the child portrait, "I alone can know what I owe her. Oh, what she said to me! ... but so tenderly." He says the community had been affected at times by "the unequable temperament" of Mère Marie de Gonzague, which explains trials Thérèse suffered from "two or three Sisters". Shortly before her own death she said, "I have my little Thérèse to intercede for me." She died on 17 Dec 1904 "in sentiments of the most profound humility".
- Cards: F-EW-1264, F-EW-1294, F-LF-243, F-OM-017
- Best details:
  - « Tout ce qu'elle m'a reproché!... mais si doucement!... » (F-EW-1264).
  - Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul, the lay Sister who had made her suffer, became the keenest keeper of Thérèse's souvenirs and portraits, and told the bouquet story to every Sister in turn (F-EW-1294).
  - Another Sister composed a prayer to invoke her daily (F-EW-1294).
  - "the unequable temperament" of the prioress (F-OM-017).
  - "I have my little Thérèse to intercede for me" (F-LF-243).
- Evidence: Céline (saw and heard Gonzague, 1910 and 1915). High. Shows Thérèse did correct her prioress (scene S-EW-045). | Laveille, citing Céline's Process testimony. Medium-high; rests on Céline as summarised. Laveille names no Sister who opposed her.
- Conflicts: The "grace" is not described (see M-LF-016).
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: high: the prioress in tears before a child's portrait.

### E-P6-408 — 1897–1916, Lisieux Carmel — After her death: critics won over, the community "transformed"
- What happened: MM says belief in her holiness became unanimous in the monastery after death. Most of the Sisters who thought little of her are dead, and the two or three alive no longer object; they love and pray to her. MM thinks the change comes from the proven power of her intercession. MT says Thérèse "truly transformed" the community: no trace remained of the irregularities and party spirit before. Pauline calls this transformation more important than the perfumes some nuns smelled. Marie says she heard no one, inside or outside, give an opinion against Thérèse's holiness. Almost all new postulants came because of her, the first being Mère Marie-Ange de l'Enfant-Jésus. Jeanne Guérin says that in the first years after Thérèse's death she did not expect the spread of "the supernatural". This spread showed her a holiness she had not known was so great.
- Cards: F-EW-1306, F-EW-1302, F-EW-1243, F-EW-1277, F-EW-1233, F-EW-1324
- Best details:
  - "a real transformation": the whole community became fervent and regular (F-EW-1243).
  - Mère Marie-Ange de l'Enfant-Jésus, a Breton, first postulant drawn by her, later prioress, offered her life for the Cause and died at 28 (F-EW-1233).
  - Postulants came from Provence, the Pyrenees, Brittany and the Vendée; requests from Constantinople, Ireland, Portugal and Italy (F-EW-1233).
  - « une sainteté que je ne savais pas si grande » (F-EW-1324).
- Evidence: MM, MT, Pauline, Marie (interested witnesses, Process 1910-1916). Medium to high. | Jeanne Guérin (Mme La Néele, sworn statement, Process 1911). High.
- Conflicts: Marie says she heard no opposition (F-EW-1277), which contrasts with her own alpargates story and dissent reported by others (F-EW-1709, later). Pauline's 1915 opinion (perfumes "secondary") differs from 1910, when she said nearly all nuns had perceived perfumes. The pre-1897 party spirit implied by MT and Pauline matches the Gonzague memoir (F-EW-1243 notes). | In PA-23 [1357] Jeanne says the opposite in part: already in childhood she thought Thérèse's perfection "quite extraordinary". The two statements do not fully agree.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low: summary evidence.

### E-P6-409 — to 1897 — Nobody, not even Pauline, expected a beatification
- What happened: Pauline says the nuns esteemed Thérèse more than any other but during her life did not imagine a beatification. Pauline, who saw her as a saint especially in the last illness, did not think of canonization, believing one needed miracles and striking deeds in life.
- Cards: F-EW-1241
- Best details:
  - No one, Pauline included, thought of beatification (F-EW-1241).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (1915). High. Contrast F-EW-217 ("a little saint who would draw blessings on us").
- Conflicts: contrast with F-EW-217, per the card's notes.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-410 — 1897–1911, China, Siam, India — Missionaries adopt her early
- What happened: Soon after her death Roulland saw fellow missionaries invoke her. M. Deronin founded a community of Chinese virgins at Chongqing under her protection. M. Arlas of Chengdu placed verses of his own on her tomb. Others named are MM. Holhann and Guénan (Hong Kong), M. Ferlay (Siam), M. Vial (Yunnan) and M. Nassoy (India). Young departing missionaries visited her tomb before leaving.
- Cards: F-EW-1327
- Best details:
  - Departing young missionaries visited her tomb (F-EW-1327).
  - In 1917 Roulland adds that many Trappistine nuns in Japan said they owed her their vocation (PA-25 [1390]).
- Evidence: Roulland (letters and talks). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-411 — 1897–1905 — Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul turns into a devotee
- What happened: After Thérèse's death Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul, the lay Sister who had made her suffer, picked up the rejected prints of Thérèse's photographs and kept them as souvenirs. After her cure she collected rejected photographs everywhere. In 1898 she told Céline she could be proud to have been a saint's sister. Céline adds that she collected discarded photographs of Thérèse everywhere "before her outside reputation had influenced opinions" and told all the Sisters her story herself.
- Cards: F-EW-1334, F-EW-1380, F-EW-1708
- Best details:
  - She picked up the rejected photograph prints (F-EW-1334, F-EW-1380).
  - "proud to be the sister of a saint" (F-EW-1380).
  - Collected discarded photographs (F-EW-1708).
- Evidence: Carmel archive editors (source not given); Céline. Medium (summary) to high. See I-M02 / K-M08 for her cure. | Céline. High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-412 — after 1897; Léonie's profession 1900 — Sr Marie de l'Incarnation's collage
- What happened: No one knows what Sr Marie de l'Incarnation thought of Thérèse. Soon after the death she cut out a photo of Thérèse and pasted it on a photo of the garden she loved. The picture was used for Léonie's profession card and later painted by Sr Marie du St-Esprit.
- Cards: F-EW-1352
- Best details:
  - An early photomontage of Thérèse (F-EW-1352).
- Evidence: Editors (Carmel archive). High.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-413 — after 1897 — Gonzague's note in the profession register
- What happened: Gonzague wrote in the margin of Thérèse's act of profession that she filled the difficult office of novice mistress with wisdom and perfection equalled only by her love of God. After the death Gonzague filled the entire page of Thérèse's profession in the professions register instead of the usual few lines, probably in two sittings. First she wrote that "this angel of the earth" flew to her Beloved in an act of love and begged her to watch over her Carmel. Later, in the left margin (after Histoire d'une âme began spreading, c. 1899+), she praised her as mistress of novices and referred to "the dear manuscript that edifies the whole world". She gives her age at death as "24 years 9 months".
- Cards: F-EW-1375, F-EW-1695
- Best details:
  - « Elle remplit la difficile obédience de Maîtresse des novices avec une sagacité et perfection » (F-EW-1375).
  - "this angel of the earth" flew to her Beloved in an act of love (F-EW-1695).
- Evidence: Gonzague (biography §11). High. | Register text, via editors. High.
- Conflicts: She says Thérèse filled the "difficult obedience of novice mistress", though officially Thérèse never held that title.
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-414 — 1897-1900, Lisieux Carmel / rue Paul Banaston — After Thérèse's death: Marie feels changed; "a donkey loaded with relics"
- What happened: Marie wrote after Thérèse's death: "It is certain that since the death of our little Saint I no longer feel the same, and others notice it. It is incredible how she loved and still loves my soul." By special permission her letters became weekly. Isidore wrote humorously: "Fortunately all our holy relatives and my little Queen will give me a leg-up, while my other daughters push me from behind. Saint Peter may let me in like a donkey loaded with relics."
- Cards: F-EW-2645
- Best details:
  - "a donkey loaded with relics" (F-EW-2645).
- Evidence: Piat (1967), quoting letters. Medium-high. "My little Queen" here is Marie, per the earlier Piat card.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-415 — after Oct 1897 (undated), Lisieux Carmel — The voice: "Very humbly!"
- What happened: Céline said that once, when a Sister had taken an object she needed, she was about to reclaim it sharply. She heard clearly the words "Bien humblement!" (Very humbly!). She knew the voice of Thérèse, and her heart was at once changed toward humility.
- Cards: F-EW-2484
- Best details:
  - « Bien humblement! » (F-EW-2484). A quarrel over an object between two nuns.
- Evidence: Céline (PO 4 f. 410r). High that she testified it; private experience.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-416 — 1897–1898, Lisieux / Mondaye / Bayeux — How the manuscript was approved and printed
- What happened: After her death the manuscript was reviewed by Premonstratensian fathers of Mondaye (Fr Godefroy Madelaine, prior, and Fr Norbert), who urged publication. On the prior's report Bishop Hugonin gave the imprimatur on 7 March 1898, after retouches Fr Godefroy indicated. Agnès aimed to send it to Order monasteries instead of the usual obituary circular. To get Gonzague's permission she made small changes so all three parts seemed addressed to Gonzague, who signed it. Marie later restored the original wording. More detail from the Carmel archive and Laveille. A dead Carmelite normally produced a short circular (about three pages) for other Carmels. Agnès saw the illness was far advanced when Thérèse told her on 30 May 1897 of the first haemoptysis of April 1896. Marie de Gonzague wrote a preface near the end of 1897. On 29 October 1897 Gonzague wrote to Godefroy that Thérèse left "delightful pages" and asked him to correct them (see E-P6-406). Fr Godefroid Madelaine studied the manuscript about three months, divided it into chapters, and says he made small corrections of form only; but his letter of 1 March 1898 says he would mark in blue pencil what should be left out for print: details "so intimate, so far above the common level", small French and style faults, and repetitions. Fr Norbert Paysan corrected the poems. Madelaine saw the dying Bishop Hugonin on 7 Mar 1898 and got an oral imprimatur (Laveille: imprimatur dated 8 Mar 1898); Hugonin died in early May 1898. The editors made the three manuscripts appear addressed to one person, the prioress; divided the whole into 12 chapters; added letters, about fifty poems, four prayers, passages of the plays, novices' memories and a selection of last words. The working title was "Un cantique d'amour ou le passage d'un ange"; title fixed by April 1898 (Madelaine suggested "Histoire d'une âme"). Isidore Guérin paid for printing; on the advice of Fr Marie des Augustins (Assumptionist) he chose the Œuvre de Saint-Paul, Bar-le-Duc, for 2,000 copies in two runs of 1,000. Quotes ranged from about 1,400 to 2,100 francs per 1,000 copies. It appeared on 21 Oct 1898, later than hoped (30 Sept), and sold at 4 francs. 121 copies went to French Carmels, 18 to French-speaking Carmels abroad and three, via Br Siméon, to Leo XIII. French Wikipedia (citing the 1956 introduction) says Marie de Gonzague imposed the single addressee, and when nuns asked to see the autographs she is said to have wanted to destroy them; Mère Agnès saved them by retouching the passages that showed the real addressees; Marie restored them in 1910.
- Cards: F-EW-1224, F-EW-1247, F-LF-241, F-EW-1746, F-EW-1745
- Best details:
  - « en remplacement de la lettre circulaire » (F-EW-1247).
  - Pauline gives October 1898 as the publication date (F-EW-1224).
  - Marie later restored the original wording (F-EW-1247).
  - Madelaine's "blue pencil" (he later apologised) (F-LF-241, F-EW-1746).
  - « il y a des détails si intimes, si élevés au-dessus du niveau commun, qu'il vaut mieux, je crois, ne pas les faire imprimer » (F-EW-1746).
  - Print run: 2,000 copies; 4 francs; 121 copies to French Carmels; 3 copies to Leo XIII via Br Siméon (F-LF-241).
  - Working title "Un cantique d'amour ou le passage d'un ange" (F-LF-241).
- Evidence: Mère Agnès (first-hand, 1910); Céline (first-hand, 1910). High. Matches Marie (F-EW-1754, later chunk). | Carmel archive pages; Godefroy's letters read into the record (PO-24); Laveille; French Wikipedia (facts only). High for the Carmel's data; medium-high for the Wikipedia claim about destroying autographs (single source).
- Conflicts: The editors' introduction gives 30 Sept 1898; Pauline says "October 1898" (F-EW-1224). Pauline says she proposed and Gonzague sent the copy to Mondaye; elsewhere (f. 294r) she says Gonzague "had the manuscript printed". | Publication date 21 Oct 1898 (Céline's note; FR Wikipedia 20 or 21 Oct) versus 30 Sept 1898 (editors) and "October 1898" (Pauline). Imprimatur 7 Mar (Madelaine, Pauline) versus 8 Mar (Laveille). Godefroy: "corrections of form, not of substance" versus his letter proposing cuts of intimate passages. Laveille says Gonzague had the manuscript revised and adopted printing on Agnès's urging (LAV p. 402 fn. 2). The Carmel says editors' changes: one addressee, chapters, spelling, "soudures", skipped passages, style corrected; many erasures in the autographs are by several hands.
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: medium.

### E-P6-417 — Oct 1897 to Jan 1899, Lisieux cemetery — Few pilgrims in the first 18 months
- What happened: In the 18 months after the death, before she entered the Visitation, Léonie often went to pray at the tomb. In 1910 she said she saw no crowd of pilgrims then. In 1915: "a few faithful came, but in small numbers."
- Cards: F-EW-1712
- Best details:
  - "a few faithful came, but in small numbers" (F-EW-1712, 1915).
- Evidence: Léonie (saw; 1910, 1915). High. Useful baseline for how slowly the cult began.
- Conflicts: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Scene potential: low.

### E-P6-418 — exhumation 6 September 1910; grave of 1897, Lisieux cemetery — The 1910 exhumation, the grave and the wooden cross
- What happened: She was buried in the Lisieux (Saint-Jacques) cemetery. At the time of the Ordinary Process the grave was opened and the remains put in a zinc coffin. Only bones remained; the body had undergone "ordinary corruption". The coffin went back to the same place in the Carmel's plot. Before and after, a plain wooden cross like the other nuns' bore her name and "died 17 [sic] September 1897". The Bishop took every care that nothing resembled a public cult at the exhumation. Later cards on the exhumation of 6 Sept 1910. Her remains were exhumed by order and in the presence of the bishop, put in a lead coffin (Marie, Céline) or a brick-lined vault (Marie) and reburied a short distance away. Céline noticed nothing extraordinary in the funeral honours. After the exhumation the wooden cross from the first grave was brought to the Carmel, "literally covered" with pilgrims' written invocations and thanks. Maupas attended the transfer, presided by the bishop of Bayeux; the official report was published. Sr Marie-Élisabeth was present at the exhumation of 6 Sep 1910 and again at that of 10 Aug 1917 (end of the Apostolic Process), and testified in the "Non-Cult" process: she and her companions often climbed to the cemetery to remove the ex-votos, petitions, candles and flowers that pilgrims left. Piat says crowds later came to pray there, until the translation to the Carmel on 26 Mar 1923.
- Cards: F-EW-1332, F-EW-1727, F-EW-1728, F-EW-1731, F-EW-1733, F-EW-1741, F-EW-2562, F-EW-2641
- Best details:
  - Plain wooden cross like the other nuns', with a wrong date (F-EW-1332).
  - The cross "literally covered" with pilgrims' invocations and thanks (F-EW-1728).
  - The exhumation of 10 Aug 1917 is a new date (F-EW-2562 notes).
- Evidence: Godefroy ("personal knowledge" and public knowledge). Medium. | Marie (hearsay); Céline (hearsay and saw the cross); Maupas (present); Carmel biography of Sr Marie-Élisabeth; Piat. High.
- Conflicts: The date on the cross ("17 September") is surely wrong; she died 30 September. | Lead coffin (Céline, Marie F-EW-1727) versus brick-lined vault (Marie F-EW-1731); both may be true (lead coffin in a brick vault). The exhumation took place during Marie's own testimony week (F-EW-1727 notes).
- New to English readers: partial
- Scene potential: low.
